# Changelog

All notable changes are recorded here. The format follows [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/), and the project uses [semantic versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html) with the caveat in `spec/18-package-layout.md` section 18.6: pre-1.0 versions carry no compatibility promise at all.

## Unreleased

### Added

- `__GLIBC_MINOR__` comes from the target, and only when the headers are the tree we bundle. One header tree serves every glibc release with the differences written inside the files as `#if __GLIBC_MINOR__ >= n`, so the release is the part of it the target supplies, which is what `env_version` is in the tuple for. Zig patches the same macro into the same tree the same way, keeping `__GLIBC__` at 2 in `features.h` and leaving the minor to the compiler, so this is glibc's own spelling rather than a name of ours: `__GLIBC_PREREQ` reads it and so does every autoconf probe. `--target=x86_64-linux-gnu.2.28` now reaches the preprocessor instead of being parsed and dropped. tamnd/rucc#926.

- The condition on that macro is the interesting half. A host glibc defines it in its own `features.h` and so does a tree the user named with `--sysroot`, and a second definition with a different value is a warning on every compilation of every file, so the same condition that chose the header directories chooses this. A musl or mingw target gets nothing, because there is no such macro there and a program probing for one has to hear no, and `-nostdinc` gets nothing because there are no headers of ours on the path to have a version.

- A glibc newer than the bundled tree is refused by name, with both versions in the message and the two ways out. Asking for an older release turns declarations off, which is what the macro is for, but asking for a newer one would turn on declarations the text does not have, so every probe would pass and the program would fail later and further away, at a missing declaration at best and a missing symbol at link time at worst. `rucc_sysroot::BUNDLED_GLIBC` is the release the tree is produced from, which is the glibc pinned in `sysroots/manifest` in tamnd/rucc-cross, so the two cannot drift apart silently.

- `rucc_safe_rt::init` is the init plane of `spec/safe-memory/09-type-init-and-races.md` section 9.2, a bit for every byte of storage, which is the class MSan exists for and the kernel infoleak with it. The bit means uninitialized rather than initialized, and that inversion is the whole design: MSan believes a byte some uninstrumented library wrote is still uninitialized and reports the next read of it, which is why every library a program links has to be instrumented and why MSan is a fuzzing tool rather than something anybody ships. Here a byte nobody has said anything about counts as written, so untouched shadow already says the permissive thing, an adopted region full of live data says it without anything walking the region, and a gap in instrumentation loses a check rather than inventing a refusal. An instance beginning is the only thing that makes a byte uninitialized and everything else clears bits. tamnd/rucc#431.

- The padding rule of section 9.3 is a question of which range the compiler names rather than a mode the plane has. A store that writes an object as a whole names `sizeof`, padding included, and a store through a member names the member's width, so a structure filled member by member keeps padding that nothing wrote. A copy carries the answer for the bytes it read to the bytes it wrote, which is what makes the infoleak visible: a structure handed whole to `write` carries its padding along, and the read that is refused is the one at the boundary where the bytes would leave the program.

- Every read that names a type asks the plane whether the bytes it is about to read agree with it, which is judgement J3 and the last piece of the compiler's half of the type plane. The two writes were already in, a store recording what it stored through and a copy carrying what its source said, and this is the question they were recorded for: the effective type rule of C 6.5 says an object's stored value may be read only through a type compatible with the one it was stored through. `check_type` becomes `__rucc_check_type`, which the runtime has had since the plane went in, and the type travels as the same number the store recorded, which is the only way the two can be compared. `--emit=safety-summary` reports it as a class of its own beside bounds, lifetime and derivation. A store asks nothing, because the plane covers the storage C gives no declared type and the effective type of one of those is whatever the last store set, so a check in front of a store would refuse the reuse of a buffer the standard permits. A read the front end named no type for asks nothing either, since that is a copy of an aggregate or anything else reached by address rather than a read of bytes nothing has stored through. tamnd/rucc#431.

- An access through a member of a union names the character type rather than the member's own, which is what makes the punning idiom of C 6.5.2.3 survive a check against the type plane. Every member of a union starts at the same byte, so an access to one names bytes another may have been written through, and the character type is the one every access agrees with. A store through `u.as_int` therefore leaves bytes that a later read of `u.as_float` is allowed to read, however the read reaches them, and a member of a member of a union is the same case. The optimizer did not need this, because layer 4 of the alias analysis settles two accesses to one object on their offsets before the types are looked at, but the plane has no layer 4: it holds a number per byte of the program's own memory and a check against it would have refused the idiom outright. What is left uncovered is a union whose bytes were only ever named through a pointer taken earlier, which is not distinguishable from an ordinary aliasing violation. tamnd/rucc#431.

- Every copy carries whatever the bytes it read said over to the bytes it wrote, which is the other half of the judgement C 6.5 describes and the last write the type plane was missing. A copy does not store through a type, so there is nothing for the compiler to name: what the copied bytes are is whatever the bytes they came from were, and the plane over the source is the only place that is written down. So the new `meta_type_copy` names two ranges and a length and no type at all, and `__rucc_meta_type_copy` moves the entries across. Without it a structure copied into a fresh allocation would come out saying whatever the bytes there said before, and the very next read of a field would be refused on a program that is correct. tamnd/rucc#431.

- Every store records what the bytes it wrote were stored through, which is the judgement C 6.5 says a store makes and the first thing to read the type names the front end puts on an access. `rucc_safety::plane` turns the aliasing node the access carries into the entry the plane holds, `rucc_safety::insert` puts a `meta_type` after each store, and the lowering turns it into the `__rucc_meta_type` the runtime has been waiting for. The question is not here yet and the order is deliberate: a check against a plane that only some of the writes maintain reports on programs that are correct, and the write a copy makes is the bullet above this one. tamnd/rucc#431.

- Three of the things a byte can say are not types, and the entry a store records is which of them it is. A store through a character lvalue records the value that is compatible with everything, which is C 6.5's rule that a character store sets no effective type rather than a concession to it, and a store that names no type at all records that the bytes are untyped rather than recording nothing, so that a plane never goes on describing memory that is no longer there. Untyped is compatible with every access, so what that costs is a check that is not made, which is the direction a plane is allowed to be wrong in.

- A Linux target searches four header directories rather than two, because two of them are the kernel's. `linux/`, `asm/` and `asm-generic/` are the system call interface and not the C library, and they are not optional for anybody: 31 of glibc's installed headers and 3 of musl's include one of them, `sys/ioctl.h` and the socket headers among them, so a sysroot without them fails on ordinary programs. They are also the same files for every target that shares an architecture, since the system call interface does not know which libc is calling it, so `rucc_sysroot::Kernel` puts them in the cache beside the sysroots rather than inside each one. That turns nine megabytes per Linux row into nine megabytes plus a small `asm/` per architecture. tamnd/rucc#926.

- The kernel's two directories come after the libc's and they are the one part of step 3 that a named `--sysroot` does not get. Both trees have a `sys/`, and the libc's is the one a program asking for `<sys/types.h>` means, while the kernel's own names are in no libc at all, so putting the kernel last shadows nothing. A tree somebody else assembled has its own `linux/` from its own kernel version, and one field from each of two kernel versions is exactly the failure a pinned header tree exists to prevent, so ours is not layered underneath theirs. `Origin::Kernel` says which of the two a directory came from, because a user asking where `linux/stat.h` was found is asking about the kernel and not about glibc.

- The kernel has a third name for the machine, after ours and the libc's. Its directories under `arch/` are `arm64` and not `aarch64`, `s390` and not `s390x`, one `x86` for both widths and one `powerpc` for every width and endianness, so `Kernel::arch` is a separate function from the one that names a libc's header directory and neither is the tuple's spelling. Three namings of one piece of hardware is not a thing to fix, it is a thing to write down once where every caller can read it.

- `Licence::LinuxUapi` spells `gpl-2.0-with-linux-syscall-note`, which is the exception and not just the licence. GPL-2.0 on files a compiler hands to every program it builds reads alarming until `LICENSES/exception/Linux-syscall-note` is read, and that note says using the system call interface does not put the calling program under the GPL, which is what every libc and every cross toolchain relies on. Redistributing the headers themselves carries the GPL's own obligation and it is met the way glibc's is, by the pinned source and hash every manifest input carries.

- The number an entry travels as is a hash of the type's canonical spelling, because the plane outlives the object that wrote it. A counter numbers each object's types from zero, so `int` is 3 in one file and 7 in the next, and a store in one object and a read in another would disagree about a type they spell the same way, which is a refusal of a program that is correct. A function of the spelling is the same number in every object anybody builds, with no table to keep and nothing to merge at link time, which is the same argument section 8.2 makes for keying the aliasing nodes on their names. What it costs is that two spellings could collide and be read as one type, which is a check that is not made rather than a wrong answer, and a test holds the whole of today's vocabulary against that. The hash is reduced into thirty one bits rather than thirty two, because the plane keeps the top bit of a slot to say that a granule's bytes do not all agree and the rest of such a slot is an index into the table those bytes are kept in. Half of all spellings hash above that bit, `int` and `long` and `double` among them, so a whole granule stored through one of those left a slot the plane later read as an index and followed to an address nothing had mapped.

- The front end names the type every access goes through, which is what layer 3 of the alias analysis has been waiting for. `rucc_lower::aliasing` builds the tree and every load and every store that goes through a C type carries the node for that type. The layer was already written and tested and answered correctly for the accesses that carried a node, which until now was none of them. Nothing compiles differently yet, because no pass at any level asks the alias analysis anything today, and the pass that will is the memory SSA load forwarder of `spec/optimizer/16-gvn-and-pre.md` section 16.2. This is the half of it that has to exist first, and it is also what the type plane of `spec/safe-memory/09-type-init-and-races.md` section 9.1 needs, since a `meta_type` names a type with one of these nodes. tamnd/rucc#431.

- `char` is the root of that tree and every other scalar hangs directly under it, which is C 6.5 paragraph 7: a program may read any object as bytes, so the byte reader has to be treated as reaching every object, and two different scalar types reach nothing in common. A signed type and its unsigned counterpart share one node because the paragraph allows either, an enumeration shares with the integer type it is represented in, and every object pointer is one node, which is what gcc's C front end does. A struct, an array, a vector, a function and `void` get no node, and no node means the access conflicts with everything, so leaving a type out costs speed and never correctness. Union punning still works, because layer 4 settles two accesses to one named object on their offsets before layer 3 is reached.

- A node is found by a canonical spelling of the type and never by the order the walk met it, so two accesses through `int` in two different functions are one node because they spell the same string. Section 8.2 asks for that and the reason is document 35's link time optimization: merging two modules is merging two metadata tables, and a table keyed on visit order gives the merge no way to tell that this module's `int` and that module's `int` are the same thing.

- `-fno-strict-aliasing` clears those names rather than being taken and dropped. Dropping it was honest while nothing named a type at all, and the change that makes a pass read the names is not the change anybody will remember to wire the flag into, so it is wired now. The flag being the absence of the names keeps it one condition in one place, and it keeps the answer with the unit it was built for rather than with the pipeline that later reads it.
- A cross link finds our own runtime wherever it is, and is written without it when it is nowhere. `librucc_builtins.a` is the one file on a cross link line that is ours rather than the target's, so unlike every path beside it, it is a question about the machine: `-B` first, then the sysroot's `lib` beside the libc, then beside the compiler, which is the build tree. The first real cross link this compiler ever ran is what found it. A musl sysroot built from source has a libc and no runtime archive, because nothing builds that archive for a target yet, and the line named it anyway, so `ld.lld` stopped at a file that was missing rather than compiling a program that was there. Leaving it off links the program, and the program that reaches one of those routines now hears about the routine by name instead of about a file whose connection to it is not obvious. `rucc_sysroot::argv::Builtins` is the three answers, and the line stays a function of its arguments because the looking is the driver's half. tamnd/rucc#732.

- The type plane is mapped over every region the monitor watches, and `rucc_safe_rt::check` gained judgement J3 with the two judgements that record what it asks about. `__rucc_check_type` is C 6.5's effective type rule, `__rucc_meta_type` is the judgement a store makes, and `__rucc_meta_type_copy` is the rule that a copy carries the source's types to the destination. The compiler emits the second and the third of the three, which are the two bullets at the top of this list. tamnd/rucc#431.

- A region's reservation is now the lifetime plane, the type plane, the type plane's side table and then the region, which is two and a half times the region rather than one and a half. It is address space rather than memory: the mapping is anonymous, and a plane a program never touches never costs it a page. The side table gets one entry for every eight granules, which is the 12.6 percent heterogeneity that document 05 section 5.2.5 measured on SQLite rounded the safe way, and it puts the plane at one byte per byte against a budget of 1.25. An adopted region gets all three the same way, so a region this crate did not reserve is watched exactly as closely as one it did, and a region that cannot have all of them is not watched at all rather than half watched.

- A fresh instance has no effective type, which is the other half of judgement J4. C says allocated storage takes its type from the first store, so a block handed out again forgets what its previous occupant stored through. Without that the first honest read of a reused block would be reported as type confusion, which is the false positive that would make the plane unusable. It is cleared over the whole block rather than the request, because the rounding shares granules with the request and an overflow inside a block should be reported as the bounds bug it is rather than as a type bug.

- `rucc_safe_rt::types` is the type plane of `spec/safe-memory/09-type-init-and-races.md` section 9.1: what the bytes at an address were last stored through, which is the fact four of document 03's type classes and one of its spatial classes are decided by. It is C 6.5 written down rather than an approximation of it, so a store records the type it stored through, an access asks whether what it is about to read agrees, a byte nothing has stored through agrees with everything, and both halves of the character rule are in one function. A `memcpy` carries the source's types to the destination, which is what makes the punning idiom work and is also what catches copying a `struct A` over a `struct B` and reading it back as a `B`. tamnd/rucc#431.

- The granule is eight bytes and the slot is four, which is one slot per eight bytes of program and a side entry of eight identifiers for a granule whose bytes disagree. That is the layout document 05 section 5.2.3 costs as `4/g + 4h`, at the `g` section 5.2.5 measured rather than the one that looks natural: on a sixty four bit target a pointer, a `long` and a `double` are all eight bytes, so a sixteen byte granule holds two distinct types and a structure alternating two of them disagrees in every granule it has. SQLite's declarations are 13 percent heterogeneous at eight bytes and 65 percent at sixteen, and the plane costs 1.00 bytes per byte at eight against 2.84 at sixteen, where the budget is 1.25.

- A granule that has once disagreed keeps its side entry for the life of the program, and a later store that covers the whole of it writes one type over all eight bytes rather than folding the granule back up. What that buys is an allocator that is a single `fetch_add` with no free list, no lock and no ordering to get right, on a path generated code reaches from any thread, and it is the first structure on the plane side of this crate that anything allocates at all. What it costs is one indirection on every later read of that granule and thirty two bytes that stay out. Reclaiming them wants the free list and the spin lock `rucc_safe_rt::alloc` already has, and that is not worth writing until a measurement says which programs fold granules back up often enough to care.

- A granule that has to disagree and cannot have an entry is recorded as untyped, which every access is permitted against. Running out of side table therefore thins the plane's coverage out and never makes it wrong, which is the same direction section 9.1 picks for bytes that uninstrumented code wrote and the only direction available to a tool whose false positive rate is a release blocking property.

- The store that makes the judgement calls into it and the access that asks the question does not yet, which is the compiler's half of the same milestone arriving in two pieces. The shadow and the side table are handed in rather than mapped here, for the reason `rucc_safe_rt::plane` gives about the lifetime plane.

- `-print-sysroot`, which is the question somebody asks when a cross build read a file nobody expected. It answers with the tree the user named if there is one, then the directory under the cache for a target that is not this machine, and with an empty line for a compile against this machine, because that is what GCC prints when it was configured without a sysroot and a `/` there would be a claim about the filesystem rather than an answer. It is the root rather than one of the directories under it, since what asks is a build system looking for a file of its own. tamnd/rucc#732.

- A cross compile reads the target's own headers. Step 3 of `spec/cross-compile/08-sysroots.md` section 8.5 used to be this machine's directories whenever the target's operating system happened to match this machine's, so a build for `x86_64-linux-musl` on a glibc box compiled against whatever `/usr/include` held and then linked against musl's stubs, and what that produces is not a header that could not be found but a declaration that is quietly wrong. The headers and the libraries now ask the same question, which is `link::cross_sysroot`, so the two halves of a compile cannot disagree about which kind of compile it is: a target that is this machine reads this machine's directories, and a target that is not reads the two under `<cache>/sysroots/<tuple>/include`, the architecture specific one first. Those two are named whether or not they are on the disk yet, unlike the machine's own, because the useful answer to `-v` on a machine where the tree has not been built is the path it would be at rather than silence. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `rucc_sysroot::search::include_paths` is the one statement of that order and the driver asks it rather than keeping a copy, which is the point of `Origin::Host` being reachable from exactly one guarded place. Steps 1 and 2 stay the driver's own: `-I` and its relatives are on the search path already in the order the command line gave them, and the compiler's own headers are not a directory at all but the `<builtin>` entry they are read out of. `Options::sysroot` is a list of directories now rather than a `Sysroot`, because a tree somebody else assembled has whatever shape they gave it, which for a buildroot or a distribution tree is `usr/include`, so a root the user named replaces step 3 with what is actually under it rather than with what would be there if we had laid it out. tamnd/rucc#732.

- A mingw-w64 target links. PE in that environment is the second format `rucc_sysroot::argv` writes a line for, and it is the other half of `spec/cross-compile/09-libc-stubs.md` section 9.4, whose import libraries have had nothing to put them on until now. The line is written in the GNU style, which is what GNU ld's PE port and `ld.lld` in its MinGW mode both read, so one line serves both and the difference from the ELF line is the flags rather than the shape. Microsoft's ABI is still refused, and now on its own grounds rather than as a format: `lld-link` takes a different command line and the import libraries a program there links against come out of the Windows SDK, which section 8.6 says cannot be redistributed. tamnd/rucc#732.

- Four things are different on that line and each one is a fact about the format. There is no dynamic linker, because a PE image names no interpreter and the loader finds a DLL by name rather than by a path written into the program. There is no `-pie` and no `-no-pie`, because every PE image carries a relocation table and may be placed anywhere, so what is left of that question is whether the loader is told it may use that freedom, which is `--dynamicbase`. The subsystem is named rather than left to the linker, because both linkers otherwise take it from the entry point they happen to find, and a cross link that decides anything from what is in its inputs is the failure section 11.3 is about. And `--no-insert-timestamp` is on every line, which is this format's `--build-id=none`: a PE header carries the second it was linked, and section 11.4 names that as one of the four ways byte identical output is lost. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `Libc` has a fourth case, `Import`, because a set of import libraries is not a stub shared object in a different container. The C library on Windows is several DLLs rather than one: `libmsvcrt.a` is the CRT, `libmingwex.a` is everything C requires that the CRT does not have, `libmoldname.a` is the unprefixed spellings of the names Microsoft deprecated, and the four Win32 libraries mingw-w64's own code calls into are on the line for the same reason they are on gcc's, which is that a program using none of them directly still reaches `kernel32` through `malloc`. The order is the one a single pass linker needs, because GNU ld's PE port is one and lld's COFF linker resolves archives to a fixed point, so writing the line for the stricter of the two is what makes it serve both. A static link is not refused there, since the CRT is a DLL on every Windows machine and `-static` is a statement about our libraries rather than about it. tamnd/rucc#732.

- The start files are one rather than four. `crt2.o` runs before `main`, `dllcrt2.o` is its counterpart for a DLL, and there is no `crti.o` or `crtn.o` because PE has no `.init` and `.fini` sections for a pair of files to open and close: the same job is done by a table of pointers the linker sorts by section name. `crtbegin.o` and `crtend.o` are left off on purpose, because they are GCC's files bracketing GCC's own list of constructors and a toolchain that is not GCC writes that list the way the platform writes it. Ours is not written yet, so a mingw link runs `main` and does not run a file scope constructor, which is a known gap that belongs with the codegen for the format. tamnd/rucc#732.

- The linker looked for on a cross link to Windows is `ld.lld` and then the prefixed mingw binutils under `<arch>-w64-mingw32-ld`, which is what every distribution's mingw packages install and not the multiarch name a Linux target is filed under. It is the same `ld.lld` as the ELF one, because its MinGW mode is a mode of the one linker rather than a second binary. tamnd/rucc#732.
- `-ffp-contract=` is taken, with gcc's three values, and the value it was given rides into the IR as an attribute on every function with a body and on no declaration. Nothing fuses a multiply and an addition here, on any target, at any level, under any `-march=`, so the flag changes no instruction today. It is kept rather than dropped because the place that would act on it is the code generator, which runs long after the command line is gone, and because a reader can now see in `--emit=ir` that the flag was understood. The default is `off` rather than gcc's `fast`, which is the same program while nothing fuses anything and is the one that does not write a licence nobody reads onto every function in the file. tamnd/rucc#489.

- `-fexcess-precision=`, `-frounding-math` and `-ftrapping-math` are taken and are descriptions of what already happens rather than requests being granted. No floating point arithmetic in a function body is folded at compile time, nothing is reassociated, and every operation is computed in the type it was written in, so `__FLT_EVAL_METHOD__` is 0 and the restrictive spelling of each of these is what this compiler already does. The permissive spellings are licences that are taken and not used, which costs speed and not correctness and is the answer `-fstrict-aliasing` and `-fdelete-null-pointer-checks` already get. A value of `-fexcess-precision=` that gcc does not take is refused rather than read as one of the three. tamnd/rucc#489.

- `crates/rucc/tests/fpmath.rs` holds the flags to that claim. It compiles a fused multiply and addition in both widths, an addition and a division the rounding mode and the exception flags would change, a reassociation, and the two identities that hold for real numbers and not for floating point, at every optimization level, under every value of `-ffp-contract=` and under `-march=haswell`, and asserts that no fused instruction is ever selected and that every arithmetic is still done at run time. It also asserts that the module comes out byte for byte the same under every spelling of the three flags that are descriptions, so a flag that quietly started doing something fails here rather than in somebody's numbers.

- `cargo xtask pressure` takes `-f` flags and compiles both sides with them, so the question of which pass a program's spilling belongs to can be asked of the tool rather than of eight hand-written compile lines. An argument that is not a flag is refused before anything is compiled, since the level here is fixed at `-O2` on purpose and a run that took one would be a measurement of a compiler nobody ships. tamnd/rucc#893.

- The driver links against a target's own sysroot when the target is not the machine it is running on, so a cross link stops reading `/usr/lib` and starts reading `<cache>/sysroots/<tuple>`. `link::cross_sysroot` is the one place the two paths are told apart and it asks two questions: whether the target differs from the host, and whether `--sysroot` was given. The second is what keeps every working configuration working, because a user who assembled a tree and named it is asking for the native line with their own root in front of every path, which is what cross compiling against a real distribution tree has always meant here. It is also the escape hatch for a machine that has its distribution's cross files installed, where `/usr/lib/aarch64-linux-gnu` really does hold an AArch64 `crt1.o`: `--sysroot=/` takes the native line and every directory it decides is that machine's again. A freestanding target gets a line for the first time rather than a refusal, since nothing about `x86_64-none` needed the host path. tamnd/rucc#732.

- The linker a cross link looks for is a shorter list than the native one and a different one, which is section 11.2: `ld.lld` first, because one binary of it links for every architecture it was built with, then `lld`, then the prefixed cross binutils under the name a distribution gives it. mold is not on the list and neither is the platform's own `ld`, because both link for the machine they are running on, and a `-fuse-ld=` naming either is still honoured for somebody whose build is not. The candidates are decided by the same function that decides the line, so the linker that is looked for and the arguments it is handed cannot disagree about which kind of link this is. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `link::preflight` asks what has an answer before the first object exists, which is whether this target and mode have a line at all and whether the sysroot it would read is on the machine. It runs before anything is compiled, for the same reason the linker itself is looked for first: a minute of compiling followed by a message about a directory that was never there is a minute nobody needed to spend. A sysroot that has not been built names the target, names the directory, and says the two ways to supply one. The line is built rather than inspected to answer the first half, because the refusals belong to the one function that builds it and a second copy of them here would be a second thing to keep in step. `-pg` on a cross link is refused there too: the counting is started and stopped by `gcrt1.o`, which is compiled code out of a C library's own sources, and a generated sysroot holds the names a libc exports rather than the bodies behind them. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `-print-search-dirs` and `-print-file-name=` follow the cross line, so a target that is not this machine answers with its sysroot's library directory and not with `/usr/lib`. What those two print is what a build system pastes into a link line of its own, and an answer naming a directory the real line never goes near is worse than no answer, which is what GCC prints when it finds nothing. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `rucc_driver::cache` is the cache directory of `spec/cross-compile/13-distribution.md` section 13.2, which is `RUCC_CACHE_DIR` when it is set, then the platform's own place for a cache a user can delete without losing anything, which is `LOCALAPPDATA` on Windows and `XDG_CACHE_HOME` or `~/.cache` on a Unix, and a temporary directory when a machine has none of them, because a build container that cannot link because nothing set `HOME` is worse than one that fetches a sysroot again on its next run. An empty variable is read as one nobody set rather than as a request to put the cache at the root of the filesystem. It is in the driver and not in `rucc-sysroot` because answering it means reading the environment, and the link line being a function of its arguments is what makes it testable for every target from any host. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `Invocation` carries `-rdynamic` and `-fno-builtins-lib`, so the two flags mean on a cross link what they mean on a native one. A flag the cross path ignored would be a flag that works until the target is somebody else's, which is the kind of difference a build system finds for you. `-fno-builtins-lib` means something narrower here than it does natively: there it leaves our runtime off so that the machine's `libgcc` answers for the wide arithmetic, and a generated sysroot has no `libgcc`, so here it leaves those names undefined for somebody who is supplying them another way. The archive is named once, in `rucc_sysroot::link::BUILTINS`, because the line that puts it on and the flag that takes it off are two places and a second spelling of the name is a flag that quietly stops working. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `rucc_sysroot::argv` is the cross link line as a pure function of the target, the sysroot and what the user asked for, which is `spec/cross-compile/11-linking.md` section 11.3. It returns the whole argument list a linker is handed, and it reads nothing else: no environment variable, no `std::env::consts`, no filesystem. That is the mirror of the rule the header search paths already follow, and it is what claim 5 in section 2 rests on, because a line assembled from anything on this machine is a line that differs between two machines. Every path on it is under the sysroot or is a path the caller wrote, and the one absolute path that is neither is the loader, which describes the target machine's filesystem rather than ours. It is in `rucc-sysroot` at layer rank 1 rather than in the driver, because the driver's existing link code is the native host discovery path on a three field triple and this is the cross path on the ten field tuple. tamnd/rucc#732.

- The line carries the eight things section 11.3 says a cross link needs, in the order a linker wants them: `-m` for the output format, `--sysroot` with every `-L` inside it, `-dynamic-linker` when something will start the result, the start files by absolute path, our default libraries, `librucc_builtins.a` after the libc that calls it, no host directory anywhere, and the format's own extras. On ELF those extras are five answers to defaults nobody wants: `--eh-frame-hdr`, `--hash-style=gnu`, `-z relro`, `-z now`, `-z noexecstack` and `--build-id=none`. The build id is the reproducibility one. A linker computes it over the inputs, and the inputs carry absolute paths, so a build id is how a host path reaches a binary that names no host path anywhere else. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `LinkMode` has five cases rather than three, and `-static-pie` is not one of the flags it produces. That flag is a compiler driver spelling and none of ld, ld.lld or mold has it: the link is static, the result is position independent, and there is explicitly no interpreter, because a static binary that names one gets one mapped and then relocates itself twice. The other new case is `-no-pie`, which is the same link as the dynamic one with a different first start file, since the reference to `main` in `crt1.o` is absolute and the one in `Scrt1.o` goes through the global offset table. `LinkLine` lost its `flags` field in the same change, because what has to be linked is a fact about the target and how it is spelled is a fact about the linker, and keeping both in one struct is what let the second one leak into the first. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `rucc_sysroot::link::glibc_loader` is glibc's loader table, which is a different table from musl's and not a different spelling of it. musl names every loader after the architecture in one directory. glibc's names come from each port's history, so three of them are called `ld64.so` with a number that means something different per port, s390x's is in `/lib` and PowerPC's is in `/lib64`, and i386's carries no architecture in its name at all because it was the only one when it was named. The rows with more than one answer are the ones where the loader and the program have to agree about registers: 32-bit ARM has the hard float split, RISC-V and LoongArch spell the float ABI and the data model into the name. Every one of these is a string nothing at link time can check, and a wrong one produces a binary the kernel refuses to start with a message about a file that is on nobody's disk. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `rucc_sysroot::link::libc` says which of the three cases in section 8.2 a target is, and the line follows from that rather than from what the target is called. musl is an archive, which is the only case a static link can use, because it is the one libc we build from source. glibc is a stub, and so are bionic, the BSDs and illumos, for the same reason: their libc is a shared object on the target machine, so a list of the names it exports is enough to link against one. Freestanding is nothing at all, so a freestanding line has no `crt1.o`, no `crti.o` and no libc, and keeps `librucc_builtins.a`, because an architecture with no division instruction needs it whether a libc exists or not. `emulation` answers `None` for a format that is not ELF in the same change, since an emulation is an ELF idea and a Mach-O target answering `aarch64linux` would be answering in a word its linker has never heard. tamnd/rucc#732.

- A combination with no line gets refused by name rather than approximated. A static link against a stub sysroot is impossible rather than unimplemented, and the message says so and points at musl: a stub carries the names and none of the code, which is everything a dynamic link needs and nothing a static one does, and glibc's own `libc.a` is megabytes of objects that cannot be synthesized from a description of an interface. A target whose format is not ELF is refused too, because `ld64` and the COFF linkers want a different line rather than a different spelling of this one, and those two lines are their own work. The alternative to both refusals is a line that runs and produces several thousand undefined symbols for somebody to read the first forty of. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `tests/link-lines` holds the recorded line for every target, one file each, with all five modes in it and one argument per line. Section 11.3 asks for this and the reason it asks is that a loader path, an emulation name and the position of a start file are three strings nothing at link time verifies, and none of them is covered by compiling anything. A file per target means a loader table change is one file of diff per architecture it touched and a change to the flags every line carries is forty two files, and one argument per line means a reviewer reads a diff of the argument that changed rather than of a line that wrapped. The modes a target cannot use are in the file as the refusal and its reason, which is where a reader finds out that a static glibc link is impossible without running into it. `cargo xtask link-lines` writes them, `--check` is in `cargo xtask ci` and in the quick job of CI, and the cache directory prints as a placeholder so the files are the same on every machine. tamnd/rucc#732.

- `rucc_stub::coff` writes the import library a Windows link line wants, which is the container those names go into and the other half of section 9.4. It is not the ELF writer with different constants: a Windows program links against an archive of tiny objects rather than against a stub DLL, so what comes out is an `ar` archive holding two linker symbol indexes a Unix archive does not have, three objects of boilerplate, one 20 byte import record per export, and a weak alias object for the few exports that are a second spelling of another export in the same library. The two things section 9.1 says must be right are both in it: a data export gets `__imp_name` and no plain name, because a plain one would resolve to a thunk and the program would read a jump instruction as the value of a variable, and an i386 record carries both halves of the `__stdcall` decoration, the symbol `_GetProcAddress@8` to define and the name `GetProcAddress` to ask the DLL for. tamnd/rucc#732.

- It is held against `llvm-dlltool` by byte equality rather than by a round trip, and that is the whole of how it was built. There is no specification for this file worth the name: Microsoft documents the 20 byte record and says nothing about which of the five name types a given `.def` line turns into, nothing about either linker index, and nothing about the three boilerplate objects, so a writer built from the documentation produces something a linker accepts and a loader then cannot resolve. Every one of mingw-w64's 2124 `.def` files was built both ways for four architectures, and the 8496 libraries are the same file to the byte. A round trip would not have done, since a reader written next to the writer agrees with the writer's mistakes, which is a failure this crate has already had once over the size of a `.hash` entry.

- That comparison is what found the three rules, none of which is written down anywhere. A name carrying its own decoration gets no i386 underscore, where decorated means it starts with `@` or `?` or holds `@@`, and 9648 exports in the 32-bit definitions start with `?`. A `== name` has three answers rather than one, tried in LLVM's order: a name type that reaches the target, so `X3DAudioCalculate@20 == _X3DAudioCalculate@20` is a plain name and `UpdateDriverForPlugAndPlayDevicesA@20 == UpdateDriverForPlugAndPlayDevicesA` is an undecorated one, then a weak alias where some record in the same library already claimed that name, which is what the CRT's `getch == _getch` is, and only then the rename with a third string. And the long names member of the archive declares its padded length and pads with a newline, where the two indexes pad with a zero. The first two of those were wrong in the first version and produced libraries that link.

- `cargo xtask implib` is that comparison cut down to what runs anywhere llvm is installed, eight cases across every Windows target in the table, each built by both writers from the same `.def` file on disk and compared byte for byte. A library that differs is reported with the offset, the member it falls in and both values, since an offset on its own is not a diagnosis. `llvm-readobj` is asked about our file as well where it is installed, which rules out the one thing byte equality cannot catch, two writers agreeing on a file no reader will look at. Neither tool is a build dependency. GNU dlltool is deliberately not accepted as the reference, because it names its members after the output file and defines `_head_<file>` where LLVM defines `__IMPORT_DESCRIPTOR_<dll>`, so its output is a different file by design. The reference has to be llvm 19 or newer and an older one is refused rather than compared against, because llvm 18 writes a different file: the handling of a `.def` rename was rewritten for ARM64EC in 19, so holding this writer to 18 would mean writing libraries no current toolchain writes. What says which is which is the usage message, since llvm-dlltool has no `--version` at all and the list of machines it accepts gained `arm64ec` at that same release. Ubuntu 24.04 has 18 as its default llvm and 19 and 20 by name, so the CI job asks for one of those.

- ARM64EC is refused rather than guessed at. Its records carry the mangled spelling of every function and export the plain one, so an import library written for it the way the others are written would link and then fail to load, which is exactly the quiet failure section 9.1 is about. Section 6.8 has the target at tier 4, so the cost of saying no is low and the error says what would have to be done instead.

- `fold`, the machine level addressing mode pass, takes an address that several instructions read rather than only one that a single instruction reads. That is the half of `spec/optimizer/37-machine-level-optimization.md` section 37.4 that was missing, and it is the half GCC's own description of `fold-mem-offsets` is about: an address worked out once and read by several memory operations at different offsets. Nobody owns such an address, so no peephole can take it, and the question stops being whether one memory operand can absorb it and becomes whether every one of them can. tamnd/rucc#785.

- It is all or nothing over the set of readers. Folding into some of them leaves the address computation where it is for the rest, so the folds bought nothing and the registers the address reads are now live across instructions that no longer read them. One reader that cannot take the address therefore turns down the whole set it belongs to, and so does a write to a register the address reads anywhere between the address and the last of its readers, which is the set's question rather than the first reader's. An instruction that reads the address twice counts as a reader that cannot take it, since only one of its two reads is the memory operand.

- A symbol relative address is left to its one reader, and that is a measured refusal rather than anything about correctness. A reader that reads through a register has room in it for a register and a displacement, and an address made of those goes into the room the reader already has, whoever takes it. A symbol does not: the reader has to name the symbol, which is a whole address word, so each reader that takes one grows by the difference while the `lea` it replaces is saved once. Folding those as well loses 2643 bytes over the corpus at -O2 against 386 gained, and nearly all of the loss is the soft float and bit counting expansions, which read one global thirty or forty times each. The gains are the same shape with three readers rather than thirty, where the register that comes free is worth more than the displacements cost, and pricing that trade wants an encoding size model this pass has no business holding, since it is written against `FrameInsts` rather than against one machine.

- What is left gains 8 bytes over 1802 programs at -O2, in four of them, and makes nothing larger. That is the honest size of it today and the reason is worth writing down, because the shape the pass was written for mostly never reaches it. The example in the issue is `q[0]`, `q[3]` and `q[7]` off one pointer, and the instruction selector already folds all three, because the address each store reads is an add of its own with exactly one reader. What does reach this pass is an address several instructions read after value numbering has given them one name, and today that is `alias-analysis.*.computed-same-index`, a store and a load through the same computed subscript, where `leaq (%rax,%rdx,4), %rdx` goes and both readers carry the whole address themselves. tamnd/rucc#784 and tamnd/rucc#866 are what make that set large.

- The instruction selector now shows an operand as the instruction that computed it whenever every reader of that instruction can take it, where before it asked for exactly one reader. The reasoning behind the old test was that folding does not delete the instruction for anybody else, so doing the work twice is not a saving, and that reasoning is about the set of readers and was being applied one reader at a time. When all of them take it there is nobody left to read it and the instruction goes. An address a store and a load share is the shape this was written for, since a memory operand has room for the whole of an address and both readers have one. tamnd/rucc#866.

- It is all or nothing, and it is decided twice. A value some readers took and some did not is the case that buys nothing: the instruction stays for the reader that did not take it, and the reader that did take it now does the same work again. So the block is decided, every value that was taken by fewer readers than it has is put back, and the block is decided again, because taking a value away from a reader changes which rule that reader matches. The loop ends because the set of values put back only grows.

- Whether a reader can take it is the question the rule table already answers, and no new list of what may be folded was written. A plan that shows the operand expanded either matches a rule or it does not, and a load or a store in the operand position matches nothing, because no rule reaches through one. The count of readers is over the whole function rather than over the block, so a value read from another block is read from a register there and is never taken here.

- Over the corpus at -O2 it gains 13479 bytes across 1830 programs, in 122 of them, and makes nothing larger. All 1876 results are unchanged. The four largest are the unrolled `two-loops` restructurings at about 1480 bytes each, which is the case the issue was written about: a loop that fills an array and a loop that reads it back share one address per element once value numbering has given the two subscripts one name, and each of those addresses was being worked out into a register of its own and held from the first loop to the second. `loop-idiom` counting and copying at sixteen wide is the next group, at 200 to 430 bytes each. Measured against the pipeline before value numbering the same change gains 367 bytes in ten programs, so most of what it is worth is what it gives back to `number`.

- `-fdelete-null-pointer-checks` and `-fno-delete-null-pointer-checks`, both accepted and neither doing anything, which is the honest answer rather than a request being dropped. Nothing here concludes that a pointer is not null from the fact that the program read through it, because no pass records such a conclusion: a load says where it read and nothing else, and a comparison against null is an ordinary comparison of two values no fact is held about. So a function that reads through a pointer and then tests it keeps the test, which is exactly what the negative spelling asks for and what the kernel writes it for. The positive spelling is a request to assume more than this compiler assumes, and giving less costs speed rather than correctness, so it is taken rather than refused, the same way `-fstrict-aliasing` is.

- `crates/rucc/tests/nullchecks.rs` compiles three shapes gcc folds away with that conclusion in hand, at every optimization level, and asserts each still asks its question, so the day a pass starts drawing the conclusion is the day the flag has to start turning it off. One of the three is a place this compiler is more conservative than gcc is in either mode: gcc folds a test of `&p->b` to true whether or not the flag is given, and this keeps it. tamnd/rucc#489.
- `-fsigned-char` and `-funsigned-char`, which say what a plain `char` is, with `-fno-signed-char` and `-fno-unsigned-char` as the second spelling of each, which is how gcc reads them, so there are four ways to write two answers and the last one written wins. Nothing is assumed until one of them is given, because the target's own ABI is the answer otherwise and it is not the same answer everywhere: x86-64 and Apple's arm64 say signed and Linux's arm64 says unsigned. What the command line decides goes into the target rather than beside it, so the one place every part of the compiler already asks is the one place that knows, and `__CHAR_UNSIGNED__` follows from the same field, so a header sees what was decided. A plain `char` stays a third type distinct from both `signed char` and `unsigned char` under either answer. tamnd/rucc#489.

- `-fshort-enums` and `-fno-short-enums`, which represent an enumeration nothing wrote an underlying type for in the smallest integer type that holds its enumerators. The candidate list starts at `unsigned char` and `signed char` instead of at `unsigned int` and `int` and is otherwise the list that was already there, so the signedness is still settled first by whether anything is negative and the width second by what fits. That is why `200` is one byte and `-200` is two, and why an enumeration too wide for an `int` is the same type under both answers and cannot tell the flag was given. An enumerator is still `int` wherever the value fits in one, and an enumeration the program wrote a type for under C23 is still that type, which is the only way to write a size down and have it survive a command line that says otherwise.

- Both of those change the ABI rather than the code, which is what makes them different from the rest of that group: a program built half with them and half without is not a slower program, it is one whose halves disagree about the size of a structure, and nothing about that disagreement is a link error. So `crates/rucc/tests/abi_flags.rs` asserts what a program can see rather than what the driver recorded. Every case is a `_Static_assert` compiled under both answers, so a flag taken by the driver and dropped before the type system fails there rather than in somebody's object file, and neither set of assertions can be vacuous. The sizes are gcc 16's, measured in both `-std=c17` and `-std=c23`, which agree.
### Changed

- A glibc target reads a header directory named after its architecture family and a musl target one named after its architecture, which is a question for the libc rather than a scheme of ours. Zig 0.16 ships twelve glibc directories and seventeen musl ones for the same machines, because glibc installs one `bits/` tree per family and musl installs one per architecture and ABI. glibc's `x86` serves i386, x86-64 and x32 together and the files in it do the splitting themselves, 22 of its 31 `bits/` headers branching on `__x86_64__`, `__ILP32__` or `__WORDSIZE`, starting with `bits/wordsize.h`. So `Sysroot::header_arch` now takes the environment as well as the architecture, and `x86_64-linux-gnu` reads `include/x86` where it used to read `include/x86_64`. The sysroots are still one per tuple, because the link inputs are not shared even where the headers are. tamnd/rucc#926.

- Section 13.1's budget has a row for the kernel headers and a larger total. The row was missing, and adding it showed that the existing rows already came to 62 MB against a stated base of 60, so the total is restated at 75 rather than nudged. The two kernel figures are measured rather than estimated: Zig 0.16's shared tree is 990 headers and 9.0 MB, 1.9 MB gzipped, and the seven per-architecture directories this target table needs come to 1.3 MB, 187 KB gzipped.

- Section 8.7 says that `--sysroot` replaces our header directories rather than composing with them, which is what the code has always done and what gcc and clang both do with the flag. The sentence that said otherwise was written before there was an implementation to disagree with. A user who wants their own headers and our libc says so with `-isystem`, where the ordering is theirs to state. tamnd/rucc#907.

- `ivopts` lets a loop's own counter serve an address, which is the ordinary `a[i]`, so the cost model finally gets to choose between reading an array through an addressing mode and walking a pointer of its own. It could not before. An address group's sequence is a pointer and a counter is an integer, and the type guard in `serve` read that difference as a conversion, which for a value it is: converting a sequence from one width to another is arithmetic on the sequence and `crate::scev` will not do it for a symbolic one, so that half of the guard stands. For an address it is not a conversion at all, because the pointer is not the counter converted, it is `base + counter * scale`, and the width the counter is read at is the index register's rather than the sequence's. What the refusal did was leave every address group with exactly one candidate that could serve it, the pointer made for that group, and a group with one server is a choice made before the cost model is asked rather than by it. Reading a counter narrower than an index register now costs the extension in front of it, which the cost table already prices. tamnd/rucc#918.

- `ivopts` runs at `-O2` and `-O3`, followed by `simplify-cfg`. The pass has been written, tested and registered for a while and no level ran it, because switching it on made programs bigger. Over the 1830 programs of tamnd/rucc-corpus that compile at `-O2` on x86-64, the pass on its own is 13440 bytes of `.text` larger, which is 1.05 percent; with the `simplify-cfg` behind it that takes away the counter the loop stopped using, 7566 larger; and with the choice above, 1219 smaller, over 214 programs that shrink and 74 that grow. All 1876 results compile to the same status and run to byte identical output in every one of those arrangements, so what was wrong with the pass was never that it was wrong. The bytes come from the families it exists for, the induction variable programs by 644 and the loop restructuring ones by 424, and a loop walking an array of `int` through a mode the machine has is now left alone instead of being given a pointer that costs it an increment and a register. tamnd/rucc#917.

- `ivopts` and `simplify-cfg` cannot be separated, and there is a test on the levels that says so. `crate::dce` cannot remove the parameter that carries a counter nothing reads any more, and says so in its own documentation: the only reader left is the addition feeding the parameter back, and a use count never reaches zero on a cycle. `crate::simplify_cfg` can, and was written for this. Half of what the pass used to cost was that nobody had run it.

- `licm` lets a cheap computation ride out of a full loop when something expensive behind it is waiting on the result, and takes it back out again if nothing was. The pass decides each instruction as it reads it, and at that point what reads the instruction has not been read yet, so a cheap link in a chain that ends in something worth moving was being refused on pressure and the expensive end of the chain was then never a candidate at all, since an instruction whose operand stayed behind is not invariant. That is the same passenger idea `trim` already applies to the instructions that cost nothing, applied to the instructions that cost something and are not worth it alone. The pressure miss is now counted where the passenger is put back rather than where it is provisionally allowed, because reporting a computation that carried an expensive one out as having been left in the loop says the opposite of what happened. tamnd/rucc#898.

- On the matrix multiply in `bench/safety` the chain that this unblocks starts with a sign extend and ends in the loop split guard's third query about how much room is left from a pointer, which is invariant in the middle loop of a three deep nest. The program goes from 373113826 instructions to 356648839 and from 30255077 in `__rucc_extent` to 15221423, so the four queries per entry to the innermost loop that started this are now one. The other seven programs move by less than a hundredth of a percent. `cargo xtask pressure` goes the other way, from 79 slots and 337 stack moves to 89 and 351, and part of that is the pressure accounting being wrong about chains, which is #898.

- `licm` runs again after `split` at `-O2` and above, and it is the only pass in either level that looks at what `split` wrote. The guard goes in the preheader of the loop being split, and the preheader of an inner loop is a block inside the loops around it, so on a matrix multiply the guard's four queries about how much room is left from a pointer run 40000 times and two of them are about a pointer that has not changed since it was allocated. The pass that would have taken those out ran seven passes before the guard existed. tamnd/rucc#893.

- Moving them needed three more things, each of which was `licm` answering a question it had not been asked. The cost table priced a query at 1, the default for anything not named, when it lowers to a call that retires about 377 instructions, so the pressure test threw it out as not worth a register. `CapOf` was priced at 1 as well and holds no register at all, because `crates/rucc-safety/src/lower.rs` deletes every one of them. And the refusal for a loop that writes memory applied, though a store through a program pointer cannot change how big an allocation is. What can change that is a free, so the test is now the one `split::shaped` already uses to decide whether it may touch a loop at all: a call without `nofree`, inline assembly, or the end of a lifetime.

- `speculate` no longer refuses to move a query about how much room is left from a pointer. It was being refused by the catch-all for anything with effects, which is the exact conflation that file's header warns about for division, in the other direction. The query reads no memory the program has, dereferences nothing, cannot trap and cannot fault, and it is on the effects list for one reason only, that two of them either side of a free are different numbers. That is a question about where it may move, which is `licm`'s to ask and which `licm` now asks, and it is not a question about whether it is safe to run early.

- Together that is 10 to 26 percent of the instructions on the four `bench/safety` programs whose cost is the code around the checks rather than the checks: the strided column sum goes from 2.13x the uninstrumented build to 1.57x, the string scan from 1.70x to 1.43x, and the matrix multiply's time in `__rucc_extent` halves to the instruction. The three pointer chasing programs do not move at all, which is what document 13 section 13.1's census predicted for them. Recorded there with the table.

- Milestone S4's register pressure measurement has an answer and it is not the one document 05 section 5.2.1 warned about. The monitor takes `bench/safety` from 24 stack slots and 86 stack moves to 89 and 374, and the pointer-heavy rows are not where it happens: the tree walk spills nothing either way and the two chasing rows add 6 and 22 moves against 42, 91, 66 and 61 for the four array rows. Compiling both sides with `-fno-split` leaves 170 moves, so 204 of the 288 the monitor adds are the loop split's guard, which is 71 percent, and none of it is a materialized capability. Recorded in document 13 sections 13.5 and 13.7 and tracked on tamnd/rucc#893.

- Document 13 section 13.1 has the instruction census the rest of the section argues about, which splits the Tier E set in two. Three programs spend three quarters or more of their instructions inside the runtime and cost 1.5x to 2.5x on the clock, which is the section's own claim as a number. The other four have nothing left in the runtime at all: `a-matrix-multiply` runs no bounds check and no derivation check anywhere, its 13.8 percent is 160003 guard queries, and it is still 2.8x. Half the set has no checks left at run time and is still twice as slow, and what is left is the code emitted around where they used to be.

- Check elimination can be asked one source of facts at a time, which is what `spec/safe-memory/17-open-questions.md` question 3 needed and what it is now answered with. `-fdischarge-objects`, `-fdischarge-dominance` and `-fdischarge-summaries` each run the pass with one of document 07 section 7.2's sources on and the rest off, `-fdischarge-narrow` runs it with all three and no range widening, and `-fdischarge-every` runs it with everything under a name of its own so the parts and the whole land in the same place in the pipeline. `cargo xtask cost` now takes `-f` flags beside its level, so the same question can be put to the clock. None of this changes what `-O2` does.

- The answer is two answers pointing opposite ways. The sources hardly overlap in which checks they discharge: on the SQLite amalgamation with the loop passes off they take 11.9, 16.5 and 2.8 percent of 102858 checks alone and 26.0 percent together, against 31.2 percent added up. They do not compose on the clock at all: the Tier E programs run at 4.33x with neither kind of elimination, 4.09x with the discharge pass alone, 1.73x with loop hoisting and splitting alone, and 1.80x with both, which over three more passes is the same number as the loop passes on their own. A quarter of a program's checks can be a quarter that never runs twice, and the loop passes have already taken the ones that were costing anything. Tier E is 1.74x to 1.80x rather than 1.3x, and what closes the rest of that gap is the loop rows `split` still refuses rather than a fifth source of facts. tamnd/rucc#430.

- The list of passes that read what `extents`, `params`, `heap` and `nofree` write held one name, so the first version of that measurement got no table of globals and no caller guarantees and answered that there were none. That is the missed optimization the list's own comment warns about, it made the numbers wrong rather than merely low, and a test now holds the list and the pass registry to the same answer.

- Loop splitting takes a derivation check whose walk begins a distance into the object that the loop works out rather than one written in the program. `a[start + i]` walked over `i` is the shape, and it is what an inner loop over one column of a matrix looks like once the outer loop's subscript is folded into the start. The window used to have to go on the address the first iteration touches, which for this shape is a different address from the one the check is about, so the check stayed in both halves. It now goes on the pointer the check names, which is the object the check asks about, and the guard takes the distance off the window it measured there and asks for that distance to be at or above zero. That is a multiply and two subtracts in the block in front of the loop, over a value the outer loop already worked out. `bench/safety/a-strided-column-sum` goes from 4.3x to 1.6x on the Tier E table and stops being the worst row on it, the geomean goes from about 2.0x to about 1.8x, and on the SQLite amalgamation that census row goes from 446 checks at 155 sites to 402 at 149. tamnd/rucc#885.
- Loop splitting takes a derivation check whose own pointer does not move while the walk it follows does. The guard measures a window at the first iteration's address, and a derivation check names the capability of the pointer that went in, so those two have to be the same object before the window says anything. Until now the only pairs that qualified were a walk starting on the pointer itself and a pair of pointers walking side by side at one step. A pointer that sits a fixed number of bytes from where the walk starts and stays there now qualifies as well: the window is measured from whichever of the two addresses is lower and widened to cover the gap, so the first iteration's window holds the pair and every later one says where the walk has reached. On the SQLite amalgamation that census row goes from 608 checks at 221 sites to 446 at 155, the object carries 148 fewer calls to `__rucc_check_deriv`, and it is 24368 bytes smaller. A pointer that walks away at a step of its own is still refused, because no window a fixed number of bytes wide holds a pair whose distance changes every time round. tamnd/rucc#885.

- Every level above `-O0` now runs the peephole once more, after the `fold` that comes late in the list. Folding does not remove an instruction whose answer is a constant somebody still adds to something, it only writes the constant down, so an index the unroller worked out to zero was left as `ptr_add x, 0`. That is an identity the peephole takes and nothing else in the list is about, and until now the last thing any level did to that arithmetic was fold it, so the addition reached the selector and was written out as an `addq $0` the machine runs for nothing. tamnd/rucc#875.

- Over the corpus at `-O2` the extra run is worth 3000 bytes across 1830 programs, 224 of them smaller and 4 larger by 22 bytes between them, with all 1876 results unchanged. The largest savings are the `loop-hoist` cases under register pressure at about 126 bytes each, then the twelve wide `register-pressure` loops, the eight way switch lowerings and `baseline.sort` at about 55 each. Measured before the selector learned to fold into every reader at once the same change was worth 3105 bytes, with ten programs larger rather than four, so what the two of them do to the same addresses composes.

## 0.10.19

### Changed

- Check elimination asks the allocation rule about a walk whose step is a value, and not only about an address it can name outright. `discharge` reads how many bytes `malloc` made off the call and believes it where the program has tested the answer against null, and until now it only asked that on the paths where both ends of the access come out as one base and two constants. A subscript is not a constant, so a loop reading `p[i]` inside a `malloc` it tested kept every check it started with however plainly the call said how big the object was. Now the range of addresses the walk can reach is asked of the allocation the same way it is already asked of a local, for the bounds check and the derivation check. The lifetime check is not asked, because what a call to an allocator says is an extent and never a lifetime, and a `free` in the same function is exactly what that check is there to report. tamnd/rucc#880.
- The value range analysis reads the end of a loop counter that the exit test does not mention off the counter's own recurrence, so `i` in `for (i = 0; i < 200; i++)` comes back as `[0, 199]` rather than as `[-2147483647, 199]`. Everything built on the counter gets the same benefit, which is what lets check elimination see that an index into a `malloc` of a known size stays inside it.
- `-fopt-info-missed` now says why a derivation check `discharge` could not read is being kept, instead of saying one thing about all of them. The old line was `derivation check left alone, its two pointers are not one base and two constants`, it was 4539 checks on SQLite and the largest single row in the discharge census, and it was wrong about every one of them. All 4539 have both ends readable, both ends off one base and a step the ranges put numbers on. What is actually missing is how many bytes belong to the object, and where the pointer came from is what says which piece of work would supply it, so the row is now eight rows: 2155 for a pointer this function loaded out of memory, 1321 for one it was handed, 485 for one into a global, 360 for one a call gave back, 218 for a local the walk can reach past the end of, and none at all for the two shapes the old message described. tamnd/rucc#878 is the global slice, which is the one where somebody already knows the answer. Nothing is discharged that was not discharged before and no object changes size. tamnd/rucc#869.

## 0.10.18

### Added

- `number`, block local value numbering, which is the other half of `spec/optimizer/16-gvn-and-pre.md` and the half `load-forward` deliberately left out. One table per block from what an instruction computes to the first instruction that computed it, and a hit points the later one's readers at the earlier one and removes it. It runs immediately before `load-forward` in all five levels that have `load-forward`, and the two are one arrangement: `load-forward` compares addresses by identity, and a subscript the front end lowered twice is the same multiply and add twice, so until the two have one name a store and the load that reads it straight back look like two addresses. The version over the dominator tree finds strictly more and is not this. Section 16.1 is where the argument for that lives: hash-consing under the e-graph gives the acyclic case for nothing, what is left over is the cyclic case and wants Tarjan's algorithm over the SSA graph, and both halves of a subscript are in the block the subscript is in. tamnd/rucc#837.

- What counts as the same thing is the opcode, the flags, the result type, whatever the instruction carries besides its operands, and the operands, and a difference in any of the five is two values. The flags are in the key rather than merged, because merging two adds of the same pair onto the one that promises its result cannot wrap hands the other a promise nobody made, and merging onto the one that promises nothing throws away a promise somebody did make. The operands go through what the pass has already decided, so a value on its way out is compared as the value it is going out in favour of, which is what lets a chain of three or four instructions collapse rather than only its bottom one. Two operands of a commutative opcode are put in one order. A constant is keyed on its bits rather than on its index into the immediate table, since that table is not interned and two sevens written at two times are two entries in it.

- What may be merged is `has_effects` answering no, which the IR already defines as exactly this property, and which is written as a list of the pure opcodes rather than the impure ones, so an opcode added later is impure until somebody says otherwise. Three refusals on top of it: `mem_entry`, which is the name of memory on the way in rather than a computation, anything with other than exactly one result, which is the checked arithmetic, and anything carrying a payload that cannot be compared by value, which in practice is `blockaddr` alone. Division is allowed, and that is only safe because the pass is block local: the first division is in the same block, so it has already run, and if it was going to trap the second was never reached.

- Over the corpus at -O2, `.text` across 1786 comparable programs goes from 1312588 bytes to 1280809, which is 31779 bytes and takes the ratio against gcc 16's 1053125 from 1.2464 to 1.2162. 667 programs are smaller by 47374 bytes in total and 210 are larger by 15595. Correctness is unchanged, the same 22 results wrong and the same 26 needing something not built yet on both sides. Compile time is unchanged, measured interleaved over 1864 programs for three rounds each: 14855, 14517 and 15052 ms without the pass against 14735, 14367 and 14716 ms with it. The medians are 14855 and 14716, which has the pass 0.9 per cent ahead against a 3.6 per cent spread inside the baseline's own three runs, so the reading is that it costs nothing this measurement can see rather than that it pays for itself.

- The case this was written for is the largest single win and also, in another shape, the largest single loss, and both belong in the same sentence. `loop-restructure.i64.8.column-major` goes from 3193 bytes to 1138 and `loop-restructure.i64.4.fused`, which was the worst ratio in the corpus at 1941 against gcc 16's 385, goes to 1283. `loop-restructure.i32.8.two-loops` goes the other way, from 1375 to 2904. 10642 of the 15595 bytes lost are that one mechanism, across `loop-restructure` and `loop-idiom`, and it is not a decision the pass made. Giving one address two readers is the whole point of the pass, and the instruction selector shows an operand as the instruction that computed it only when exactly one instruction reads it, on the reasoning that folding into one of two readers leaves the computation there for the other. That is right when what is folded is work and wrong when it is an address every reader could have carried in its own memory operand, which would have left the computation dead. A pair of unrolled loops that fills an array and then reads it back has one address per element with a store reading it and a load reading it, so every one of them is worked out into a register of its own instead, and sixty-four of those live at a time is a 432 byte frame of spills. tamnd/rucc#866 is that refusal and is what closes it.

### Changed

- The guard in front of a split loop now asks the runtime one question per address rather than one per check. A loop that walks a single pointer carries a bounds check, a liveness check and a derivation check on it, and after tamnd/rucc#869 the guard sizes a window for each of them, so the preheader asked how big the object at that address was three times over and sometimes five. On SQLite that was 4184 calls to `__rucc_extent` for 591 split loops, and it is 1674 now, with the object 126352 bytes smaller and the same 591 loops split and the same 78801 checks left standing. What stopped two questions at one address being the same question was the byte count each one carried, which came from how many times the loop went round. That number was only ever a bound on how far the query walked the lifetime plane, and tamnd/rucc#861 replaced the walk with a probe and a halving, so a larger ask costs the same and is answered just as truthfully. tamnd/rucc#873 took the bound off the loops nobody counted and this takes it off the rest, so every window is sized from one ask for everything and the trip count is out of this pass entirely. The benchmark set does not move, because what this saves is paid once in front of a loop that then runs for a long time. tamnd/rucc#871.

## 0.10.17

### Added

- `-ftrapv`, which is the other answer to the question `-fwrapv` answers: a signed overflow stops the program rather than producing the number the machine gives. It is the one flag in that family that asks for something to be generated, so each of the four operations that can overflow in a signed type becomes a call to the runtime routine that does it and checks it. An add becomes `__addvsi3`, a subtract `__subvsi3`, a multiply `__mulvsi3` and a negation `__negvsi2`, with `di` in place of `si` for a `long long`. Those are libgcc's names and libgcc is linked already, so an object this compiler produced stops the same way as an object gcc produced beside it.

- A shift is not among them and neither is a divide, matching gcc: what a shift that moves a bit past the top does is undefined for a reason of its own, and the one division that overflows already stops on the hardware. Nothing narrower than a word is checked either, because C promotes a `char` and a `short` before any operator sees them and the addition that could overflow is at `int` by the time it is lowered. Unsigned arithmetic, floating point and the multiply behind a subscript are all left alone.

- Writing `-ftrapv` and `-fwrapv` on one command line is writing a contradiction, since a program cannot both wrap and stop, and the last one written is the answer. That is gcc 16's behaviour and was measured rather than read out of the manual, which says nothing about it. One difference from gcc is deliberate: gcc folds a signed overflow it can work out at compile time and emits no check for it, so a `-ftrapv` build of gcc runs past an overflow the optimizer could see, and this one stops. The check folding removes is the one a person who wrote the flag would most want kept. tamnd/rucc#489.

### Changed
