It is one program in four files. `callee.c` defines fifty eight functions, each of which checks every parameter it was handed against the value it was promised, `caller.c` calls them all and checks what came back, `abi.h` is the types and the prototypes and it is everything the two sides share. Ten of the signatures are written by hand for the cases a classifier gets wrong, more integers than there are registers, more doubles than there are registers, the two mixed, small aggregates, float aggregates, an aggregate too big for registers, a return by hidden pointer, a return of nothing, `long double`, and a nested struct beside a union. The other forty eight are drawn from a fixed seed. Every scalar in the program has a value of its own, so a value that turns up in the wrong place belongs to some other parameter and the report can say which.

- `report.c` is the fourth file, and it exists because rucc has no built-in system include directories. It holds the failure counter and the one `fprintf`, so neither file under test includes a libc header, and the two sides reach it through `void abi_fail(const char *, const char *)`, which is the one signature every ABI on the table agrees about.

- `cargo xtask abi-differential`, which compiles that corpus four ways and runs each result: both halves by the reference compiler, both halves by rucc, and one each way round. The two mixed builds are the point, because a calling convention is not visible in the source and `_Static_assert` cannot reach it, so the only way to ask two compilers whether they agree about one is to make them finish each other's calls. The two matching builds are what make a failure readable, since a corpus that is wrong about C fails all four. All four pass today on x86-64 Linux, which is the one target with a back end, and that is the honest scope of the claim.

- A crash is retried up to three times and a wrong value is believed the first time, which is not a hedge but a measurement. The first mixed run segfaulted once and could not be made to do it again, so the all-reference control was run nine hundred times: seven of those crashed too, in a build where every instruction came from gcc. The core said `qemu-x86_64`. So the emulated container drops roughly one program in a hundred and thirty on the floor, a rate that would have been reported as an intermittent rucc bug if the control build had not been there, and it is the first entry in the known divergence list in `spec/cross-compile/14-testing.md` section 14.10.

- `bin/compile-abi-signatures` in tamnd/rucc-cross compiles both halves for all thirty nine spellable rows of the target table, which is a much smaller claim than the differential run makes over a much larger set of targets. It is what catches a generated shape that is fine on x86-64 and a constraint violation somewhere else, on the day it is added rather than on the day somebody writes that back end.

- `xtask/src/runner.rs`, which is now the one answer to how an x86-64 Linux program gets run from here: directly on x86-64 Linux and in a container anywhere else. `safety` and `cost` each had a private copy of it and the differential harness would have been the third.

- A bounds check on bytes inside a local is discharged from the declaration rather than from a check that ran earlier. `spec/safe-memory/07-check-elimination.md` section 7.2 lists four sources of a discharge, puts the frontend first and says it is where most of the win is, and this is that source: an `alloca` of a fixed size makes one storage instance of that many bytes and says how many in its own payload, so the bytes from its address to that many further along are inside one instance for the same reason a passing check says its own range is. It is the same `covered.i64` rule answering, asked with the local as the fact instead of a range some earlier check established, so there was nothing new to prove.

- Two things this gets that a fact left by a check does not. It is there before anything has run, so the first access to a local goes rather than only the second, and plenty of functions touch a local once. And no call takes it away, because a callee cannot free a frame slot: what it could free is whatever a pointer stored in the slot points at, and that is a different instance and a different check. So the local is asked separately rather than kept in the set the walk empties at every call it cannot see through.

- A lifetime check in a local widens to the whole local, which is the same fact used the other way round. One of those checks says one byte and is worth nothing until a bounds fact widens it to a range, and the widest range there can be for an address computed from a local is the local, so a lifetime check on one field of a local struct now discharges the checks on every other field up to the first call.

- Only the fixed size form. A variable length array is an `alloca` with an operand, and its payload's size field reads zero because how many bytes it is is a value the program works out, so reading the field anyway would discharge every check in the array. That has a test of its own.

- Measured at `-O2 -fsafety=detect`. On the SQLite 3.53.4 amalgamation the bounds checks discharged go from 7698 to 10606 of 35720 and the lifetime checks from 7603 to 8405. Over the 1122 programs of the optimizer corpus, bounds go from 269 to 746 of 2577 and lifetime from 242 to 502. Over the 124 programs in `tests/safety`, bounds go from 17 to 39 of 207 and lifetime from 15 to 26. Derivation checks are untouched at none discharged, which is the next thing to do rather than a surprise.

- Derivation checks are discharged too, which is the third kind and the one nothing had touched. `rucc-safety` puts a `check_deriv` after every `ptr_add` off a pointer, and it does not ask about a range: it asks whether the pointer that came out is still in the storage instance the pointer that went in belongs to. What answers it is one fact holding both ends. A `check_bounds` that passed put its whole range inside one instance, so if both addresses are in that range the second is in the first's instance, and a local is a fact of the same shape asked the same way.

- One fact and not one for each end, which is the part worth writing down. Two facts saying two addresses are each inside some instance say nothing about whether it is the same instance, and that is the only thing the check is about. There is a test for exactly that: two checked ranges sixty four bytes apart, both ends covered, and the walk between them stays.

- Nothing is recorded from a derivation check that stays. What it establishes is that two addresses share an instance, which is not a range of bytes, does not fit in what the walk carries and has no rule to be asked with. The pointer it is about nearly always gets a `check_bounds` of its own a few instructions later that establishes the range properly.

- Measured the same way. On the SQLite amalgamation the derivation checks discharged go from none to 1697 of 32604, over the optimizer corpus from none to 438 of 989, and over `tests/safety` from none to 27 of 190. The gap between SQLite and the corpus is the point: a corpus program walks inside things it declared, and SQLite walks off pointers it was handed. Of what is left on SQLite, 3816 are walks this pass cannot read at all because the step is a value rather than a number, and the rest are walks it read and could not answer because nobody knows how big the object at the far end of a parameter is. That is section 7.5's summaries and not this pass.

## 0.9.2

### Fixed

- `__sync_lock_release(&lock, guard)` stored `guard` rather than the zero that gives the lock back. Everything after the first argument of a `__sync_` call is the list of variables the barrier promises to protect, which is a list to read and not a value to write, and the check for the one name in the family that is handed no value asked whether a second argument was there instead of asking which name it was. So the one call whose whole job is to put a zero somewhere wrote whatever the caller happened to name, and a spin lock written the way gcc documents never unlocked. The value written now comes from the name.

- Complete unrolling produced invalid code for a loop with another loop inside it. Copying a body means renaming every value it makes, and the pass renamed as it went, so an operand was settled at the moment the instruction using it was copied. That is only right if the block making a value is always copied before the block reading it, and nothing says it is: the block list comes from the loop analysis in whatever order the search found the blocks. A nest is where the two orders come apart, since the outer loop's blocks include the inner loop's and the inner latch reads what the inner body worked out. The copy of that latch then still named the original body's value, which the copy does not reach, and the verifier said so. Copying now happens in two parts, the instructions first and what they read second, so every name the copy makes is there before anything is settled. `for (x = 0; x < 4; x++) for (y = 0; y < 3; y++) total += x * y;` is the program that found it, sixteen cases in the corpus, and they pass now.

- Loop invariant code motion hoisted a trapping instruction over a call that never comes back. The pass asked whether the instruction was in a block the loop cannot be entered without reaching, which is a question about the shape of the function, and took that for the whole answer. It is half of it. A block the header cannot get past is still a block the program never arrives at if something in front stops it, and a call is what stops it, since a callee may exit, may loop forever or may jump out and what it does comes from the module a pass holding one function does not have. So a division in a loop whose body calls `exit` was worked out in the preheader, where nothing had stopped the program yet, and a program that returned zero died of a division by zero instead. The guarantee is now a walk: the blocks of one turn round the loop are visited in the order they run, and a flag that starts true at the header goes false at the first instruction the program might not come back from. `gcc.c-torture/execute/pr38819.c` is the program that found it, three of the gcc torture crashes at `-O2`, and it passes now.

### Added

- Complete unrolling, `spec/optimizer/29-unrolling-and-peeling.md`, running at `-O2` and `-O3` after `licm`. A loop whose trip count is a number small enough to be worth it becomes that many copies of its body with no branch back, so the counter is a literal in each copy and everything downstream sees straight line code where it had a loop. Partial unrolling and peeling are not here: GCC has both off at every level including `-O3`, and section 29.1 says the reason is that they cost code and win nothing without a vectorizer behind them.

- The three limits are GCC's, `max-completely-peel-times`, `max-completely-peeled-insns` and `max-completely-peel-loop-nest-depth`, and they live in `rucc-cost` with the provenance next to them rather than in the pass. The instruction limit is measured after the folding the unroll enables rather than before it, so an instruction whose operands all become numbers once the counter is one does not count against it. Innermost loops go first, which is what stops a nest from multiplying out: the outer loop is priced against what the inner one actually became.

- No copy keeps its exit test. Every copy but the last is known to take the branch that stays in the loop and the last is known to take the one that leaves, so each gets a jump. Leaving the tests in for a later pass to fold would be the more forgiving thing to build and it is not built, because nothing after this folds an `icmp` of two constants, which is #352 and is deliberate, so they would still be there at the end and the exit block would have gained one predecessor per copy.

- `Bound::under_undefined_overflow` on `scev`, which is the count for a caller compiling a language where signed overflow is undefined. `Bound::proven` answers nothing for any `for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)` in any C program, because the solver puts `StrictOverflow` on every count taken from a signed test, so a pass built on it never fires. What that assumption says is that the count rests on the counter not wrapping, and `-fwrapv` is implemented by not setting `nsw` in the first place, so a count carrying that assumption and nothing else is one the front end has already promised. `hoist` was carrying this reasoning inline and now reads the accessor.
- A loop whose count is an expression rather than a number, which is what section 7.4 is really about, since `for (i = 0; i < n; i++)` is what array code looks like. The check that comes out is the form that carries its extent as an operand, and the preheader works the extent out as `max(scale * value + offset, 0) * step + reach` from the count `scev` gives. The clamp is `Assumption::Approaching` discharged rather than believed: that assumption says the counter starts on the near side of its limit, and when it does not, a loop that tests at the bottom goes round no times and reads one access, which is exactly what a count of zero gives.

- Two conditions on that, and each has its own remark. The count has to have been read as a signed number, because the extent arithmetic sign extends the limit operand, and on a loop whose test was unsigned a large limit would come out negative there, clamp to zero, and leave a check covering one element in front of a loop reading thousands. `Assumption::StrictOverflow` is pushed exactly when the exit test was signed, so asking for it is how that is asked. And the arithmetic has to be arithmetic that cannot wrap, which is established by bounding the count from the width of the type it is read out of and doing the whole calculation in wider arithmetic first.

- `swept.sym.i64` in `crates/rucc-opt/rules/safety.rules`, which is the same question as `swept.i64` for a loop whose extent the compiler does not have as a number. The span and the distance to the furthest access are values there rather than numbers, so the three things the pass promises about what it built are hypotheses inside the formula instead of guards on the numbers, and the pass earns them before it asks. There is no four gigabyte limit on this one, because there is no `i128` reading of a value to part company with the solver's sixty four bit reading.

- A counter as wide as the arithmetic is refused, and so is an unsigned exit test. The first is `for (size_t i = 0; i < n; i++)` and the reason is that bounding a sixty four bit count from the width of its type leaves nothing to bound it with. The second needs the extent computed with the count read the way its own test read it, which is a second arithmetic rather than a condition to loosen. Both are real gaps rather than corners and both are what this owes next. Only forwards, too, since a walk that counts down has its furthest address before its first rather than after.

- Measured on the SQLite 3.53.4 amalgamation at `-O2 -fsafety=detect`, the pass takes 41 checks out of loops across 16 functions where the count that has to be a number took 3 out of one function. Over the 125 programs in `tests/safety` it goes from 15 checks across 4 functions to 18 across 6. Over the 1122 programs of the optimizer corpus it is unchanged at 52 across 24, because those programs are generated with answers the generator computed and their loops run a number of times that is written in them.

- Nearly every refusal reason fires on something real, which is what says they are not conditions nobody reaches. On SQLite the counts are 644 loops that can be left before their bottom test, 379 with a call in them, 328 checks whose address does not walk by a constant, 210 loops whose count is not settled, 164 with no preheader, 64 checks an iteration can finish without reaching, 55 loops with a loop inside, and one each of a count too wide for the arithmetic and a count that is not read as a signed number. The last of those fires twice more over `tests/safety`. The one reason no program here reaches is the address that walks backwards, and that one has a test of its own.

- The last five names of the atomic family, which finishes it apart from `__atomic_signal_fence`: `__atomic_load`, `__atomic_store` and `__atomic_exchange` without the suffix, and `__atomic_test_and_set` and `__atomic_clear`. The first three are the accesses the family has for an object too big to come back in a register, so the value travels through a second pointer the caller hands over rather than being taken or answered. That is one plain access on either side of the atomic one, plain because the object at the end of the caller's pointer is the caller's own and no other thread has its address, which is the entire reason the shape exists. The family is now forty two of the forty three names.

- The flag pair is one byte whatever the pointer it was handed points at, including a `void *`. That is the standard's reading rather than a liberty: the object is an `atomic_flag` and there is no way to read or write one except through these two names, so the type the pointer carries says nothing about the access and the width is the implementation's to fix. gcc 16.2.0 writes a one byte exchange through an `int *` here too. The value that means set is a one, written in one place because what makes the pair work is that `__atomic_clear` puts back exactly the byte `__atomic_test_and_set` reads as evidence the flag was already held. What the first answers is a comparison against zero rather than the byte itself, since the type of the call is `_Bool` and a byte that is neither zero nor one is not one.

- The compare and exchange, in all four of the spellings gcc has for it: `__atomic_compare_exchange_n`, `__atomic_compare_exchange`, `__sync_bool_compare_and_swap` and `__sync_val_compare_and_swap`. Each becomes one IR instruction, `cmpxchg`, which answers two things at once, what was at the address and whether that was what the program expected, and on x86-64 that is `lock cmpxchg` at the width of the object with a `sete` behind it. The width comes from the value rather than from the address, so all four of 8, 16, 32 and 64 bits are there. The ordering and the failure ordering are folded and diagnosed like any other ordering and then dropped, because a locked instruction on this machine is a full barrier whatever was asked for, and the `weak` flag is dropped for the same kind of reason, since the instruction does not fail spuriously and a strong exchange answers a weak request.

- The write back the `__atomic_` pair does on failure is a real branch rather than a store the program always makes. `__atomic_compare_exchange_n(&x, &x, v, 0, o, o)` is a legal program in which the object and the expected object are the same object, and a store made either way would put the old value back over the value the exchange had just written.

- The read modify writes, for the exchange and the addition and the subtraction, which is eleven of the names gcc has: `__atomic_exchange_n`, `__atomic_fetch_add`, `__atomic_fetch_sub`, `__atomic_add_fetch`, `__atomic_sub_fetch`, the four `__sync_` spellings of the same four, and the two lock names `__sync_lock_test_and_set` and `__sync_lock_release`. Each becomes one IR instruction, `atomic_rmw`, and on x86-64 that is `xchg` at the width of the object or `lock xadd`, with a subtraction reaching the second by negating its operand into a register of its own first. A name that asks for the value after the operation rather than the value before gets the instruction and the arithmetic behind it, which is what the machine gives and what gcc writes. A reference count and a spin lock are what these names are in glibc and in the kernel, which is why they come before the rest.

- The object a read modify write names may be a pointer, and gcc does not scale the operand by the size of what it points at. Adding one to an `int *` moves it one byte, measured on gcc 16.2.0, and that is matched rather than corrected, because a program written against gcc is meant to get gcc's answer.

- The two lock names are the two in the older family that are not sequentially consistent. `__sync_lock_test_and_set` is an exchange at acquire and `__sync_lock_release` is a store of zero at release, the zero being of the object's type so the name works on a pointer as well as on a counter.

- The bitwise read modify writes, which is sixteen more of the names gcc has: `__atomic_fetch_and`, `__atomic_fetch_nand`, `__atomic_fetch_or` and `__atomic_fetch_xor`, the four `_fetch` spellings beside them that answer the value afterwards, and the eight `__sync_` spellings of the same four operations. The family is now twenty seven of the forty two names.

- The nand means the and with every bit of the answer flipped, which is what gcc has meant by the name since 4.4 and what both families agree on.

- x86-64 has no single instruction for any of the four, so each is a loop around `lock cmpxchg`: read the word, work out what should be there, put it back if nothing else got in first, and go round again carrying what the exchange found when something did. The loop is built in the IR before instruction selection runs, since a lowering rule rewrites one instruction into instructions and has nowhere to put a block.

- `xchgb`, `xchgw`, `xchgl`, `xchgq` and the four `xadd` widths in the x86-64 instruction description, with the encodings checked against the assembler byte for byte. `xchg` carries no `lock` prefix, since an exchange against memory is locked whether it was asked or not. These are written by name in `rucc-codegen` too, but for a different reason than the compare and exchange: they write one value, so a rule could have named one, and what stops it is that the operation is carried beside the instruction rather than in the head a rule matches on, so one pattern would be all thirteen operations at once. That is a third exemption list with a test of its own.

- `lock`, `cmpxchgb`, `cmpxchgw`, `cmpxchgl` and `cmpxchgq` in the x86-64 instruction description, with the encodings checked against the assembler byte for byte. The lowering is written by name in `rucc-codegen` rather than by a rule, because a rule replaces a term with the value one instruction computes and this instruction computes two, and the exemption list that records the decision is guarded by a test that only lets an instruction onto it when the description says it writes more than one value.

## 0.9.1

### Fixed

- Restored the record layout corpus, the differential check accounting and the sysroot reproducibility note, all three of which #654 removed. Its branch was up to date with the release commit, so the deletions were not a stale base: the single commit on it records them, which is what happens when a commit is made from a tree that some other piece of work had already taken those files out of. The effect was that `tests/abi-corpus`, `cargo xtask abi-corpus`, the `abi-corpus` half of `build-tools/rucc-targets`, the `bit_field_style` and `unnamed_bit_field_aligns` fields on `TargetInfo`, the accounting half of `xtask/src/safety.rs`, its nightly job and section 14.9 of `spec/cross-compile/14-testing.md` all went away in a change whose subject was loop invariant code motion. Nothing in it is new work: every file comes back at the content it had at the 0.9.0 tag, and the corpus regenerates byte for byte from the restored generator, which is the check that says the restore is complete rather than approximately complete.

- `scev` reads through a block parameter of a block with only one way into it, which is what canonicalization leaves behind. `canon` splits the back edge of a loop to give it a latch of its own, and after that the value going round is not the increment the loop computed, it is a parameter of a block that does nothing but pass the increment on. So the trip count of a loop in a real function came back as nothing, and every pass that asks for one was being told no on every loop the pipeline actually produces. Reading through it is undoing a rename rather than an analysis: a block with one predecessor has one value for each of its parameters and it is the argument that predecessor passes.

- The three record layout disagreements the corpus of 0.9.0 found, and a fourth that came out of writing the tests for them. All fifteen files in `tests/abi-corpus` now compile clean against the pinned Zig 0.16 reference, where seven of them did not.

- Windows lays bit-fields out by Microsoft's rule, on mingw as well as on MSVC, and `layout_record` followed Itanium everywhere. A run of bit-fields goes into a unit the size and the alignment of the declared type, the unit closes when the next field's declared type has a different size or the bits do not fit, an ordinary member closes one too, and a closed unit costs its whole declared size whether or not the bits were used. So a struct of an `unsigned :3` and a `char` is eight bytes on Windows and four everywhere else. `TargetInfo::bit_field_style` carries the choice and it is keyed on the operating system, because a rule keyed on `Env::Msvc` gets `x86_64-windows-gnu` wrong by four bytes and gets it wrong quietly.

- Microsoft gives a union's bit-field its storage and no say in the alignment, so `union { unsigned m:3; char c; }` is four bytes aligned to one, which is an alignment smaller than either member has on its own.

- An unnamed bit-field raises the record's alignment under AAPCS64 and not on Apple's AArch64, on Windows on AArch64, on x86-64 or on RISC-V, and rucc said it never does. The rule is wider than the zero width case that found it: `struct { char c; unsigned :20; }` is four bytes aligned to four under AAPCS64 and four aligned to one on x86-64. `TargetInfo::unnamed_bit_field_aligns` carries it, measured across every row that has a reference, because it is neither an architecture rule nor an operating system rule but the ABI, and Apple and Microsoft each dropped it.

- A record with no storage in it is four bytes under MSVC and nothing anywhere else, carried as `TargetInfo::empty_record_size`. The 0.9.0 notes called this a question rather than an answer, on the grounds that the number had to come from MSVC rather than from clang's model of it. It cannot: MSVC rejects the declaration outright with C2016, so clang is the only C compiler for `x86_64-windows-msvc` and its four is the number to match, while mingw's incumbent GCC says zero. That makes it a fact about the environment rather than about the operating system, and it covers the record with no members, the one holding nothing but a zero width bit-field and the one holding nothing but a flexible array member.

- A trailing zero width bit-field's padding belongs to the record, so `struct { char c; int :0; }` is four bytes and rucc said one. The corpus did not find this one, because every shape in it with a zero width member had another member after it that ended further along than the padding did.

- `long double` on wasm32 is a sixteen byte IEEE quad aligned to sixteen and `rucc-abi` said it was a `double`. The rule grouped wasm32 in with the thirty two bit machines, on the assumption that a target with four byte pointers has nothing wider than a `double` in it, while `facts/wasm32-none.facts` in tamnd/rucc-cross had recorded `long_double_format=quad` and `sizeof_long_double=16` since the day those facts were taken. The fact was on disk and the rule contradicted it, which is what a generated corpus catches and a fact file on its own does not. It is also the second half of a mistake whose first half was already known, since `__int128` exists on that same target with those same four byte pointers. RISC-V 32 has the quad as well, measured by running `zig cc -target riscv32-linux-musl` rather than assumed from the family, and it is not a row of the target table so nothing else here would have found it.

- One corner is deliberately left unmodelled and is recorded in `spec/cross-compile/14-testing.md` section 14.9: how `packed` interacts with a Microsoft bit-field. mingw and MSVC disagree there, a packed struct of an `unsigned m0:3` and a `char m1` being eight bytes aligned to four under `x86_64-windows-gnu` and five aligned to one under `x86_64-windows-msvc`. rucc gives MSVC's answer on both rows. The corpus holds no packed records so nothing in it asserts either number.

- `licm` hoisted a division out of the test that made it safe. `speculate` asks document 10's ranges whether a divisor can be zero, and a range is narrowed by the branches that dominate the block it is asked about, so a divisor under `if (d)` is known non-zero inside the guard and unknown in front of the loop. The pass asked about the block the division was in and used the answer to move it to a block the guard does not reach, which is a program that divides by zero where the original did not. It now asks about the preheader, which is where the answer is used. Found by the `loop-hoist` facet added to tamnd/rucc-corpus alongside the pass, on the unsigned cases: the signed ones were saved by accident, because the overflow arm refuses what the divide by zero arm had already let through.

- `licm` moved a load out of a loop that wrote what the load was reading. The design said the IR threads memory through the instructions that touch it as an operand of type `mem`, so a load whose memory operand comes from outside the loop reads memory the loop did not write. An IR dump says otherwise: in the pipelines this pass runs in a load carries only its address and a call carries no memory operands at all, so the operand the reasoning rested on is never there and an unchanging address was read as an unchanging value. A loop that added up a global while calling a function that incremented it printed 24 where the answer is 52. Nothing that touches memory now leaves a loop that writes any unless the memory operand is really present, and the refusal is reported as its own missed remark rather than passing in silence. That is coarser than section 27.5 asks for, and the two ways out of it, the memory chain or the module so the pass can ask `alias`, are both #655. Found by the same `loop-hoist` facet, on all four types.

- `va_start`, `va_end` and `va_copy` say what any call says about an argument of the wrong type, so the dialect and `-fpermissive` decide whether that is an error. gcc has `va_arg` as an operator, since it takes a type name and no function can, and the other three as builtin functions declared to take the address of a list, which is why a `va_end` of an integer there is `passing argument 1 of '__builtin_va_end' makes pointer from integer without a cast` and is one of the rules gcc 14 promoted. rucc gave all four the one message it has for `va_arg` and made it an error under every dialect, which refused `test/varargs.c` of the chibicc suite that gcc 16 compiles. `va_arg` keeps that message and keeps the severity, because an operator's complaint about its operand is nobody's to soften.

- The obsolete array designator, `int a[8] = { [3] 7 };`, which is what GCC had before C99 settled on `[3] = 7`. It has been deprecated rather than removed for about thirty years, so code written against a compiler of that era still carries it, and rucc stopped on it with an error asking for the equals sign. The form is narrower than it looks and the width was measured against gcc 16 rather than guessed: it is taken only where the designation is one array designator and nothing else, so `[0 ... 2] 7` is taken and `.x 7` and `[0][1] 7` are both errors there as well. Like the member spelling `x: 7` that was already accepted, it says nothing unless `-pedantic` asks.

### Added

- `check_bounds` takes an optional third operand, which is how many bytes it is about. Section 7.4 of `spec/safe-memory/07-check-elimination.md` writes the hoisted check of a loop that runs `n` times as one check over `n * sizeof(T)` bytes, and that is a length the program computes rather than one the front end knew when it parsed the access. The two operand form is unchanged and is still what insertion emits, so the size and the alignment of an ordinary access stay in the memory payload where they were.

- The lowering hands that operand to the runtime in place of the constant it would otherwise have built, fitting it to the target's `size_t` first, and writes zero in the descriptor's size field, which is that field's own reading of a check that is not about an access of a known width. `discharge` leaves such a check alone in both directions, neither reading it as a fact nor asking whether it can go, because every range it compares is a pair of numbers and the payload of a hoisted check holds the size of one element rather than the size of the question.

- `licm`, section 27, which moves a computation the loop does not change out in front of the loop. Invariance is one walk over the blocks in dependency order rather than a fixpoint, because the IR is in SSA, so an operand defined outside the loop is an operand the loop does not change. Memory is the exception and it is handled coarsely: nothing that reads memory leaves a loop that writes any, because the pass is handed neither the memory chain nor the module it would need to say which memory. The pass runs at `-O1` and above, after the second `canon` that follows `header-copy`, since a rotated loop is entered from a block that also leaves it and only the second canonicalization turns that into a preheader worth hoisting into.

- `speculate`, section 27.1, the shared answer to whether an instruction may be worked out earlier than the program asks for it. It gives one of three answers rather than yes or no: move it anywhere, move it only where it was going to run anyway, or leave it where it is. A division whose divisor the ranges have not ruled zero out of is the second answer and not the third, which is what lets it come out of a loop that tests at the bottom. It is written as its own module because unswitching, if conversion and the scheduler all have to ask the same question and none of them should answer it themselves.

- Two thresholds in `rucc-cost` with their provenance, per section 40.12. `LICM_EXPENSIVE` is GCC's `lim-expensive`, twenty, which is the line between a computation worth a register held for the length of the loop and one that is cheaper to do again. `ASSUMED_ALLOCATABLE_REGS` is twelve, which is what the x86-64 allocator actually hands out once the stack pointer, the frame pointer and the two scratch registers are taken off. Fourteen was tried first and it hoisted values into loops that then spilled, which is the argument for counting what is handed out rather than what exists.

- Measured on the 1628 program corpus, the same build with `-fdisable-licm` against itself without it: 663 computations moved, on 185 programs, with 47 left alone because the loop writes memory and nothing in the pass says which memory, 20 because the register would cost more than the computation, 19 because the loop has no preheader, six because moving them would change what the program does, four because they could fault where they do not run on every entry, and three because the loop has no way out. Retired user instructions on those 185 programs fall from 384278 to 334904 once the fixed 111710 instruction start up cost is taken off, which is 12.8 percent fewer, with 162 programs faster, 19 slower and four unchanged. Text size at `-O2` rises 823 bytes across the corpus, 0.103 percent. Against gcc 16 every loop facet agrees at every level, and what the corpus still fails on is the builtins rucc has not implemented yet and one `setjmp` case, none of which this pass touches.

- `hoist`, the pass that takes a bounds check out of a counted loop and puts one check in front of it. This is `spec/safe-memory/07-check-elimination.md` section 7.4, which calls it the transformation that matters most and gives the reason in a line: array loops are where the checks are. `discharge` does nothing for them, because the address is different every time round and no two of the checks inside are about the same bytes. What is the same every time round is the range they all fall in, so one check of that range in the preheader says everything the checks inside were going to say.

- It is in `-O1`, `-O2` and `-O3`, between `simplify-cfg` and `discharge`, so it sees the shape `header-copy` and `canon` leave and so the check it puts in the preheader becomes a fact `discharge` can then use inside the loop. `-Os` and `-Oz` decline it, because it trades bytes in the preheader for time in the body and a level that will not pay for that has to be able to say so.

- Whether one check covers a whole loop is a rule and not a condition somebody wrote in a pass, the same split section 7.7 asks for and `discharge` is built on. The rule is `swept.i64` in `crates/rucc-opt/rules/safety.rules` and it is not the one `discharge` asks: that one is about a distance the compiler has as a number, and this one is about every distance from zero to the furthest at once, because the pass is removing one instruction that stood for as many accesses as the loop has iterations. It is written with the distance as a symbolic value, so a single solver query covers every iteration, and z3 agrees with it before the crate finishes building.

- The loop has to be one that runs a known number of times and reaches the check every one of them, and every way of not being one has its own remark. One latch, one edge out, the block that edge leaves from dominating the latch, no block inside that ends without a successor, no loop inside, and no call anywhere inside. The call condition is stronger than the one `discharge` puts on a call and is there for a different reason: `discharge` cares whether a call frees, and this pass cares whether it comes back, because a call that does not come back leaves the loop having run fewer times than its count says and the hoisted check covering bytes nothing read, which document 02 calls a release blocking bug.

- The one assumption the count is allowed to rest on is that signed overflow is undefined, and that is not a shortcut. Every loop whose test is signed comes back from `scev` carrying it, so a pass built on `Bound::proven` would never fire on any `for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)` in any C program. What the assumption says is that `-fwrapv` withdraws the count, and `-fwrapv` is implemented in `rucc-lower` by not setting `nsw`, so an increment that still carries the flag is one the front end already promised about. A counter with no such flag comes back with `NoWrap` instead and its loop keeps its check.

- The differential check accounting turns this pass off in build A alongside `discharge`, because a pass left off that list is a pass whose removals are in both builds and a comparison of two builds that both did the thing has nothing to say about whether the thing was right. With `hoist` in the comparison the 123 programs of `tests/safety` still come out with no divergence.

- `TargetInfo::for_tuple`, which describes any row of the target table rather than the fifteen a three field triple can spell. `TargetInfo` carries the ten field tuple and the whole of `rucc_abi::DataLayout` now, instead of a triple and three scalar widths, and `TargetInfo::new` takes a `Triple` as before and forwards to it, so nothing in the compiler proper changed shape. The widths on the type are views of that layout and the alignments are not, which is the reason it is kept whole: a `long long` is eight bytes on every row and is aligned to four on System V i386 and to eight everywhere else, and no width can say that.

- Scalar layout in `rucc-types` comes from that description rather than from the type's own size. `Layout::scalar` set the alignment equal to the size, which is the right answer on most of the table and wrong on the rows where the two come apart: System V i386 aligns a `long long`, a `double` and an `__int128` to four, and s390x caps every scalar alignment at eight so its sixteen byte `long double` sits on an eight byte boundary. `max_field_align` on the layout is what expresses the cap and it is applied to `__int128` and `_Float128`, the two other scalars wide enough to reach it. A `_BitInt` gets there by a different road: one of eight bytes or fewer is aligned like the standard type it is laid out as, and a wider one is aligned to the target's granule, which is four bytes on the thirty two bit rows and eight on s390x already.

- `tests/abi-corpus` has a file for every one of the forty two targets, where it had fifteen and printed the twenty seven it skipped. Thirty nine of them compile clean against the pinned Zig 0.16 and the other three are the rows zig has no spelling for, which `bin/compile-abi-corpus` in tamnd/rucc-cross names and counts rather than compiling against a neighbour. `__int128` is the one thing the files differ about declaring, since i686, both ARM rows and RISC-V 32 do not have the type: the two shapes that name it are absent there and the type is out of the seeded half of the grammar entirely, so every `gen_NN` declaration is identical in all forty two files and a diff between two of them stays a list of layout decisions.

- `rucc_abi::DataLayout::has_int128`, which is what the generator asks. It is a fact about the architecture and not about the pointer width, which is the trap: `x86_64-linux-gnux32` has four byte pointers and the type, `i686-linux-gnu` has four byte pointers and not the type, and both references provide it wherever the machine has sixty four bit registers to hold half of it in.

- `TargetInfo::va_list` and `TargetInfo::float64x_format` are `Option`, and `TargetInfo::regs`, `TargetInfo::call_regs` and `rucc_target::Machine::for_target` answer for the architectures that have a backend and say so for the rest. Widening the type to the whole table meant deciding what an AArch64 register file means on s390x, and the answer is nothing: a field that has no measured value on a row says `None` there rather than borrowing the neighbouring answer, which is the same rule `TargetInfo::call` already followed for AArch64 on Windows.

- `rucc_ir::Module` records the target as a tuple and prints it with `to_llvm_string`, so an IR file for a row with no backend round trips through the parser. `ObjectFormat` grew a `Wasm` variant for the same reason, since three rows of the table are wasm and a target's object format is not a question about whether an assembler for it exists here.

- Five shapes in the corpus generator for the cases that tell the two bit-field rules apart, `bits_mixed_bases`, `bits_after_member`, `bits_trailing_zero_width`, `union_of_bits` and `flexible_only`. Everything the corpus held before this either used one declared type throughout or had no bit-fields in it, and the two rules agree on all of that, which is how the first version managed to be wrong about Windows in only two places when it should have been wrong in a dozen.

## 0.9.0

### Added

- `cargo xtask abi-corpus`, the `_Static_assert` corpus of `spec/cross-compile/14-testing.md` section 14.2, generated into `tests/abi-corpus` and checked per commit. One C file per target, a few hundred records, and an assertion for every size, every alignment and every ordinary member's offset. Half the shapes are written by hand for the cases section 14.2 names, bit-fields of every width, the zero width member, the straddle, `_Alignas`, `long double`, `__int128` and the empty struct, and half are drawn from a fixed seed. The shapes are the same in every file, so a diff between two targets is the list of layout decisions the two make differently and nothing else.

- Every number in it comes from `rucc_types::layout_record`, which is the function the compiler calls when it parses a struct. A corpus generated from a second description of the rules would be a corpus that agrees with the reference and says nothing about the compiler.

- It covers fifteen of the forty two rows and prints the twenty seven it skipped on every run. Laying a record out needs a `TargetInfo`, which holds a three field triple, while `rucc-abi` describes the scalar layout of all forty two, so the compiler knows what a `long double` is on s390x and cannot be asked what a struct containing one looks like. The generator refuses to write a file for a row it cannot reach rather than borrowing a neighbour's numbers.

- Compiling those fifteen files with the pinned Zig 0.16 in tamnd/rucc-cross found three disagreements on the first run, recorded in section 14.9. A zero width bit-field contributes its type's alignment under AAPCS64 and not on Apple's AArch64, on Windows on AArch64, on x86-64 or on RISC-V. Windows lays bit-fields out by Microsoft's rule on mingw as well as on MSVC, so a struct of an `unsigned :3` and a `char` is eight bytes there and four everywhere else. rucc is wrong about both and right about everything else the corpus asserts. The third is that clang's Microsoft record layout gives an empty struct a size of four where mingw gives it zero, and that one is a question rather than an answer, because an empty struct is not C and the number to match has to come from MSVC rather than from clang's model of it. The fix is the next change rather than this one, because the corpus has to exist first for the fix's diff to be readable.

- `Triple::from_tuple`, the inverse of `Triple::tuple`, computed by running that function over all forty eight triples rather than by writing the narrowing out a second time. It answers `None` for the twenty seven rows this type cannot spell, which is the honest answer and the one the corpus generator needs.

- `cargo xtask accounting`, the differential check accounting of `spec/safe-memory/14-verification.md` section 14.3, running nightly. Every case in `tests/safety` is built twice at `-O2`, once with `-fdisable-discharge` and once without, and the two runs have to say the same thing. A report the build with all the checks makes that the normal build does not is a check the elimination removed that had something to say, and it is printed as such.

- It is the one check that catches the walk being wrong rather than the rule being wrong. The rules in `safety.rules` have a solver agreeing with them before the crate finishes building, so the arithmetic is not where the bug will be. Where it will be is in which facts the walk was holding when it asked, and nothing about a rule table has anything to say about that.

- What is compared is whether a report happened and which judgement it named. Section 14.3 keys reports by class, source location and dynamic occurrence, and the other two are not available yet: nothing fills the `pc` field of a report in, and the runs are made with abort semantics as the section asks, so there is at most one report per program. The judgement is what is left and it is enough for the failure this exists for.

- The first run over the suite compares 123 programs and finds no divergence, which is the baseline the job is there to defend rather than a result in itself.

### Changed

- `spec/cross-compile/08-sysroots.md` grows a fourth validation check and the result of running it: a sysroot built twice on two hosts, compared. That is the exit criterion of the sysroot half of #619, and it found the host path that leaks into a cross build even when nobody asks for debug information. clang writes the working directory into `DW_AT_comp_dir` of every object it emits, `.s` files included, so the 219 headers of a musl sysroot matched between a mac and a Linux box on the first try and the six compiled artifacts did not. With `-fdebug-compilation-dir` and `-ffile-prefix-map` all four musl targets reproduce byte for byte, `libc.a` included, and `spec/cross-compile/02-the-goal.md` claim 5 records that its input half now holds. The producer is `bin/sysroot` in tamnd/rucc-cross, which is where fetching lives because a crate the compiler links against should not have a network policy.

## 0.8.2

### Added

- `header-copy`, section 26.6, which is the fifth loop property and the one `canon` deliberately left alone. A `while` loop asks its question before the body, so there is a branch in front of the loop and a branch at the end of it and the body is split across two regions. Copying the header in front of the loop turns that into a `do-while`: the copy asks once on the way in, the original asks again at the bottom, and what the later loop passes get is one region with one test in it.

- The copy is made at `-O1` and above with GCC's limit of twenty instructions, and at `-Os` under the name `header-copy-small` with a limit of five, both in `rucc-cost` with their provenance as section 40.12 asks. `-Oz` does not run it, because header copying is the one loop shape change that writes code out twice and a level that will not pay for that has to be able to say so.

- The entry test the copy creates goes to the same range oracle `prune` uses rather than a second one written for the occasion, so a loop the ranges prove runs loses its guard entirely and a loop the ranges prove never runs is deleted. That is why the pass is not simply a size cost: across the corpus at -O2, 567 of the 635 copies it makes have their entry test removed outright and 68 keep it, so nine times in ten the copy is not a copy of anything the program still executes.

- It declines rather than guesses, and each refusal has its own remark. A header whose value something outside the loop reads, a header that may write to memory or that holds anything document 17.1 does not call pure, a loop with no preheader, a header over the level's budget, and a loop that already tests at the bottom are all left as they are. Across the corpus the only refusals that fire are 49 for an escaping value and 2 for an effect. Nothing is refused for size and nothing for a missing preheader, which says the twenty instruction limit is not what binds here and that `canon` running first does what it is there for.

- Counting the loops the pass looks at across all 1461 programs, 635 are copied and 19 already tested at the bottom against 51 declined, so 93 per cent of loops are in `do-while` form afterwards. Section 26.9 asks for exactly that measurement and says the answer should be nearly all of them, and that if it is not then the size limit is too tight. It is not the size limit.

- What it costs is 12514 bytes of `.text` at -O2 over the corpus, 1114300 to 1126814, which is 1.12 per cent overall and about twenty bytes for each loop copied. 480 programs grow, 33 shrink, and the largest single win is 175 bytes on a scheduling case where the bottom test let the scheduler fill a region it could not reach before. Run time did not move in a way the machine could report: the four levels came out between 1.6 per cent faster and 0.7 per cent slower with no consistent direction, on a shared box under load, so the honest reading is no measurable change rather than a win. The corpus runner's compile throughput gate reads two per cent more. Correctness is unchanged case for case: the same 150 results come out unexpected on both compilers, zero of them a wrong answer on either.

- `discharge`, the pass that takes out a bounds check whose bytes an earlier bounds check already covered. It walks the dominator tree carrying the ranges checks have been passed on, normalizes each pointer to the value it was computed from and a constant offset, and where a fact shares that value it works out how far apart the two accesses are. It is in every level except `-O0`, sitting between `simplify-cfg` and `dce` so that it sees the merged blocks and so that the `cap_of` it strands is cleaned up after it. This is `spec/safe-memory/07-check-elimination.md` section 7.3 and the first box of milestone S4.

- The `safety/` rule namespace, which is what section 7.7 asks for and is the reason this pass is not simply a condition somebody wrote in Rust. Whether a check may go is a rule in `crates/rucc-opt/rules/safety.rules`, a solver has to agree with it before the crate finishes building, and the pass asks the table rather than deciding for itself. The split is between a walk, which is ordinary code that gets caught by the differential check accounting when it is wrong, and a removal condition, which is arithmetic that is off at the ends of the type, gives the right answer on every test, and lets one access through in the case nobody wrote down.

- `discharge` as a third kind of rule in the rule language, alongside `simplify` and `lower`. Its pattern is not an instruction and its table is never matched against the IR, which is why it is a kind of its own rather than a rewrite that would be wrong to put in the simplifier, and `+` and `-` are now allowed in a guard so that a rule can be about a distance and a size together.

- `nofree` summaries, which is the interprocedural half of milestone S4 and the field `spec/safe-memory/07-check-elimination.md` section 7.5 says unlocks temporal elimination. A function this module defines cannot free when every call in its body goes somewhere that cannot free and it ends no lifetime itself, and a function it does not define cannot free only when a short table of library names vouches for it. The walk starts with every definition in the set and takes one out when something it calls is not in it, so a pair of functions that call each other and free nothing are both in the answer.

- The answer is written into the IR as a `nofree` flag on the call site rather than kept in a table, because the fact belongs to the callee and a pass is given one function. `discharge` reads it off the instruction in front of it and keeps its facts across a call that carries it, which is the first pass to spend a summary on anything. Nothing else about a call is believed: an unmarked call, a call through an address and inline assembly all still throw the facts away.

- A `weak` or `common` definition is not believed, because the linker may take a different one. An ordinary exported definition is, and that is a stated assumption rather than a proof, since a shared library symbol can be interposed at run time. It is the assumption `-fno-semantic-interposition` makes and `-fvisibility=hidden` makes it true.

- `discharge` takes out lifetime checks as well, which is what the `nofree` summaries were written for and what section 8.8 of `spec/safe-memory/08-temporal-safety.md` calls the difference between temporal checks costing 5 per cent and costing 40 per cent. A `check_live` that passes says only that the instance holding its own address is alive, which on its own would discharge nothing, because `rucc-safety` emits a lifetime check per field rather than per object. What makes it worth something is the bounds check beside it: a range that passed a bounds check is inside one instance, so the instance found alive is the instance the whole range is in, and a later lifetime check anywhere in that range is asking a question that has been answered.

- Across seven files of the real corpus at `-O2 -fsafety=detect` this takes out every lifetime check the bounds half was already taking out and a few more, 279 of 830 on cJSON.c, 2305 of 2775 on blake2b-ref.c and 73 of 409 on llama2.c's run.c, where before this it was none of them anywhere.

- A `meta_end` and a `meta_transfer` drop every fact the pass holds, the same way a call that cannot be vouched for does. Nothing emits either one today, so this costs nothing now and is the difference between conservative and wrong on the day the instrumentation starts ending lifetimes.

### Changed

- The pass gives up every fact it holds at a call it has not been told about, which is stricter than section 7.3, and the module comment says why. The case is a `free` and then a smaller allocation at the same address, which the lifetime judgement is supposed to refuse and today does not, and a discharge rate is worth less than a hole. What the strictness costs is counted rather than argued about: a check that a fact would have covered if a call had not intervened is reported under `-fopt-info-missed`, which is what the `nofree` summaries below took most of.

- `rucc-target` reads the sizes, the alignments and the signedness of the standard types from `rucc-abi` instead of working them out itself. They were a match on the architecture and the operating system written out in `TargetInfo::new`, covering three architectures, and `rucc_abi::DataLayout` answers the same questions over the ten field tuple for fourteen of them, against a reference compiler. The two answers disagreed and the one that was written out here was wrong, which is the entry below.

- The four hand written classifiers in `rucc-target` are gone and `TargetInfo::call` hands the question to `rucc-abi`. They were about a thousand lines of SysV AMD64, AAPCS64, Windows x64 and RISC-V LP64D written as code, and `rucc-abi` has the same four plus Darwin arm64 written as data, with the mechanism they share factored out and the tests kept. Two implementations of one psABI is the same failure the two format enums were: they agree on every case anyone wrote a test for and differ on the one nobody did, and the difference is a structure arriving with its members in the wrong registers rather than a build error.

- `TargetInfo::call` returns an `Option` because one target has no answer. AArch64 on Windows is AAPCS64 with a different variadic rule and x18 reserved, it is not described yet, and this used to answer plain AAPCS64 for it. That is the almost right answer, which is the failure `spec/cross-compile/02-the-goal.md` is written to rule out: everything builds, everything links, and the calls are wrong at a library boundary where nothing is looking. It is now a diagnostic on the call, through the error channel `rucc_lower::abi::plan` already had. Nothing else changes, since the other forty four triples the compiler can name all have their ABI written down.

- The first reading of open question Q1 in `spec/cross-compile/16-open-questions.md` goes in with it, because that question schedules a measurement at exactly this commit. On a translation unit that is three thousand functions of aggregate arguments and aggregate returns and nothing else, the described classifiers cost 1.8 per cent of CPU time against the hand written ones, median of eight interleaved runs with the interquartile ranges published. That is a ceiling rather than a reading of real code and it is under the two per cent limit at the ceiling. The corpus figure is still owed and belongs on the corpus runner, since 1461 programs of a few dozen lines each spend more time in `fork` and `exec` than in the compiler.

- `wchar_t`'s width and its signedness are `rucc-abi`'s answers now, which is where the rest of the layout facts already were. Its signedness does not follow a plain `char`'s and no rule over one of them gets the other right: Windows signs `char` and not `wchar_t`, AArch64 FreeBSD makes both unsigned, and AArch64 NetBSD makes `char` unsigned and `wchar_t` signed. All three pairs are recorded in the facts in tamnd/rucc-cross and all three are asserted.

- The layer ranks moved up by one from `rucc-target` and above, because `rucc-abi` is below it now rather than beside it. `rucc-tuple`, `rucc-abi` and `rucc-sysroot` are ranks 0, 1 and 1, `rucc-target` is 2, and the binary is 14. `spec/18-package-layout.md`'s tree is the picture of that and it had drifted, so it lists all twenty seven library crates now rather than the twenty three it had when it was written.

- There is one floating point format enum now rather than two. `rucc-abi` described a target's `long double` with its own copy of `rucc-base`'s, and the two had drifted by one variant already: the compiler's had six and the description's had seven, the extra one being the IBM double-double that 64-bit PowerPC uses. A format is one fact and it has to be the same fact in a data layout, in a psABI rule and in a constant the front end folded, because those three meet, and two copies of it fail by producing a wrong number rather than by failing to build.

- So `rucc-base` gained the double-double, and with it an admission. It is not an IEEE encoding: a pair of doubles has no exponent field, no significand field and no single precision, and the two halves can sit two thousand bits apart, so the number of significand bits between them is a hundred and six for some values and far more for others. `Format::is_ieee` is the new predicate, the questions that assume an IEEE encoding fail on it rather than answering with the nominal figures, and every way of making a `Float` refuses it. Refusing at the constructor rather than at the arithmetic is deliberate, because the nominal figures are close enough to right to be believed and wrong often enough to matter, and a value in a format nothing can encode is worth catching where it is asked for. Representing one is what a PowerPC backend will need, and there is no PowerPC backend.

### Fixed

- `long double` on `x86_64-apple-darwin` is the eighty bit x87 format in sixteen bytes, which is what the system's own headers say. It was a sixty four bit `double`, because the rule that decided the width read the operating system and Apple made that change on AArch64 only. `sizeof(long double)` came out at eight where every macOS header says sixteen, so `%Lf` read the wrong bytes, `LDBL_MAX` was `DBL_MAX`, and any structure with a `long double` in it laid out differently from the platform's. Nothing caught it because both Apple targets were written on one line and the AArch64 one is the one people test on.

- `__LDBL_NORM_MAX__` and the rest of the `NORM_MAX` family were emitted as a copy of `MAX`, which is true for every IEEE format and false for the double-double. The two are the same number only where every value of a format has a full significand, and a double-double's largest values do not: the high half can be `DBL_MAX` and the low half then adds to it, so `LDBL_MAX` on PowerPC is above `DBL_MAX` while `LDBL_NORM_MAX` is about half of it. Adding the seventh format is what surfaced the assumption, and the two are separate fields now. No target here chooses a double-double `long double` yet, so nothing was miscompiled by it.

## 0.8.1

### Added

- The cross compilation design, seventeen documents in `spec/cross-compile/`, and the first two crates it calls for. The goal it states is `zig cc`'s: every target, from every host, out of one binary, with no external toolchain and no sysroot flag. It was written in a separate repository and has moved here, because the crates it produces are compiler crates and having them anywhere else means the compiler depends on another repository's release cadence to know what a target is.

- `rucc-tuple` replaces the three field triple with a ten field tuple: the architecture, the baseline within it, the byte order, the data model, the operating system, the OS version, the environment, the environment version, the float ABI and the object format. The membership rule is that a fact is in the tuple if it changes how a function is called or how a struct is laid out, and it is a flag otherwise, which is what makes a tuple usable as a cache key. Three things the old model could not say are now sayable: a 64-bit Windows target has 64-bit pointers and a 32-bit `long`, s390x is big-endian, and `x86_64-linux-gnux32` has 32-bit pointers on a 64-bit machine. The parser also stopped discarding what it did not recognise, so `x86_64-linux-gnu.2.28` keeps its glibc version instead of silently losing it.

- The target table is data, forty two rows, each with the tier it is at today, the tier the plan commits to, whether rucc runs there, and why the row is interesting. `docs/TARGETS.md` is generated from it by `cargo xtask targets` and CI checks that it is up to date, because a hand maintained copy of a support table is a promise nobody made.

- `rucc-abi` makes a psABI a description rather than a function. Register banks, how a scalar spends them, and two ordered lists of rules saying what an aggregate has to look like and how it travels if it does, read by one classifier. Five ABIs are described, which is the four `rucc-target` implements by hand plus Darwin arm64, at about two hundred lines of data against a thousand lines of code. The split is mechanism against policy: the SysV eightbyte merge, the homogeneous aggregate scan, the RISC-V floating point pair and the exact size check stay as code, and there are four of them across the five ABIs. `rucc-target`'s own thirty test cases pass against the descriptions, and there is a test that adds a sixth ABI entirely as data.

- `rucc-abi` also carries the type layouts, which is the half of a psABI that decides whether a target's own headers are read correctly rather than whether a call works. A `long double` carries its width and its format separately, because sixteen bytes on x86-64 Linux, sixteen bytes on AArch64 Linux and sixteen bytes on 64-bit PowerPC are three different formats.

- `rucc-sysroot` answers where a target's headers and link inputs are, which directories are searched for them and in what order, and what order the link inputs reach the linker in. The header search rule of `spec/cross-compile/08-sysroots.md` section 8.5 exists to stop a cross build picking up a header from the machine it is running on, which is a failure that works on that machine and nowhere else, so the whole crate is written to make it checkable: every answer is a function of the tuple and of paths the caller passed in, nothing reads an environment variable, nothing touches the filesystem, and a host directory can only enter through one option that is used only when the target is the host. There is a test that walks all forty two targets and asserts no host directory reaches any of them, and another that asks the same question from two different hosts and compares the answers path for path.

- A sysroot's directory is the cache directory plus the canonical spelling of the tuple and nothing else, which is what the ten field model was for. `x86_64-linux-gnu` and `x86_64-linux-gnu.2.28` are different sysroots because they are different headers, and `x86_64-linux-gnux32` gets its own header directory because every type in them that carries a pointer or a `long` is a different width. The headers are split into a per architecture tree and a generic one, searched in that order, because the part of a libc that differs by architecture is a few dozen files against a few hundred shared ones and one tree per architecture is a distribution size nobody wants to pay.

- The musl link line, as three lists rather than one. `crti.o` and `crtn.o` open and close the `.init` and `.fini` sections, so anything contributing to those has to land between them, and a link line that is a single list gets this wrong in a way that produces a binary which links, runs, and does not run its static constructors. The three link modes differ in the first start file rather than only in a flag, because a static-pie binary relocates itself before `main` and `rcrt1.o` is what does that.

- A sysroot carries a manifest naming every input with its source, its hash and its licence. It is sorted, so the order a directory walk happened to return files in does not reach the file, which is the same reproducibility claim one level down. Two of the seven libc sources are legal walls rather than engineering, the macOS SDK and the Windows SDK, and the manifest is where that is a function something calls rather than a rule in a document.

- `rucc-targets` is a build tool that answers what the tuple, the ABI and the sysroot say for any target, and generates `docs/TARGETS.md`. It is a separate binary from `xtask` because `xtask` has no dependencies on purpose. The `sysroot` command prints the cache directory as a placeholder rather than as wherever the run happened to look, so that two people on two machines see the same text and can compare it.

- `canon`, the pass that puts every loop into the shape the loop passes are allowed to assume. Document 07.3 named four properties and section 26.7 has them established before anything else at loop level: a preheader, a single latch, exits that belong to the loop that leaves through them, and loop closed form, which is a value used after a loop being handed over at the exit rather than named where it was defined. It does not do section 26.6's header copying, which is the fifth property, the only one that changes what the program computes rather than where its blocks are, and its own pass for that reason.

- The reason to establish them once rather than let each pass cope is section 26.1's and it is not a close call. Every loop transformation needs somewhere to put code, and without a preheader each of them makes one, each does it a little differently, and each carries the case where the header has several predecessors from outside. With one, each of them writes `insert at the end of the preheader` and stops thinking about it. Loop closed form earns its keep the same way: unroll a loop whose result is read afterwards and without it the outside use names a value the body defines, so after unrolling there are four copies and the use has to be pointed at the right one, while with it there is one argument on one edge to get right.

- The order the four are done in is the whole design. Making a preheader changes which blocks reach a header, which changes what counts as a latch. Splitting a latch adds a block that may need to be a dedicated exit. Loop closed form goes last because it is the only one of the four that reads the final shape of the graph rather than changing it. Section 26.8 says one pass is enough in that order, and that is asserted rather than assumed: a second run over the same function changes nothing, which is what `a_second_run_changes_nothing` checks.

- The forest is rebuilt between steps rather than patched across them. Every split adds a block that belongs to some loop, so the forest the pass started with is wrong the moment it makes the first one. GCC patches as it goes, which is faster and is where its loop bugs live, and section 26.8 has rucc rebuilding instead per document 06.5's rule that a stale analysis is worse than an absent one. That also makes section 26.2's correctness requirement free rather than careful: the irreducible marking has to survive canonicalization or the loop passes stop declining those regions, and a forest worked out again from a graph with the same cycles in it cannot lose the marking.

- On its own it generates almost nothing, which is the point. The blocks it adds are empty and the parameters it adds have one argument each, and `simplify-cfg` runs straight after it and takes both back out to a fixed point, so the cost of loop closed form is paid back by a pass that exists anyway. Across the corpus at -O2 over 1453 programs `.text` goes from 1114294 bytes to 1114300, which is six bytes and no change to the ratio against gcc 16 at 1.2576, with the same 57 cases failing on both sides. Compile time over the same 1453 programs is 151654 ms against main's 148937 ms, which is 1.8 per cent, and the corpus runner's own compile throughput gate is met. The machine the corpus runs on had other work on it at the time, so that figure is a bound rather than a tight measurement, and the two toolchains were run interleaved so they carried the same load.

- Those six bytes are worth naming, because they are exactly the cost section 26.5 says the property has. Both programs that grow are loops with two exits into one block, so one exit gets split off, and the extra block costs the register allocator a spill it did not need before. Section 26.4 warns about the same thing from the other side: a loop with several exits gets a parameter at each of them for the same value. Six bytes in 1114294 for a property four later passes get to assume is the trade section 26.1 describes, made and measured rather than argued about.

- A census over all 1461 programs finds 551 values handed over at an exit, 19 loops whose back edges were routed through one latch, and 8 exits split off, across 486 programs. Preheaders do not appear at all, and that is a real finding rather than a gap: by the time the loop pipeline opens, every loop the front end produces already has a block outside it whose only successor is the header, so the step is there for the loops that later passes will make rather than for the ones that arrive.

- `-fpermissive`, and `-fno-permissive` to turn it back off. It takes the six rules gcc 14 promoted from warnings into errors and makes them warnings again, which is what a build of code written before 2024 reaches for when the code cannot be changed. That is tamnd/rucc#627.

- The dialect now decides those same six. Four of them are rules C89 did not have, so under `-std=c89` and `-std=gnu89` a declaration with no type in it, a call to a function nothing declared, a parameter in an old style definition with no type, and a bare `return` from a function that promised a value are the language rather than mistakes, and nothing is said about them. The other two were constraint violations in C89 as well and are warnings there, which is what gcc has done with them since long before it started refusing them. Measured against gcc 16.2.0, one file per rule, no `-W` flags on either command line.

- A call to a name nothing declared now declares `extern int f();` for the file wherever the dialect or `-fpermissive` allows it, which is C89 6.3.2.2. The call gives back an `int` and its arguments are promoted rather than converted, since there is no prototype to convert them to, and a second call to the same name finds that declaration rather than making another. Where it is refused the wording is now gcc's, `implicit declaration of function 'f'`, rather than the one for a name that is not a call.

- Both acceptance cases that carried a gap on tamnd/rucc#84 pass now, so the known gap count in that suite is zero rather than four.

- A baseline for SQLite, the first corpus member, in five columns on real Linux hardware. `veryquick.test` runs to completion with no failures against a gcc build of the same source as a control, against rucc at `-O0` and at `-O2`, and against rucc at both levels with `-fsafety=detect`. The two instrumented columns leave five refusals and they are the same five at both optimization levels, so nothing in the list is the optimizer's. The reason for running the uninstrumented and unoptimized columns is that a suite which only passes with the gates on and the optimizer at one setting is not a baseline, it is a coincidence, and `-O0` without safety is the column that says the code generator is right on its own.

- Getting to no failures needed one change to the test suite and it is not rucc's bug. `zipfile-25.0` failed in every column including the gcc control, because `test/symlink.test` creates directories named `w`, `x`, `y` and `z` and never removes them, and much later `zipfile.test` selects from `zipfile('x')` and finds the directory. On glibc `fopen("x","rb")` on a directory succeeds and the `fread` after it fails, so the error is `error in fread()` rather than `cannot open file: x`. Deleting the four directories before `finish_test` in `symlink.test` fixes it. Worth reporting upstream, and worth recording here because the first instinct on a failing corpus member is to look at the compiler.

- All five refusals are classified. One is `capi3c-3.6.1-misuse` using a database handle the test closed on purpose, which is the test doing its job. The other four are S5 on a pointer that is never read through: two loop guards in `ext/misc/zipfile.c` that compute `p + k` in order to decide whether `p + k` is in range, one null `zTail` in SQLite's own test harness at `src/test1.c:5205`, and `fillInCell` advancing its source pointer after the payload has become a `zeroblob` and the copy has become a `memset`. Every one is undefined by 6.5.6 and none of them touches a byte outside any object. `spec/safe-memory/03-bug-model.md` has a row for the shape, `spec/safe-memory/18-sqlite-idioms.md` records it as the second idiom that running found and reading did not, and whether S5 should keep reporting at the derivation at all is now question 12 in `spec/safe-memory/17-open-questions.md`.

- What the `fillInCell` refusal cost to explain is written into question 8. The buffer the pointer walks off is a lookaside slot of about a kilobyte, and the report did not fire until the pointer had left the whole forty eight kilobyte lookaside block thousands of bytes later, so it named an object the program does not believe it has. An overflow of one lookaside slot into its neighbour is not merely undetected under Tier D, it is undetectable, which is the sub-allocator hole seen from the outside rather than argued about.

### Fixed

- An old style parameter narrower than an `int` is converted from what the call promoted it to. There is no prototype for a call to convert its argument to, so the argument is promoted and an `int` arrives for a parameter the body reads as an `unsigned char`. rucc had the signature right and stored the promoted value into the narrow parameter unconverted, so `f(-1)` against `f (c) unsigned char c;` read -1 rather than 255. That is tamnd/rucc#636 and `execute/950605-1.c` in the torture suite is the program that checks it. A floating parameter had it worse: `float` promotes to `double`, and a body reading it as a `float` built a multiply with one operand of each width, which the verifier refused as an internal error. A parameter a prototype names arrives as itself and is untouched by this.

- The safety runtime answers `malloc_usable_size`. It interposed `malloc`, `free`, `calloc` and `realloc` and not this one, so the call resolved to the C library's, which read a chunk header this arena never wrote and returned whatever the eight bytes in front of the payload happened to be. SQLite's page cache is one of the callers and shows what that costs: it divides the answer by a slot size and carves that many page slots out of the one allocation, so an answer too large by any amount is a heap overflow with nothing in the program to blame for it. The answer now is the instance's class extent, which is more than was asked for and is storage the plane agrees the instance owns, so a caller that uses all of it is let through. Zero for null, for an instance that is over, and for a pointer this arena never handed out, because a size is a question rather than an access and a program that goes on to use the bytes gets refused where the judgement belongs.

- A function called above the line that defines it is emitted again, where the call is the one that declares the name. The implicit declaration was kept off the top level of the tree on the grounds that the source does not contain it, and a definition further down merges into the declaration already made rather than making a second one, so the body was attached to a node nothing walks and no function came out. Nothing about the call looked wrong and the program reached the linker before anyone found out, as an undefined reference to a name defined eleven lines below the call. `execute/cmpsi-1.c` in the torture suite is that file, and fourteen more of them stopped at the same place.

- `return;` from a function that promised a value builds valid IR. It only reaches the IR builder under C89, where the checking now lets it through, and it was building a return with nothing in it out of a function whose signature returns something, which the verifier refused as an internal error.
