Zero goes back instead. `unreachable` would have been the other answer and is the wrong one: it says the branch that reached the statement never runs, which is a claim about the program rather than about the value, and `execute/920728-1.c` in the torture suite is a program where believing it would delete the path that was taken.

- GNU's `a ?: b` evaluates `a` once again. That is tamnd/rucc#613, and it was a wrong answer rather than a slow one: `++i ?: 10` incremented twice and `f() ?: 10` called twice, so a program whose left side does something got a different result here than under gcc.

- The checking keeps one node for `a` and converts it in two directions, to the bit the branch is taken on and to the type the whole expression has, which is how the tree says the two are the same evaluation. The IR builder was walking into the arm and reaching that node a second time, so it built a second copy of whatever the node says. It now takes the value once before the branch, remembers what the node is worth while the arms are lowered, and hands that value back where the arm reaches it, so the arm converts what is in hand rather than computing it again. A written out `a ? a : b` is untouched, because the second `a` there is a node of its own and is a second read, which is what C says it is.

## 0.8.0

### Added

- SQLite builds and runs its test suite at Tier D. `veryquick.test` under `-fsafety=detect` runs three hundred and thirty two thousand tests with one failure, and that failure is `zipfile-25.0`, which fails under a gcc built testfixture as well. It was run at `-O0` and at `-O2`, each with and without the monitor, and all four columns give the same answer, so the instrumentation changes no test outcome at either optimisation level. That is the exit criterion of milestone S3, which is tamnd/rucc#429.

- The run leaves five refusals. One is J1 against a test that calls the API on a handle it closed on purpose. Three are J2 against code that computes an address in order to decide whether that address is allowed and never reads through it, which is undefined behaviour by the letter of the standard and is what a good deal of careful C looks like. The fifth is in `fillInCell` and is not explained yet, and it may be the monitor being wrong about where an object ends rather than the program being wrong; it is tamnd/rucc#623. The classification is on tamnd/rucc#605.

- The three postures of `spec/safe-memory/06-instrumentation.md` section 6.5, which say what the safety runtime does after it has reported a violation. `abort` says what happened and stops, which is what it has always done and is still the default. `continue` says what happened, performs the access as written, carries on, and says nothing further about that same check site, so that one bug in a loop does not hide the hundred behind it. `log` is the same without the quieting, for somebody counting occurrences rather than finding distinct bugs.

- The posture is read from the `RUCC_SAFETY_ON_ERROR` environment variable rather than from the `-fsafety-on-error=` flag the document names. The runtime is a static archive, so a compiler flag would have to travel from the object into the archive, and the two ways to do that are a weak reference Rust has no stable spelling for and a `.init_array` constructor the IR cannot express yet. The variable is the half that unblocks what the posture exists for, which is running a test suite to the end rather than to its first report, and when the flag arrives it will set the default and the variable will stay as the override.

- A name the runtime does not recognise reads as `abort`, because refusing to start over a typo in a variable would turn a mistake in how a program was run into a report about the monitor rather than about the program. The check sites that have already spoken are remembered in a fixed table of 256 entries, since there is no allocator here that a program under judgement can be trusted with, and a run with more distinct sites than that starts repeating reports rather than dropping them.

- The judgements the allocator decides for itself, which are J4, J5 and J6, still stop whatever the posture says. The postures that carry on are defined by the access going ahead as written and there is no access to let through in a bad `free`: the program asked the runtime to do something to its own bookkeeping and the answer is no, so carrying on would mean either doing it anyway and corrupting what every later judgement is read out of, or returning a result the caller was not told is a refusal.

- A refused derivation now says which pointer the result was computed from, the range of the object that pointer was in, and how far past the end or short of the start the result landed. J2's faulting address is by construction outside every object, so the line that follows every other report, the one saying what the plane knows about the address, can only say that nobody owns it. Turning the SQLite refusal into a sentence took a debugger and a reading of the allocator, and that is the work these two lines do. The extent is read out of the lifetime plane rather than out of the instance header, because the plane is what the judgements are decided against and a report that quoted the requested size would disagree with a check that permits the rounded one.

### Fixed

- The safety runtime's arena reuses every block it hands out, where it used to reuse nothing above a megabyte. There were seventeen size classes and anything larger was served from the bump pointer and never put on a free list, so a program that allocated and freed a few large buffers over a long run consumed the region monotonically however carefully it freed, and got a null back. That is what stopped SQLite's test suite at Tier D in `corruptB`, with the process at 334 MB on a workload that should have been flat. There are enough classes for every size a `usize` can name now, which is a couple of kilobytes of arena state against a bound that had to be guessed and was guessed wrong.

- The safety runtime's heap takes another region when the one it has is full, where it used to hand out a null. A region is a gibibyte of reserved address space and the heap was only ever built to hold one, which put a hard ceiling on the live set of any program the monitor watched, and the ceiling was reported as an out of memory that named our arena rather than anything about the program. There is a table of eight regions and every reader already walked it, so the change is that the allocator fills the next slot instead of giving up, and every existing arena is asked for a block before a new region is reserved because a free list is better than growth for the same reason it is everywhere else. That is what stopped SQLite's test suite at Tier D in `memdb`, whose in-memory VFS holds a whole database in one buffer and grows it by reallocating, which no arrangement of size classes gets around.

- A single allocation larger than a whole region gets a region of its own rather than a null. What makes a region a gibibyte is that a gibibyte is a reasonable amount of address space to reserve for a heap nobody has measured, not anything the arithmetic depends on, so a request past it is a reason to reserve more rather than a reason to refuse. SQLite's `spellfix4-410` asks for four hundred megabytes in one call and a block is a little over three times what was asked for, so under a fixed region that test reported an out of memory that the program had not caused. The only size still refused on sight is one past a quarter of the address space, which has no block at all, and it is refused before anything is mapped.

- Payloads are no longer rounded up to a power of two. The classes are granule counts, exact up to four granules and four to a power of two above that, so 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 10, 12, 14, 16, 20 and onwards. That caps the rounding at a quarter where it used to be a half, and a 208 byte request that took 256 bytes now takes 224. There is a test that walks every granule count up to a megabyte and holds the waste against that quarter, and another that says the rounding is idempotent, which the free path depends on: it reads a size out of a header and refuses the pointer unless the rounding would have produced it, so a class size that rounded onwards would refuse every free.

### Changed

- J2, the judgement on pointer arithmetic, now permits a result one element below the start of its object as well as one past the end. `spec/safe-memory/03-bug-model.md` section 3.1 has the argument and the cost; the short version is that `&a[-1]` as a loop's starting point is how a great deal of working C is written, SQLite's bytecode interpreter is written that way, and refusing it stopped the first full run of SQLite's test suite at Tier D on code that has no bug in it. Two elements below the start is still refused, and so is reading through the one that is now permitted, because J1 was not widened and an access at `lo - stride` is still outside the object.

- The width of one element travels to the runtime as a fourth operand on `check_deriv` rather than sitting in the descriptor, because a walk over a variable length array steps by a width the program computes and a descriptor is constant data. The safety pass runs before the optimizer, so it recovers the width from the shape the frontend leaves behind, which is a multiply by a constant under an optional negation. An offset it does not recognise gets a width of one byte, which is the narrowest window and so the strictest answer rather than the most permissive one.

## 0.7.8

### Added

- `-fgnu89-inline` and `-fno-gnu89-inline`, which say whether the whole unit is under GNU's reading of `inline` or C's. C99 swapped which of `inline` and `extern inline` leaves a definition behind for another unit to call, and this is how a program written before that swap says so under a later dialect. That is tamnd/rucc#588, and it was refused as an unknown option until now.

- Both readings were already here, since `__attribute__((__gnu_inline__))` asks for GNU's one name at a time and the C89 dialects are under it throughout. What was missing was the flag that asks for it over a whole unit. The C89 dialects stay under GNU's reading whether the flag was written or not, so `-fno-gnu89-inline` there changes nothing. gcc refuses that command line rather than ignoring it, and accepting it is the more permissive of the two answers on a line that could only have meant one thing.

- The flag also decides which of `__GNUC_GNU_INLINE__` and `__GNUC_STDC_INLINE__` is defined, because that is how a header asks which reading it is under and glibc writes its `__extern_inline` macro two different ways depending on the answer. Exactly one of the two is defined at a time and there is a test that says so, since a header that sees both or neither takes a path nobody wrote.

- Twelve of the forty three programs in `gcc.c-torture/execute` that carry a `dg-options` line ask for this flag, and seven of them were failing here for want of it.
- `__attribute__((mode(M)))`, which says the declared type is whatever type this machine has in mode `M` rather than the type that was written. `unsigned int __attribute__((mode(QI)))` is an unsigned type one byte wide, and until now it was an `unsigned int`, which is four times the size the program asked for. That is tamnd/rucc#583, and six programs in the GCC torture suite were walking such an object byte by byte and running off the end of what they meant.

- A mode is GCC's name for the shape a value has on the machine rather than a name C has for a type, so it says a width and a register file and nothing about what the type was called. The class and the signedness come from the type it was written on: the same `mode(QI)` on an `int` and on an `unsigned int` gives two types that disagree about what all ones means. The integer modes are `QI`, `HI`, `SI`, `DI` and `TI`, the floating ones are `HF`, `SF`, `DF`, `XF` and `TF`, the complex ones are those five letters with a `C`, and `byte`, `word`, `pointer` and `unwind_word` are the ones that say what they want rather than naming a width.

- Two of the three classes are settled by a format rather than by a width, because a width does not tell one mode from another on every target: eighty bits of x87 and a hundred and twenty eight bits of quad precision are both sixteen bytes on x86-64 and are `XF` and `TF`. The order the floating types are searched in is what makes the answer right where two of them share a format, since `long double` is a `double` on Apple and on Windows and is quad precision on AArch64 Linux.

- Three refusals are worded the way gcc 16 words them, and all three are `E0698`: a name that is not a mode, a mode whose class is not the written type's class, and a mode this target has no C type for. Every size and every signedness the attribute produces was compared against gcc 16.2.0 on the same program, fifteen of them, and all fifteen agree.

- A vector mode, `V4SI` and the like, is refused with `E0699` and a note pointing at `vector_size`. That is the one place this does not agree with gcc, which builds the vector and warns that the spelling is deprecated, and its own note points at the same place. Ignoring it would declare one lane where the program asked for four, so refusing is the honest answer until a mode can name a vector here.

- `prune`, a pass that takes out the branch and the switch case document 10's ranges rule out. Section 24.4 says dead case removal is the one middle end transformation on switches and that it belongs in the same pass as the range based branch simplification of document 21, so it is one pass doing two things rather than two passes asking the same oracle the same question. It sits between `phiopt` and `simplify-cfg` in every pipeline above `-O0`, after the two passes that change the graph most and before the pass that cleans up what it leaves.

- A branch whose condition the ranges settle whichever way control reached the block becomes a jump to the arm they settle on. `if (x > 10) { if (x > 5) f(x); else g(x); }` loses the inner branch and the `g(x)` block entirely, because on every edge into the inner block `x` is above ten and the oracle says so without being asked twice. The question goes to `Ranges::compare` when the condition is a comparison and to `Ranges::at` when it is not, so a value that cannot be zero is a branch that always holds.

- This is not jump threading and the difference is worth writing down. Threading is about one edge into a block and it pays for itself by copying the block onto that edge, which is why tamnd/rucc#557 counts 623 corpus edges that decide their branch and only one that threads without a copy. This asks whether the branch goes the same way on every edge, so when the answer is yes there is nothing to copy and the branch just goes away. The two do not overlap: what threading wants is a branch that different edges decide differently.

- A switch case whose value the switched value cannot hold leaves the switch. `switch (x & 3u) { case 0: case 2: case 7: }` drops `case 7`, so the lowering gets two compares to place rather than three, and section 24.4 is explicit that this matters beyond the compare it saves because taking cases out can turn a sparse switch into a dense one and change the lowering decision entirely. A switch none of whose cases can be reached becomes a jump to its default, which is how `switch (x & 1u) { case 5: case 9: default: return 3; }` ends up as two instructions.

- It works out every answer before it rewrites anything, because the ranges are built on a control flow graph and a dominator tree that describe the function as it was a moment ago, and the first rewrite makes both of them wrong. The plan is collected in one walk and applied in a second, and the analysis cache is cleared by the pass itself rather than by the manager afterwards, which would be too late for the pass. What it leaves is a jump where a branch was and a block nothing reaches, and section 6.5 makes taking those out the obligation of whichever pass stranded them, so the pass calls `simplify_cfg`'s reachability walk rather than writing a second one next door.

- Across the corpus at -O2 this changes nothing: `.text` is 1114294 bytes on both sides over 1453 programs, the ratio against gcc 16 stays at 1.2576, no case grows or shrinks by a byte, and the same 57 cases fail on both sides. A census over all 1461 programs with `-fopt-info-all` finds 23 branches decided and one case removed across 24 programs, and in every one of them a later pass reached the same code on its own. That is the honest result and it is not a reason to leave the pass out, because the shape it is for is a caller that has already checked what the callee checks again, and that shape arrives with inlining, which is document 33 and M4.7.

- On that shape it is worth having. A loop that reads a table through a small checked accessor after checking the index itself is 454 bytes and 0.086 s without the pass, 411 bytes and 0.071 s with it, and 313 bytes and 0.023 s from gcc 16, all three printing the same 2778750000. The 657 branches the census reports as undecided are the other half of the picture: the oracle is asked at every conditional branch in the corpus and settles 23 of them, so the pass is cheap to ask and rarely answered yes on code nobody has inlined into.

### Fixed

- The register allocator gave a two address instruction's answer the register it read one of its operands from in a case where it may not: when the answer is a value a loop carries round. The shortcut is meant for an answer that starts at the instruction that writes it, and one written at the bottom of a loop and read at the top on the next turn is already live where the instruction reads. It then wants a register at the same time as the operand it was given, and an addition ends up reading the answer to the last one instead of its own operand.

- The condition the shortcut was asking, that the operand is read here for the last time, is necessary and was not sufficient. It now also asks that the answer's own range starts here, which is the half a loop breaks. The allocation checker was excusing the same pair on the same reasoning, so it agreed with the bug rather than catching it, and it has been corrected the same way.

- This is what a SQLite build with `-fsafety=detect` was going wrong on. `btreeComputeFreeSpace` has the shape in it, the free space it computed came out wrong, and `pragma integrity_check` on a table of a few hundred rows with an index reported free space corruption. The instrumentation was not the cause: it raised the register pressure to the point where the allocator took the shortcut, and the same function compiled without it did not. A shell built at that tier now gives byte for byte the same answers as a gcc built one on a script of two hundred thousand rows, `pragma integrity_check` included.

- The safety runtime now defines `rust_eh_personality`, without which a program that takes a maths function from the runtime archive fails to link. The archive carries `compiler_builtins`, and one of its units holds a weak `fmod` beside a reference to that name. A program that calls `fmod` with no libm on the link line takes the weak one, which pulls the unit in, which leaves a name to resolve that no part of the program ever calls. SQLite is such a program, which is how this was found.

- The definition has an empty body, which is the right one: being called would mean an unwind is in progress in a build where unwinding is off, and returning normally from a personality routine is what a phase it has nothing to say about looks like anyway. There is a case in the safety suite for it now, because every other case there is small enough that no unit of `compiler_builtins` is ever needed, so the archive had only ever been linked against programs that could not hit this.

- A `const` object whose image holds the address of something now goes in `.data.rel.ro` rather than `.rodata`. The address is not a number the compiler knows, so the image carries a relocation, and a relocation in a section the loader never makes writable is one the linker cannot apply without giving the whole image `DT_TEXTREL`. What that costs is the protection the read only section was for in the first place, since a program with `DT_TEXTREL` has its text pages made writable while the loader works on them, and some hardened toolchains refuse the link rather than do it.

- `.data.rel.ro` is the section that exists for exactly this: it is writable for as long as the loader is applying relocations to it and read only afterwards, which is what the `PT_GNU_RELRO` segment does. The variable ends up with the property the program asked for, and the link stays clean.

- A constant whose addresses are all of things this file both defines and keeps to itself goes in `.data.rel.ro.local` instead, which is the same section with a layout hint attached. The linker puts the local half in the first pages of the segment, so those pages are the ones the loader finishes with soonest. One name the file only declares is enough to lose the hint, because a name the link resolves from somewhere else is one another object may turn out to define.

- Mach-O has the same problem under a different name and gets the same treatment: a section in `__TEXT` is never writable, so a constant holding an address goes in `__DATA,__const`, which `dyld` writes and then protects. COFF needs none of this and is left alone, because a Windows image is relocated as a whole rather than a symbol at a time and an address in a read only section costs a base relocation and nothing else.

- Every variable that wants `.data.rel.ro.local` shares one section rather than getting one of its own. The ELF writer has no name of its own for that section, so it is added by hand, and asking for it a second time makes a second section with the same name rather than handing back the first. SQLite has enough const tables of function pointers in it to turn that into eighty odd sections in one object, each with a relocation section beside it, which is a pile of section headers describing eight bytes apiece.

- This is what made a SQLite build link with a warning. The shell has several `const` tables of function pointers in it, which is the shape that hits this, and the tables are the reason the whole image was picking up `DT_TEXTREL`.

- Every `long double` subtraction and every `long double` division came out with its operands the wrong way round, which is tamnd/rucc#585. `2.0L - 1.0L` gave `-1.0` and `4.0L / 2.0L` gave `0.5`, in every program, at every optimization level, since the x87 arithmetic first landed.

- The cause is a name rather than a table. The code generator pushes the left operand first and the right one on top of it, so what it wants is the one below against the top in that order, and which mnemonic computes that differs between the two assembly dialects. Intel's `FSUBP ST(i), ST(0)` is `ST(i) - ST(0)` and is `DE E8+i`, and AT&T's `fsubp` is `DE E0+i`, which is the other subtraction. This compiler writes AT&T and its own encoder agrees with gas byte for byte, so the answer is to ask for `fsubrp` and `fdivrp`, and the `r` in those two names is not a reversal of anything the code generator decided.

- An addition and a multiplication have one form each and do not care which operand is which, which is why half the arithmetic looked right and the bug lived as long as it did. It is also why the test for this reads the bytes of an object rather than the mnemonic in the listing: a name is what got this wrong, so a test that repeats the name agrees with the bug. `crates/rucc/tests/x87.rs` compiles four one line functions and asserts `DE E9` and `DE F9` are present and `DE E1` and `DE F1` are absent.

- Four gcc torture programs go from a wrong answer to a right one, `execute/960215-1.c`, `execute/conversion.c`, `execute/ieee/20010226-1.c` and `execute/regstack-1.c`, and all four were verified against gcc 16.2.0 on the same machine.

- `__builtin_expect` threw away anything the hint argument did, so `__builtin_expect(n, i++)` never incremented `i`, which is tamnd/rucc#584. The same went for `__builtin_expect_with_probability`. The value of either call is its first argument, and that was answered by handing the first argument back and dropping the rest of the call on the floor, hint and side effects together.

- What gcc does with the hint depends on the first argument, which is not a rule anybody would design and is what gcc 16.2.0 does at `-O0`, `-O1` and `-O2` alike. A first argument that is an integer constant expression folds the whole call where it is written and the hint goes with it, so `__builtin_expect(5, side())` never calls `side`. A first argument that is not one leaves the hints standing. That is now what this does: the constant case is unchanged, and the other case builds a comma expression so the hints run in the order they were written and the value comes last.

- The behaviour was measured across five shapes rather than reasoned about, being the hint dropped and the hint kept, the value used and the value thrown away, and a constant first argument against a variable one. gcc prints the same five answers at all three optimization levels. This compiler agreed on two of the five and is now the same on all five. `gcc.c-torture/execute/pr85156.c` is a program in the GCC torture suite that notices, since the value it returns is an increment written inside a hint.

- A function whose blocks dispatch to each other through a computed goto no longer takes minutes to compile. The range machinery deliberately refuses to cache a range it worked out while a cycle was open, because that range was worked out under an assumption, and the three limits it already had bound one walk each rather than what a function's worth of questions adds up to. Eight blocks that jump to each other through a label array are eight block parameters in one cycle, nothing about any of them is ever cached, and every question about any one of them walks all eight, so the cost multiplies rather than adds. A corpus program with 32 arms sat at the runner's two minute compile cap and now finishes in 0.21 s.

- The fix is a fourth limit, `Options::budget`, which counts how many definitions one set of queries works out before it stops narrowing, and is 4096 by default. Past it every answer is the whole of the type, which is what a range knowing nothing is, so a program over the limit loses code quality and not correctness. It is spent where the work is rather than at the query, since a question the cache answers costs nothing. `Counts::exhausted` reports how many answers came back that way, which is the difference section 10.7 cares about between a range that lost information at an opcode and a range that never got worked out at all.

- Nothing real pays for it. The whole corpus at -O2 gives byte identical `.text` with the limit in and out, so 4096 is above what any of the 1453 programs needs, and the three computed goto programs that were over it are the only ones that notice.

## 0.7.7

### Added

- `__atomic_always_lock_free` and `__atomic_is_lock_free`, which are the two names in the atomic family that ask about the machine rather than doing anything to an object. Both answer whether an object of a given size and alignment is one the compiler writes an instruction for rather than a call to a library, and both are folded to a constant where they are written, so a program that asks gets a `mov` of a nought or a one and no call anywhere.

- The size has to be a power of two no wider than the widest access this compiler writes, which is a new `lock_free_width` in the target description and is eight bytes on every target it has. x86-64 does sixteen bytes atomically with `cmpxchg16b` and AArch64 does it with its pair instructions, and nothing here writes either, so claiming sixteen would be a yes followed by a call into a library that has to take a lock. gcc answers the same way for sixteen bytes on both machines, which was checked rather than assumed.

- The pointer is what says how the object is aligned, and a null one means the type's own alignment, which is what gcc documents and what every use in a header passes. The alignment is read off the type the pointer points at rather than off an analysis of the address, so `__atomic_always_lock_free(4, &p->v)` where `v` sits at an odd offset in a packed structure is yes here and no in gcc. It is not a wrong answer on x86-64, where the `lock` prefix works at any alignment, and it would be one on a machine where it does not, so it is written down in the code rather than left to be found.

- gcc lets the second name become a call into libatomic when it cannot decide, and there is no libatomic behind this compiler, so both names give the first one's answer. That is the stronger claim of the two and so is never wrong where it says yes. A size the compiler cannot fold is answered no rather than refused, because refusing would break the header idiom these appear in, where the size is often a macro that came out of some other platform.

- Every answer that gcc gives a constant for was compared against gcc 16.2.0 on the same program: one, two, four and eight bytes, sixteen bytes, three bytes, and eight bytes through an `int *` against eight bytes through a `long *`. All seven agree, including the last pair, which is the one where the alignment rather than the size decides it. The non constant size is not in that list because gcc answers it with a call and there is nothing to compare against.

- `value_replacement`, section 22.2's second transformation, which is the one where no select gets written at all. When the two arms of a branch hand the join block different values but the branch condition is enough to settle which of the two is right, the join takes that one directly. `a == b ? b : a` is `a`, and it compiles to a move and a return where it used to compile to a compare, a conditional move and the two values feeding them.

- It is the first thing in the compiler to call document 10's relational oracle. `Ranges::compare` has been sitting in `crates/rucc-opt/src/range/query.rs` since the range work landed, answering whether two values compare a given way at a given block, and until now nothing asked it anything. This asks it one question per candidate pair: are these two equal on the edge that arm leaves by. If they are, the other arm's value is right on both edges, so it is the one the join gets.

- The question is only asked when the branch is on an equality or an inequality, which is the same gate GCC puts on this. Without it the pass would run a range query on every diamond it sees, and section 22.7 names that query as the expensive part of the whole pass. With it the query runs only where an answer is possible, since a condition that is not about two things being equal is not going to establish that two things are equal. It costs nothing measurable: compiling the whole corpus takes 110726 ms with this in against 113336 ms without, over 1453 programs, so the pass is not paying for the oracle in any way a measurement can see. gcc 16 takes 240832 ms on the same set.

- The question gets asked before the refusals rather than after, and that ordering is worth the note because it is what makes the pointer case work. One of the refusals is that the join value has a width no select is lowered at, and a value the condition settles never gets a select, so asking first turns a refusal into a fold. `void *f(void *p, void *q) { return p == q ? q : p; }` kept its branch until now, because a select of two pointers has nothing to lower to. It is one `movq` and a `ret`.

- The thing it finds that no peephole rule in the set finds is `abs` on an unsigned value. `x > 0u ? x : 0u - x` is a move and a return now against eight instructions before, because the compare rules canonicalize an unsigned `> 0` into a `!= 0`, which passes the equality gate, and then on the edge where `x` is zero the negation is zero too, so the two arms agree there and the other arm wins. The signed version is left alone, which is the right answer rather than a limitation, since `0 - x` is not `x` at `INT_MIN` and the arms genuinely disagree.

- It only looks at the values the arms hand over, not at what those values are made of. `a == b ? b + c : a + c` keeps its select, because the pair the join sees is two different adds rather than `a` and `b`, and settling it means pushing the same question down into the operands. That is a small change to make and it is not made here, because nothing has measured a use for it.

- Across the corpus at -O2 this takes 53 bytes off `.text` over 1453 programs, moves the ratio against gcc 16 from 1.2838 to 1.2837, and leaves the same 57 cases failing on both sides. Two programs shrink and none grow, and both of them are the unsigned `abs` above. A census over all 1461 programs with `-fopt-info-all` finds two remarks where the condition settled a value and fourteen where a join value still has a width no select reaches. Two hits in fourteen hundred programs is the finding rather than the disappointment: the corpus has almost no code where a branch tests two things for equality and then hands one of those two things to the join, and that gap is filed on tamnd/rucc-corpus.

### Changed

- A move of a constant into a byte, sixteen bit or thirty two bit register is one byte shorter. x86-64 writes that instruction two ways: `C7 /0` with an addressing byte naming the destination, and `B8+r` with the destination folded into the low three bits of the opcode. The encoder only knew the first, and the second is a byte shorter for every register there is.

- It is worth an entry of its own because a constant reaching a register is the single most common thing the encoder is asked to write, so the byte comes off nearly everywhere rather than in a corner. Over the 1113 corpus programs that build for x86-64 at `-O2` it takes 17309 bytes off `.text` out of 416105, which is 4.16 percent, and every one of the 1113 got smaller with none going the other way.

- The sixty four bit move keeps its addressing byte. The short form there carries all eight bytes of the constant where the addressed form sign extends four, so `C7 /0` is seven bytes against ten and wins whenever the number fits in thirty two bits, which is nearly always, and the ten byte form stays for the numbers that do not fit. The short form only reaches a register while the addressed one reaches memory as well, but nothing in the compiler writes an immediate to memory, so the three narrow moves give up nothing by keeping only the short row.

- Inline assembly whose template has no instructions in it is lowered, which is the first half of tamnd/rucc#349. Nothing lowered an `asm` at all before, so a function containing one stopped with `no rule lowers a inline_asm`. An empty template is most of the inline assembly in a test suite and it is not an accident of one: a program that wants a value computed where it stands, or a loop the optimizer must not touch, writes `asm volatile ("" : : : "memory")`, and what it is asking for is the barrier and the places its operands share rather than any instruction.

- Which means the constraints are the real work and the instructions are none. An output an input is tied to, by `+` or by a matching constraint like `"0"`, is the register that input arrived in, because the template wrote nothing over it, so it is a rename and not a move. An output nothing is tied to is whatever the assembly left in the register, which for a template that writes nothing is a value the program has no claim on, and it is written as a zero because the register allocator is owed a definition before the use whatever the program is owed. An operand in memory is an address the front end already built. The clobber list is not read, and on an empty template that is right rather than an omission, since a template with no instructions in it ruins no register.

- Reading a statement's operands back off the instruction is one rule in one place, `rucc_ir::AsmOperands`, because the constraint list is the only record of which entry took which value. It refuses a list that does not describe what the instruction carries rather than guessing, since which register file an operand travels in is a decision the front end made with the type in hand and a structure goes as an address whatever its constraint says. A guess there is a wrong program and a refusal is a message.

- A template with anything in it is refused with a message that says which of the two things is missing, and so is an `asm goto`. Assembling a template needs the assembler `spec/11-asm-objects-debug.md` section 11.1 describes and there is not one yet, and that is what is left of tamnd/rucc#349.

### Fixed

- Machine blocks are filled in reverse postorder rather than in the order the IR wrote them, which is tamnd/rucc#590. A value is written in a block that dominates every block that reads it, and a block in reverse postorder comes before every block it dominates, so filling in that order always reaches the definition before the read. The order the blocks are written in has no such property. A block written near the top of a function can read a value a block further down writes, and lowering reaches the read first.

- What went wrong when it did is that reading a value with no register yet mints one. The definition further down then writes its own register, which is not the one the read named, and nothing ever writes the one the read named. The instruction that reads it gets whatever was left in that place, and after allocation that is a stack slot no store ever reached. It is a wrong answer rather than a crash, which is why it took a real program to find.

- The real program was SQLite 3.53.4 built on x86-64 Linux. The amalgamation compiled and the shell ran, but valgrind reported 31 uninitialised value errors on a script that inserts two hundred thousand rows and reads them back. Following one of them down to the instruction found a load from a slot with no matching store, and the machine IR before allocation had a block argument printed as `%?`, which is what the printer says for a register nothing defines. The function was `sqlite3_exec`, out of a quarter of a million lines. With the order fixed the same script gives byte for byte what the gcc build gives, and valgrind reports nothing.

- Blocks the entry does not reach are filled last, in the order they were written. Nothing runs them and nothing they name is read by anything that does, but they still have to be filled, because a machine block with no terminator is not one the passes below can read. The order the blocks come out in does not change either, only the order they are filled in, so the machine function is still written the way the IR function was.

- The allocation checker asks a fifth question, which is whether every value the function reads is written first on every path that reaches the read. It asked four before and none of them was that one, so the function above was an assignment the checker was happy with. The answer is already computed: a value read before it is written is a value live on the way into the entry block, and the liveness the allocator needs anyway says which those are. It is not a question about the allocation, since the value would be read before it was written whatever register it went to, but this is where the answer is sitting.

- That check is what turned the bug from a valgrind report into a panic naming the function, and it is the reason to keep it rather than a step on the way. It runs in debug and CI builds, the same as the other four. The whole amalgamation now compiles with it on at every optimisation level.

- The address of a function this file does not define is read out of the global offset table rather than worked out from where the instruction is, which is tamnd/rucc#355. Everything we emit is position independent, so the old answer was the distance from the instruction to the name, and that is a number the linker only has when it is putting both ends in the same program. A function a shared library may turn out to define is not such a name, so taking its address built a program the link refused. The answer is `R_X86_64_REX_GOTPCRELX` and one `movq` in place of the `leaq`: the linker gives the name a slot, the slot is in this program so the distance to it is a number the linker has, and the code loads the address instead of computing it.

- Nothing is lost in the case that was already right, because the linker relaxes the load back into the address computation when the name turns out to have been in this program after all. That is what the `REX` in the relocation's name is for: it says the instruction is a `mov` with a REX prefix, which is what lets the linker rewrite it without having to disassemble anything.

- Only functions, and only the ones this file does not define. The address of an object another file defines is still computed the same way it was, and that is correct rather than a case left over. A link answers a reference to an object in a shared library by making room for it in this program and copying it there, so the name does end up somewhere this file can measure to. A function cannot be copied, since it has exactly one address every object in the program has to agree on, or two pointers to it compare unequal.

- Which names those are is a fact about the whole module and the code generator is given one function at a time, so it is worked out once in the driver and handed in. It is not in the IR and not in the flags: `--emit=ir` prints the same thing it printed before, because which relocation an address wants is a question about a machine and a file format rather than about the program.

## 0.7.6

### Added

- Conditional store replacement, section 22.2's fourth transformation, in the half of it that needs no proof. When both arms of a branch store to the same place, `if (c) *p = a; else *p = b;` becomes `*p = c ? a : b`, and the branch goes with the rest of them. It is in `phiopt` rather than in a pass of its own, because section 22.2 lists it as one of the five things that pass does and it shares the shape matcher with the other four.

- Half, and which half is the whole point. Section 22.6 calls the other half the worst bug in the document. A store made on a path that was not going to make one writes memory the program was not going to write, and the load modify store form that reads the location and writes back what it read is not a no-op: it is a write, so it races with another thread writing the same bytes, and it faults if the page is read only. What would license it is knowing the location is written whatever happens, and nothing in the compiler can answer that yet. When both arms store to the same address the question does not arise, because the shape discharges it: one store before and one store after, to the same address, of a value the program was going to write there either way.

- What has to match beyond the address is the access itself. The flags, and the alignment, size, aliasing node and `restrict` scope a store carries alongside them, because the one store written below carries one of each and two that disagree have no single answer to carry. `volatile` is refused outright, because section 22.6 says never and because both how many accesses there are and what order they come in are observable. Atomic is refused for the ordering rather than the access, since a store with an order on it is a fence as much as a write.

- The address has to be the same value rather than a provably equal one, which is the strong form of the question and the only form available without an alias analysis. It also settles where the address comes from, since neither arm dominates the other and a value both of them name is worked out at or above the branch. It is also where most of what this does not catch goes, so the refusal is counted in two kinds rather than one. `if (x > 128) q[i] = 128; else q[i] = x;` works `q + i` out twice, once in each arm, and two instructions that compute the same address are two values, so this walks away from a store pair that goes to the same place by any reading a person would give it. What fixes that is document 16's value numbering, not anything about memory, and writing `int *p = &q[i];` above the branch is enough to get the fold today.

- On a clamp loop over four million random bytes with the address hoisted, `if (x > 128) *q = 128; else *q = x;`, this is 0.61 seconds down to 0.21 and sixteen bytes off `.text`. The store below the branch leaves a diamond with nothing in its arms, which converts to a `cmovne`, and the loop body ends up with no data dependent branch where it had one. gcc 16 is 0.13 seconds on the same loop.

- Across the corpus it changes nothing at all, and that is the finding rather than a disappointment. A census across all 1461 programs with `-fopt-info-all` finds zero diamonds where both arms store, zero where both store and the two do not match, and four where one path stores and the other does not. The corpus has no instance of this shape, which is why the measurement above is a microbenchmark, and it is filed on tamnd/rucc-corpus.

- `short-circuit`, which is section 22.5 of `spec/optimizer/22-phiopt-and-if-conversion.md`. When both halves of an `&&` or an `||` are cheap and safe to work out whichever way the first half goes, both are worked out and combined with an `and` or an `or`, and the branch between them goes. Two unpredictable branches become one, and the one that is left is often a shape `phiopt` can then turn into a conditional move.

- The shape it matches is not the one a reader coming from GCC would look for. GCC's `LOGICAL_OP_NON_SHORT_CIRCUIT` works on a chain of two conditional jumps, because that is what its front end emits. The rucc front end lowers `a && b` as a value rather than as control flow, so what arrives at the optimizer is a branch on `a` whose short-circuiting side hands the join block a constant bit and whose other side works `b` out and hands that over. That is a triangle rather than a chain, and it is the same shape `phiopt` already matches, so the pass reuses that matcher instead of writing a second one. An `&&` puts a zero bit on the not taken side and an `||` puts a set bit on the taken side, and which of the two it is says whether the fold writes an `and` or an `or`.

- It runs before `thread` and after `simplify`, and the order is the difference between the pass working and the pass doing nothing. Threading rewrites the constant edge to point straight at the branch that constant decides, which turns the triangle into exactly the chain GCC starts from, and by then the shape this matches is gone. It is in `-O2` and `-O3` and not in `-O1`, per the document, and not in `-Os` or `-Oz` either, because what it removes is time and what it adds is instructions on a path that was not running them.

- It refuses on anything with effects, which is what makes `if (p && p->x)` safe without a special case for it, since a load has effects and the whole point of the second half being guarded is that it is not allowed to run first. It also refuses a right half that could trap, a right half longer than the new `SHORT_CIRCUIT_INSTRUCTIONS`, which is three, a branch the estimate says goes one way nearly always, and a condition that is already decided, which belongs to `simplify_cfg` rather than here. Every refusal records a reason under `-fopt-info-all`.

- On a hot loop counting how many of four million random bytes fall in a band, `x > 100 && x < 200` with neither half predictable, this is 0.73 seconds down to 0.22 and sixteen bytes off `.text`. The size is incidental and the time is not. What happens is that collapsing the two branches into one `and` leaves a single join, which `phiopt` then converts to a `cmovne`, so the loop body ends up with no data dependent branch in it at all where it had two. gcc 16 is 0.06 seconds on the same loop because it vectorizes it, which is a different pass and a different milestone.

- Across the corpus at -O2 this takes 373 bytes off `.text` over 1453 programs, moves the ratio against gcc 16 from 1.2842 to 1.2838, and leaves the same 57 cases failing on both sides. Eight programs shrink and none grow. That is a small number and the reason is worth writing down: only 34 of the corpus's 1461 programs contain an `&&` or an `||` at all, so the corpus is close to blind to this transformation, and a census across every program with `-fopt-info-all` finds eight remarks and no misses. The measurement above is a microbenchmark because the corpus cannot yet supply one.
- A `long double` that gets converted now compiles, which is the first thing this compiler does with the type rather than about it. A `double` or a `float` widening to one, one narrowing back, a signed integer becoming one, one becoming a signed integer, and a copy from one address to another. Six shapes, and every one of them is a group of x87 instructions the code generator writes rather than a rule, since what one instruction in a group leaves behind and the next picks up is the top of a stack no rule can name.

- Every eighty bit value gets sixteen bytes of the frame, aligned to sixteen, and keeps them for the whole function. That is what the psABI says the type takes even though ten of the bytes mean anything, so a slot holding one agrees with an array of them. What is in a register is the address, and it is worked out again at every use with a `lea` rather than kept in one, because an address held from a definition to its last use would hold a general purpose register open across everything in between.

- Converting to an integer is nine instructions, which is the one conversion here with no single instruction behind it. C cuts towards zero and the unit rounds the way its control word says, so the word is saved with `fnstcw`, ORed with the two bits that mean truncate, loaded back with `fldcw`, used, and put back. `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.8 says why the one instruction answer, `fisttp`, is not used: it is SSE3, the x86-64 baseline is not, and there is nothing in the target description yet that can gate an instruction on a CPU feature.

- A block parameter of this type is the address of the slot the value is in, and the block copies the sixteen bytes into a slot of its own at the top. It cannot keep the address it was handed, because a second edge hands over a second one and a read after the block would then be a read of whichever edge was taken rather than of one place. The copies of a block are one group: every load runs before every store and the stores run backwards, so the arriving values are all on the x87 stack at once and no store lands on a slot another load still has to read, which is what makes the back edge of a loop that swaps two of these come out right.

- Arithmetic on a `long double` now compiles too. An add, a subtract, a multiply, a divide, a negate and all fourteen comparisons, each written as a group the same way the conversions are, for the same reason: the operands of an `faddp` are the top two of the x87 stack and the answer is left where they were, so there is nothing in the middle of the group a rule could bind and nothing its replacement could name. A whole expression in `long double` compiles now, and against clang the instructions are the same ones in the same order, with a store and a load between every pair of them that clang does not need.

- The left operand goes on the stack first in the arithmetic and second in a comparison, and both orders are on purpose. `fsubp` takes the top from the one below it, so the left operand being underneath is what makes it `a - b`, which is why `fsubrp` and `fdivrp` are described here and used nowhere. `fucomip` asks about the top against what is under it, so the operand the predicate is about has to go on last. Which of the ten comparison opcodes runs, and whether the operands were swapped to get there, is read off the same table the vector comparisons use, since a `long double` comparison that picked a different condition from the `double` comparison of the same two numbers would disagree with it at exactly the unordered cases.

- A negation of a `long double` is no longer turned into an exclusive or with the sign bit before it reaches the back end. That rewrite is right at thirty two and sixty four bits and is nothing at eighty, where the integer it would flip the bit in has no register either, so what it handed the code generator was three instructions it could not write instead of one it could. It now stops at sixty four bits, and the eighty bit case is `fchs`, which is what the machine has.

- A constant of this type compiles, and it turns out to need no x87 instruction at all. A slot holding an eighty bit value is the value, so `1.5L` is its ten bytes written where the value lives, and whatever reads it does the `fld` it would have done anyway. Two moves and two stores, because the machine stores eight bytes at a time and has no store of an immediate to memory. The six bytes above the ten are left alone: they are the padding that makes the type sixteen wide and the psABI says they are unspecified rather than zero.

- The other way is a constant pool, an `fldt` of a symbol and a relocation, which is what a compiler with somewhere to put a literal does. There is nowhere to put one here yet, which is the same reason a `double` constant is spelled as an integer in a register, and four instructions in the frame is what that costs until there is.

- A cast between `unsigned long` and `long double` compiles, which is the last shape of the type that is not the calling convention. Neither is an instruction the machine has: `fildll` reads its operand as signed, so a word above the signed range comes back too small by two to the sixty fourth and gets that constant added back, and the same thing the other way has two to the sixty third taken off before `fistpll` and the top bit put back after.

- Both of those come out shorter at eighty bits than the same two do at thirty two and sixty four, because a float with sixty four bits of significand holds every value of a sixty four bit integer exactly. Nothing rounds, so the halving and the round to odd the narrower conversions need have nothing to do here and are gone.

- What picks the correction is a multiply rather than a mask. At the narrower widths the choice is a mask laid over the bits of the result, which reads the float as an integer of its own width, and there is no such integer at eighty bits any more than there is a register. So the comparison becomes a float that is a one or a positive zero and the constant is multiplied by it, which gives the constant or a positive zero to add or subtract, and adding or subtracting a positive zero leaves every value alone. Still no branch either way, which is the property the narrower pair was written for.

- A `long double` parameter and a `long double` return compile, which is the last thing tamnd/rucc#540 asked for and is two different answers rather than one. SysV classifies the type X87 and X87UP, which is the classification that means memory, so an argument travels in the argument area whatever is left of the two register files and what the two ends pass is the address of its sixteen bytes. That is what a structure the classification put in memory already did here, so a parameter of this type is bound the way one of those is and the value stays where the caller put it for the whole function.

- Giving one back is the direction that is not memory. It comes back in `st(0)`, so a `return` is an `fld` of the value's slot and nothing else, and the x87 stack is not empty when the function returns, which is the one place in this back end where that is true and is exactly what the convention asks for. A call that gives one back writes no register at all and the value is taken off with an `fstp` straight after the call. Against clang the argument area offsets are the same, including the eight bytes skipped to align one behind a seventh integer argument, and a `long double` passed to `printf` lands in the same place with the same count of vector registers in `%al`.

- So a `?:` that picks between two of these and a loop that keeps one across its back edge both compile, which was the last shape of the type the back end turned away. Two things are still refused and both are bounds rather than work not done: a block with more than eight parameters of this type, because eight is how many the x87 stack holds at once and the copies go through it, and a `long double` beside another value in a return, because a pair comes back in a pair of registers and there is no pair with that stack in it. Neither is expressible in C without going looking for it.

- `factor_out_conditional_operation`, which is section 22.2's third transformation in `spec/optimizer/22-phiopt-and-if-conversion.md` and the second of the five to be built. When both arms of a branch worked out their answers the same way from different operands, the select goes under the operation rather than over it, so `cond ? f(a) : f(b)` becomes `f(cond ? a : b)`. One operation where there were two, and the same one select either way.

- It is structural rather than a rewrite rule for the reason the document gives about all five of them: the two operations are in different blocks and no pattern spans blocks. By the time they are in one block the arms have been hoisted and the select written, and undoing that is a larger rewrite than never writing it.

- The two operations have to match in everything but one operand. The flags, because those are what the optimizer is licensed to assume and one copy written under the union of two sets of assumptions would be claiming on one path something only the other path established. Whatever else the instruction carries, which for a comparison is the predicate, since two predicates are two different questions. And exactly one operand position apart, because two positions apart needs two selects and one operation, which is what one select and two operations already cost.

- Arms that agree in every position write no select at all. Both sides working out the same thing from the same operands is what a common subexpression nothing has numbered looks like from a branch, and one copy of it serves both. That is where the largest single case in the corpus comes from.

- What is factored does not count against the length rule, because it is not speculated. Both arms did the operation, one of them was always going to do it, and afterwards one copy runs whichever way the branch would have gone. So a diamond whose arms factor away entirely converts on the same terms as one with empty arms, and a three instruction arm that factors to nothing is no longer refused for being too long.

- Across the corpus at -O2 this takes 784 bytes off `.text` over 1453 programs, moves the ratio against gcc 16 from 1.2851 to 1.2842, and leaves the same 57 cases failing on both sides. 39 programs shrink and 4 grow. On a hot loop whose two arms both add to the same accumulator, 4096 unpredictable branches over forty thousand passes, it is 390 milliseconds down to 350 and 19 bytes of `.text`.

- The four programs that grow are the finding worth writing down. All four are `cond ? p + 2 : p + 1`, where the operand the arms differ in is a constant, so each operation was taking it as an immediate and factoring turns two free immediates into a select between two values that have to be in registers. That costs five bytes. The same shape inside a loop, `total += 1` against `total += 1000`, saves fourteen, because the constants were being rematerialized every iteration anyway. Refusing every constant operand was built and measured and trades thirty two bytes of win for twenty two bytes of loss, which is ten bytes across 1453 programs and not worth a rule, so there is no rule.

- The x87 arithmetic, described, encoded and written, which is most of the fifth of the seven things tamnd/rucc#540 needs and is the first thing this target does to an eighty bit float rather than with one. An add, a multiply, a subtract and a divide each way round, the sign flipped and the sign cleared, and the ten comparisons a float has anywhere.

- These are the first instructions in the description that name nothing at all, not an operand and not an address. Both sources of an add and its destination are depths on a stack nothing allocates from, so an allocator looking at one sees an instruction that touches nothing it has any say over. The load and the store from tamnd/rucc#563 name an address and were already halfway there, and this is the rest of the way.

- A subtraction and a division come in two because a depth cannot be swapped. A pair of registers can be named in either order and which of two values is on top was settled when the code generator pushed them, so an operation the other way round is another instruction rather than the same one written differently. That is what `fsubrp` and `fdivrp` are, and it is why an addition and a multiplication need one each.

- A comparison is three instructions and one opcode, which is the vector arrangement with one more instruction in it. `fucomip` writes the same flags `ucomisd` writes rather than the status word the x87 has of its own, and it takes one value off the stack when there were two on it, so the pop that throws the other one away is part of the opcode rather than something left to whatever comes next. An opcode that left the stack deeper than it found it would be one the discipline in `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.8 cannot be stated for, since that rule is about a group and a group that does not put the stack back is not one.

- A kind of argument for a position on the x87 stack, which is a depth rather than a register and so is not the kind that names one. The encoder never reads the number, because every row that takes one is a fixed opcode with the depth already in its second byte, and what the argument does there is pick the row: the x87 mnemonics that take a stack position also have forms that take an address, and a lookup that could not tell the two apart would encode one as the other. The bytes of all eighteen are checked against what the assembler produces for the same lines.

- Switch lowering, per `spec/optimizer/24-switch-lowering.md`, as a module of its own rather than the few lines in the expander it used to be. The cases are sorted, partitioned into clusters and given a decision tree, which is section 24.2's design adopted as it is written. Two of the four shapes are there. A cluster of one value is one equality test, which is what every case was before this. A cluster of consecutive values that all go to the same place is one subtraction and one unsigned comparison however long the stretch is, so `case 'a' ... 'z'` is twenty six cases in the IR and two instructions in the machine code.

- The partition is what makes the rest of document 24 additions rather than rewrites. A jump table and a bit test are each a variant of the cluster enum and an arm in the function that writes a test, not a different way of taking a `switch` apart. The bit test came next and is below. The jump table is waiting on `Opcode::IndirectBr`, which is tamnd/rucc#353 and is the same thing a computed goto waits on, and on a read only section to put the table in.

- The tree splits with a signed comparison and the cases are sorted by their signed reading, which is the answer to a question the IR does not have. A `switch` carries a width and no signedness, so nothing here can ask whether the program switched on an `int` or an `unsigned`. What makes that harmless is that the sort and the split use the same order, so the tree is consistent with itself and every value comes down it to the one cluster that can hold it. A range test is unaffected either way, because `x - low` against `high - low` unsigned is modular arithmetic.

- Section 24.6's two arithmetic failure modes are handled by construction rather than by a check. The width of a run is worked out in `i128`, which holds the difference of any two values of any type C can switch on. A run that covers a whole type comes out as a width of every bit set, which read unsigned is a test true of everything, and that is right for a `switch` no value falls out of. There is a test at `i8` over all two hundred and fifty six values that pins it.

- Two cases of the same value now stop the compiler rather than being quietly reconciled, which is what section 24.6 asks for. C forbids them and the front end rejects them, so everything here is written believing the clusters are disjoint. Dropping the later of a pair leaves its arm with nothing branching to it, which the IR verifier refuses, and keeping both puts two clusters of the same value into a search that assumes it can tell them apart. A `switch` that arrives with a duplicate is a bug above this and stopping on it is how it gets found.

- The tests check where values go and not what the code looks like. Counting comparisons says the tree is small and says nothing about whether it is right, and a decision tree that sends one value down the wrong side is a miscompilation no amount of counting finds. So the test module runs the control flow the lowering wrote and asserts that every probe value lands on exactly the block its case named, with a probe either side of every case and at both ends of the type, over a small `switch`, a search, cases straddling zero and a mix of runs and single values.

- The bit test, per `spec/optimizer/24-switch-lowering.md` section 24.3, which is the third of document 24's four shapes and the one `case 'a': case 'e': case 'i': case 'o': case 'u':` has been waiting for. Values scattered through a span narrower than a word become one bound, one shift and one test per destination, however many values there are, in place of one equality test each. The shape is the one `gcc/tree-switch-conversion.h` states, `if ((1 << (x - low)) & mask)`, with a mask per destination sharing the one shift.

- The grouping runs over the sorted clusters before the tree is built, so a bit test is a cluster like any other and the tree searches over it without knowing what is inside. It is the greedy pass of the two section 24.3 describes, taking the longest reach from each starting point rather than the quadratic search for the fewest groups overall, because the difference between them is a group boundary in a place that costs one test and the quadratic one is quadratic.

- A group has to have three more case values in it than it has destinations before it is written, which is a number counted rather than measured, and the counting is enough here in a way it was not for the leaf size. A walk over n values is 2n instructions and ends in a branch taken about one time in n. A bit test over the same n is 5 instructions and 2 per destination and ends in a branch taken about as often. Neither shape leans on the branch predictor differently from the other, so what is left is 2n against 5 + 2t, and three is where that crosses with room to spare.

- Only single values are grouped and a run is left alone. A run is already one subtraction and one comparison however many values it holds, so folding one into a mask would replace two instructions with two instructions and spend a word of the group's span doing it. A `switch` that mixes the two comes out as a run next to a bit test, each testing what it is good at.

- The span bound is sixty four because a mask holds sixty four bits, which makes it a correctness bound and not a tuning one, and it is enforced when the group is formed rather than checked afterwards. The bound on the operand before the shift is there for the same reason: shifting by more than the width of the word is undefined, the amount is the operand, and section 24.6 is firm that this check is not an optimization decision and cannot be dropped because the value looks like it has to be in range.

- A group whose masks between them account for every value in its span writes one test fewer than it has destinations, because the last destination is where anything in range that matched nothing else has to go. That is the common shape when a `switch` alternates between two arms over a stretch with no holes in it.

- The tests assert where each value goes and not what the code looks like, over a group sharing one arm, a group with three destinations, a group whose masks cover its span, a group starting below zero, a group cut off at the word, and a group refused for being too thin. The interpreter the routing tests run on now holds every value at the width its own type says, because a bit test computes in a word whatever the operand's width is and an interpreter that assumed one width would get the mask wrong and agree with itself while doing it.

- Measured on server2 with gcc 16 for reference, a character classifier over four thousand passes of a four kilobyte buffer goes from 260 milliseconds to 110 with ten scattered letters going to one arm, and from 430 to 240 with three arms and a mix of runs and scattered values, with `.text` down 112 bytes in both. The gap to gcc on the same programs is not the switch shape, it is that gcc vectorizes the whole classification loop into SSE compares.

- Across the 1453 corpus programs that record a `.text` size, this changes not one byte and breaks nothing, and the ratio against gcc 16 stays at 1.2851 with the same 57 cases failing on both sides. That is a statement about the corpus rather than about the pass: nothing in it has a `switch` with scattered cases sharing an arm, which is what tamnd/rucc-corpus#25 was filed about, and the two programs that show the win had to be written by hand.

- The leaf size is measured rather than picked, and the measurement is the interesting part. On paper a walk of `n` cases is `n` comparisons and a search is about `log2(n)`, so the search should win from about five cases upward. The machine does not agree, because the two kinds of comparison do not cost the same. Every compare in a walk is a branch almost never taken, one case out of `n`, so the predictor gets all of them right and the front end runs several per cycle. Every branch in a search goes each way about half the time, so a fair share of them mispredict and each of those costs the whole pipeline. Measured on an interpreter loop dispatching on a sparse `switch`, four million iterations picking a case at random, a search costs sixteen percent at seventeen cases and twenty two percent at twenty four, and saves nine percent at thirty six, twenty three percent at fifty and half at a hundred. So the threshold is thirty two, which is where those two lines cross, and not the four the paper argument gives. gcc has the same knob under the name `case-values-threshold` and a small number in it, which is right for gcc because gcc reaches for a jump table first and the tree is what it falls back to.

- Across the 1461 corpus programs at -O2 the effect is almost nothing, and that is a statement about the corpus rather than about the pass. Four programs change. One shrinks by 32 bytes because its shared arms merged into a run, and three grow by 16 each because they are the only programs in the corpus with more than thirty two cases in one `switch`. `.text` moves by 16 bytes in total, the ratio against GCC 16 stays at 1.2851, no answer changed anywhere and the same 57 cases fail before and after. What the corpus is missing is switches with consecutive cases sharing an arm, which is where the run test pays and which real parsers are full of, so the number to fix is the corpus and not the threshold.

## 0.7.5

### Added

- The x87 conversions, described, encoded and written, which is the fourth of the seven things tamnd/rucc#540 needs. They are not instructions of their own on this machine: they are the same load and the same store reading and writing a different format, so `flds`, `fldl`, `fildl` and `fildll` bring a `float`, a `double` and an integer up to eighty bits, and `fstps`, `fstpl`, `fistpl` and `fistpll` take one back down. Nothing converts between two things on the stack and nothing could, because the stack holds one format and only one.

- Every widening is exact, which is why none of the four coming up can round. Sixty four bits of significand and fifteen of exponent hold every `float`, every `double` and every sixty four bit integer outright. The two narrowings to a float do round, and they round to nearest, which is what the control word says unless something has changed it and what C asks for.

- `fnstcw` and `fldcw`, which are there for the one conversion this machine has no single instruction for. C cuts a float towards zero and the unit rounds the way its control word says, so an eighty bit float becoming an integer is a group of five: the word saved, the saved word ORed with `0x0c00` and loaded back to set the rounding field to truncate, the value taken off the stack, and the original word put back. `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.8 writes the group out. The short answer is `fisttp`, which truncates on its own, and it is not used because it is SSE3: that is not in the x86-64 baseline, this target has no notion of a CPU feature to gate an instruction on, and emitting one anyway would build programs that fault on machines the target says it supports. It is the obvious thing to select once there is a way to say a feature is present.

- A third instruction shape for the two control word instructions, next to the two the loads and the stores use. They carry an address and name no register the allocator picked, the same as the other ten, and they are their own shape because a shape says what an instruction is as well as what its operands are, and these are the only two here that touch the x87 and are neither a push nor a pop. The bytes of all twelve are checked against what the assembler produces for the same lines.

- `fldt` and `fstpt`, described, encoded and written, which is the third of the seven things tamnd/rucc#540 needs and is the whole of how an eighty bit float gets to the only unit on this machine that can do arithmetic on it and back again. There is no register to register form and there is nothing to add here later, because the x87 has no instruction that names two of its registers by number: it names them by depth. So a `long double` is in memory whenever it is not being operated on, and these two are the pair that moves it.

- They are the first instructions this target describes that name no register the allocator picked. One end of the move is the addressing mode, whose registers the builder puts in the operand vector the way it does for every other instruction that carries an address, and the other end is the top of a stack nothing allocates from, which has no operand because it is not somewhere a value may be told to live. What the allocator sees is an instruction that reads an address and has an effect, which is what a store looks like to it, and that is the whole of what it has to know.

- Neither of them looks at the value it moves. A `fld` of a single or a double converts, and converting is where a signalling NaN is quieted and where a format that is not a number raises, but at eighty bits there is nothing to convert, so the load is the bits and the store is the bits back. That is why a copy of a `long double` is one of each of these rather than something that has to know what was in it. The bytes are checked against what the assembler produces for the same lines, including the two registers an address is written around and the one that needs a REX byte, which is the only byte an x87 instruction has that says anything about a register at all.

- The stack discipline written down in `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.8, which needed one word more care than the sentence it replaces. The x87 stack is empty on both sides of a group rather than of every instruction in one: a group is the loads, the operation and the stores that take the answers off again, and inside it the stack is as deep as the group has put values on it. What makes that safe is that nothing else on this machine touches the x87 stack, so a spill or a copy or a jump landing in the middle of a group cannot disturb it. The obligation that leaves is the pairing, which is a property of the code generator that writes the group rather than of any instruction in it.

- `select`, which is the opcode `spec/optimizer/22-phiopt-and-if-conversion.md` section 22.2 asks for and the shape control flow turns into when it stops being control flow. `select c, a, b` is `a` when `c` is one, both arms are evaluated, and whatever produces one owes the argument that evaluating the arm not chosen is harmless. It is an opcode rather than a pattern because it is the form the rule set gets written against: `select(c, a, a) -> a` and `select(c, 1, 0) -> zext(c)` are ordinary rules once the shape has a name, which is the factoring section 22.2 says is available to rucc and not to GCC.

- x86-64 lowering for it, at all four integer widths, as a test of the condition byte and a conditional move. One instruction rather than two for the same reason a comparison and the byte it sets are one: what passes between the halves is the flags, and the flags are not something a rule can name. The destination is the arm taken when the condition does not hold, because that is what a conditional move overwrites, so it carries the same two-address constraint the arithmetic does. The eight bit form moves thirty two bits, since the machine has no conditional move narrower than sixteen and the bits above an eight bit value are not part of it, which is the trade the eight bit multiply already makes. All four rules discharged by the solver, and the bytes checked against what the assembler produces for the same lines.

- If conversion, which is `spec/optimizer/22-phiopt-and-if-conversion.md` section 22.2's shape matcher and the `match_simplify_replacement` transformation it lists first. The pass is `phiopt` and it runs once at every level above -O0. A branch whose two arms only work out a value becomes the value and no branch: the work in the arms moves up into the block that branched, each parameter the two sides disagree about becomes a `select` on the condition, and the arms are deleted. Diamonds and triangles both match, and a triangle matches on either side, so `a < b ? a : b` and `x < 15 ? 15 : x` and `x > 0` come out as one block with a conditional move in it.

- The safety argument for moving an arm's work into the block above it, which is one sentence and worth writing down because it is what the pass rests on. An arm has exactly one predecessor, so the arm runs on every path through that predecessor that the condition sends its way and on no other path at all, and hoisting only makes it run on the other arm's path too. That is why the refusals are about what the work does rather than about where it goes: an instruction with effects cannot run twice, and a division can trap on inputs the branch was keeping away from it. The join's predecessors are not checked and do not need to be, since a third edge into the join keeps its parameters and keeps feeding them.

- No loop analysis in it, unlike `thread`, and the reason is structural rather than lucky. No edge is added, so no loop gains a second way in. An arm cannot be a loop header, because a header has a back edge and an arm has one predecessor and is not itself. An arm can be a latch, and then the block above becomes the latch, which preserves document 07.3's single latch property rather than breaking it.
