- `rucc_safe_rt::report`, which turns a refused judgement into something a person can read. The judgement in document 04's numbering with that document's own sentence beside it, the width and the address of the access, and what the lifetime plane says about that address, which is what turns a report about an address into a report about a use after free. Section 6.5 of `spec/safe-memory/06-instrumentation.md` asks for six things and this is three of them: the source location needs the program counter that nothing fills in yet, the allocation and deallocation sites need the instance header that nothing writes yet, and the type needs a plane that is milestone S5. All three are named in the module rather than left to be noticed missing.

- The text is built in a fixed buffer on the stack and goes out in one `write`. There is no allocator here that a failing program can be trusted with, since the failing program's own allocator is the thing being reported on, and a report that arrived in pieces could be interleaved with another thread's, which turns one readable report into two unreadable ones.

- `rucc_safe_rt::check`, the three functions those calls land in. Each one reads the lifetime plane at granule precision and says nothing at all about an address outside the heap the runtime reserved, since a pointer to a string literal or to a stack object is not something these planes describe yet. The bounds check refuses an access that starts inside one instance and ends outside it, the liveness check refuses a read through a pointer to an instance that has been freed, and the derivation check refuses a pointer computed off its own object, allowing one past the end because C does. A sub-granule overflow that stays inside an instance's rounded up block is not caught, which is what the sixteen byte granule costs and what the capability in S2 fixes.

- A version in the lifetime plane is even, and a range that has been freed holds its old version with the low bit set. Without that, a slot holding a version says nothing about whether anybody owns the range, because a freed range carries a version too and the only way to tell them apart is to hold the capability and compare. That is fine once capabilities exist, and until then a check handed only an address has no question it can ask. It costs one bit of the sixty four, which takes the wraparound argument from five hundred and eighty four years down to two hundred and ninety two at a billion allocations a second, and it buys a plane that can be read on its own.

## 0.5.1

### Added

- `rucc_opt::Predictions`, which is the ten static branch predictors of section 11.2 of `spec/optimizer/11-profile-and-frequency.md`, in that section's order, first match. GCC has fifty five in `gcc/predict.def`; ten of those are for Fortran and a long tail of the rest sit below sixty five percent, and a predictor at fifty nine moves a probability nine points off even, which changes no decision downstream. The ten kept are `__builtin_expect`, the arm that does not come back, the arm that calls a `cold` function, the loop exit, the loop guard, the pointer compared against null, the arm that returns a negative number, the arm that returns null, the arm that calls something, and the `continue`. Their hit rates are Ball and Larus's and Wu and Larus's measurements from the 1990s, and they are in `rucc_cost::heuristics` with the document that argued for each, the way section 40.12 requires.

- First match rather than a blend, which is what GCC uses by default and is the part of this worth getting right, because the order only does anything where two predictors disagree. `__builtin_expect` is asked first because a user who wrote it meant it, and the `cold` attribute is next because section 11.2 says the attribute is the user's explicit statement and is honoured rather than blended. The test that pins this is a branch whose named arm is also the arm that aborts: the user wins, and without first match the noreturn predictor would answer it the other way round.

- The noreturn predictor reads the IR before it reads any attribute. The front end emits `unreachable` after a call to a `noreturn` function, so a block from which no `return` is reachable is a block control does not come back from, and that is a walk backwards from the returns rather than a call graph. This is the predictor that makes error handling cold, and error handling is most of what a C program branches on. The callee's own attributes are the second source and they are per function rather than at the call site, so a caller holding the module passes `rucc_opt::Callees` and one holding a single function passes `Callees::nothing` and keeps the first answer.

- Every probability out of the predictors is `Quality::Guessed` and says so, with one exception: a block with one way out takes it, and that is `Quality::Precise` because it is not a guess. A function with no branches therefore gets precise frequencies out of the arithmetic rather than out of a special case. The probabilities on the edges out of a block sum to exactly one, which is what the frequency computation of section 11.3 needs, and a switch arm reached by two labels gets twice the share of one reached by a single label.

- `rucc_opt::Frequency`, `rucc_opt::Probability` and the `rucc_opt::Quality` field they both carry, which is section 11.1 of `spec/optimizer/11-profile-and-frequency.md`. A probability or a frequency is a number plus how much it is worth believing, arithmetic on either degrades the quality to the worse of its two inputs, and there is no constructor that takes a number without one. That is GCC's `enum profile_quality` and it is the thing to copy: without it, one guess laundered through three arithmetic operations comes out indistinguishable from measured data, and the inliner then makes an aggressive decision on a fabricated number. Both types are scaled integers rather than floats, because a frequency that differs in the last bit between two hosts is a reproducibility failure rather than a rounding question, and every operation saturates in the type rather than at the call sites, because nested loops multiply and a saturation each caller is responsible for is one a caller forgets.

- `is_hot_in_function` and `is_hot_in_program` are two predicates rather than one, per section 11.4. Hot compared with the rest of this function and hot compared with the whole program are different questions, the register allocator wants the first and section placement wants the second, and the second is unanswerable without whole program profile data. So it returns `Hotness::Unknown` today and will until that data exists, which is document 35 and after M4. It is there now so that callers are written against three answers from the start, since a boolean that quietly means "hot, or we have no idea" is how cold code ends up in the hot section.

- `rucc_opt::Frequencies`, which is how often each block runs with the entry at one, per section 11.3. Wu and Larus's method: take the loops from the inside out, work out how likely each one is to go round again, and the header runs one over one minus that many times for every entry to it, which is the sum of the geometric series. That is one walk of the loop forest and one reverse-postorder walk of the function, and it replaces solving a linear system by iterating it until it settles. The frequencies keep the predictions they came from, because every consumer wants both and the two have to be the same pair of numbers for the check below to be checking anything.

- A loop whose exit no predictor recognised gets `MAX_PREDICTED_ITERATIONS`, which is GCC's `max-predicted-iterations` and is a hundred. One over zero is not a frequency, and a cyclic probability a hair under certainty converges on a number no machine will run, so the cap is inside `Frequency::repeated_while` where no caller can skip it. A header the cap decided for is recorded as such, because it is the one place in a function where what arrives at a block deliberately does not add up to the block. A block in an irreducible region has no header and so no series to sum, and it gets the acyclic answer marked as not meaning anything, which spreads forward to everything computed from it. GCC does the same thing here and document 06.4 declines to transform these anyway.

- `Frequencies::problems` is the check section 11.5 asks the verifier for: after every pass, the probabilities out of a block sum to one and the frequencies arriving at a block sum to the block's own. The second is exact in real arithmetic even at a loop header, where the entry edge and the back edge add up to the header precisely because the series says they do, so the one percent tolerance is there for what fixed point division throws away and for nothing else. What it catches is the pass that split a block and did not split its count, which section 11.6 says is the failure that costs the most and shows up the least.

- The analysis cache holds the frequencies, so a pass asks for them the way it asks for the loop forest and they go when the forest does. A function pass is given one function rather than the module around it, so it gets `Callees::nothing` and the predictors that would like to know what a callee does fall back to what the IR says, which for the one that matters is enough.

- `rucc_opt::Liveness`, which is what is live at the edges of every block. A backward fixpoint over the postorder, which settles in one round for a function with no loops and one more for a function with them. The IR is in SSA with block parameters rather than phi nodes, so the case every liveness implementation over phi nodes has to special case does not arise here: the argument travels on the branch, the branch is an instruction in the predecessor, and the ordinary rule that an instruction uses its operands already says the right thing. A branch argument is therefore live at the branch and dead on the edge, and `Liveness::through` is where a caller sees the difference.

- `rucc_opt::Pressure`, which is that count split by register class, and is section 40.6 of `spec/optimizer/40-cost-models.md`. One function computed once, with four consumers: loop invariant motion and global code motion asking whether a loop is already too tight to hoist into, the scheduler picking between instructions of equal critical path length, the spill phase, which is the consumer that defines the quantity, and if conversion, which merges two arms' live ranges into one block. Four passes each computing their own liveness would be four chances for two of them to decide opposite things off different counts of the same quantity.

- In SSA the number of values live at a point is the number of registers the program needs there, exactly rather than approximately, which is document 39.5's chordality result and is what makes this worth computing rather than estimating. Two classes, integer and floating point, with vectors in the second because on x86-64 the same bank holds both. Values of type `mem` are the dependence chain and no register holds one, so the pressure model drops them while the liveness keeps them, since a pass asking what a store depends on wants them.

- How many registers there are is the target's and is not in this crate, so `Pressure::is_tight` takes the allocatable count from the caller and compares against it less `LOOP_RESERVED_REGS`. That is GCC's `ira-loop-reserved-regs` and it is two: a hoist that takes the pressure inside a loop up to the register count has bought nothing, because the value it hoisted is now live across the whole loop and something else gets spilled to make room for it, inside the loop, where the computation it replaced might not have been.

- `Func::insts_backwards`, which is the order a liveness walk needs. The layout links are private and collecting a block into a vector to reverse it is an allocation per block per round of a fixpoint.

- `rucc_opt::Purity` and the classifier behind it, which is section 41.3 of `spec/optimizer/41-correctness.md`. Five answers rather than a boolean, because whether a call reads memory and whether it comes back are separate questions and the pair of them is what GCC keeps in `ECF_CONST`, `ECF_PURE` and `ECF_LOOPING_CONST_OR_PURE`. A function whose result depends only on its arguments and which never returns is const and is still not deletable, and a classifier with one bit cannot say that. `purity::Facts::purity_of` matches on `purity::Callee` with no wildcard arm, so adding a kind of callee to the IR is a compile error here until somebody says what it can do.

- The default is `Purity::Opaque` and there is no `Unknown` next to it. Opaque permits everything, so a name the classifier has never been taught about costs a missed optimization rather than a wrong program, and every source of information can only ever strengthen the answer. An indirect call, a target intrinsic and inline assembly are each opaque for their own reason and each says which in the arm that returns it.

- What the user declared and what the compiler worked out are separate fields, combined with `Purity::stronger` where they are read and never written over each other. A person writing `__attribute__((const))` is asserting something and the assertion is honoured, the way GCC honours it, and document 34's analysis will later produce its own answer for the functions it can see. Keeping the two apart is what makes it possible to report the function whose attribute turned out to be a lie, rather than silently miscompiling it.

- A small table of C library names that are const or pure, which is sixteen entries out of a library of hundreds. Nothing that writes memory, sets `errno`, touches a stream or allocates is in it, which rules out all of `<math.h>`, since a math function sets `errno` unless the command line says otherwise. A name the module defines itself is not the library's whatever it is spelled, `__builtin_strlen` reaches the same entry as `strlen`, and `-fno-builtin` and `-fno-builtin-<name>` take the table or one entry of it away.

- `-fpass-fuel-global=<n>` stops the whole pipeline after n transformations, across every pass, which is the third item in section 4.5 of `spec/optimizer/04-pass-manager.md`. It is the search that comes first: halving it says which pass holds the bad rewrite, and halving `-fpass-fuel` for that pass says which rewrite it is. Two searches of twenty compilations each beat one search over a space nobody knows the shape of, which is why GCC's `-fdbg-cnt=` is the tool GCC developers actually reach for. Where both limits are given, a pass stops at whichever is tighter. A pass past the end of the budget still runs and still reports, it just transforms nothing, because a bisection that skipped passes would be searching a different pipeline at every step. `--print-pipeline` says when there is a budget, since a run that stops short is not doing what the level says it does.

- `cargo xtask bisect` does the halving, which is the second item in that section. It runs a command somebody else wrote, once per step, with `RUCC_FUEL` set to the flag for that step, and reads the exit status: zero is a program that behaved and anything else is one that did not. It checks both ends before it starts, because a search whose ends do not disagree is a search over nothing, and it finds its own upper bound by doubling rather than asking for a number to guess. Without `--pass` the search is over the whole pipeline and the answer is which transformation out of all of them is wrong. With it the search is inside one pass and the answer is which of that pass's rewrites it is. It compiles nothing itself on purpose: finding a miscompilation means building a program, running it and comparing what it printed, all of which is specific to the program, and a driver that tried to do that would be a build system that could not express whatever the next bug needs.

- `cargo xtask corpus` runs the C corpus in [tamnd/rucc-corpus](https://github.com/tamnd/rucc-corpus) against the compiler this tree builds, with GCC 16 alongside as the reference for sizes and times. The corpus is a thousand programs each written for one named transformation, and the answer each one should print was computed in Rust by the generator rather than taken from another compiler, so it says whether an optimization was correct without asking GCC anything. The commit of it that counts is pinned in `xtask/corpus.toml` rather than vendored, which is what section 42.3 of `spec/optimizer/42-measurement.md` asks for: a corpus that drifts makes historical numbers meaningless, so two rucc commits measured this way measured the same programs. Moving the pin is a commit of its own. On a machine with no GCC 16 the run still happens and still checks every answer, and it says out loud that the sizes and times have nothing to compare against.

- `-fsafety=off|detect|enforce|kernel`, which is the flag that turns the memory safety monitor on. The tier is one flag rather than a plane at a time because the tiers of `spec/safe-memory/02-threat-model.md` are the product, and it is off unless it was asked for, so a build that does not name it is compiled by the pipeline it was compiled by before any of this existed. `--print-config` reports it.

- The check insertion pass now runs, between the walk that builds the IR and the optimizer, which is where section 15.3 of `spec/safe-memory/15-integration.md` puts it. That position is the design in one line: the checks go in while the addresses the program computes still exist, and the optimizer discharges the ones it can prove. Every sanitizer that came before instruments after the optimizer so that its checks cannot be deleted, and pays for all of them forever. The verifier runs again after the insertion, so a pass that produced IR nothing else accepts says so there rather than in the assembly it turned into.

- The three tiers that are not `off` insert the same checks so far, and a test pins that they agree. What separates them is the reporter and the boundary, which are milestones S2 and S3, so the day they stop agreeing that test says so.

- `malloc`, `free`, `calloc` and `realloc` in the safety runtime, over the arena and the lifetime plane, which is section 10.4 of `spec/safe-memory/10-boundaries.md`. The milestone asks for the first two, and the other two come with them because the family cannot be split: interpose half of it and the C library's `calloc` hands back a pointer our `free` has never seen, which is a J6 report against a program that did nothing wrong, and our `malloc` hands a pointer to the C library's `realloc`, which is the monitor corrupting the heap. A resize is always a copy and never a resize in place, because growing an instance in place would have one version cover granules that were somebody else's a moment ago, and shrinking one would leave a live capability naming bytes the program has given back. A resize that cannot get memory leaves the old instance live, which is what the standard requires and what stops an allocation failure turning into a use after free.

- The four wrappers are compiled on Unix only, and not because of `mmap`, which has an answer everywhere. Replacing `malloc` by defining one is a fact about how ELF and Mach-O resolve a symbol at load time, and it is not how Windows works: there the C runtime's heap is reached through an import table, and taking it over means patching that table or shipping a replacement runtime. That is a different design rather than a different constant, so it gets its own decision. Everything under the wrappers, which is the planes, the layout and the arena, is portable and is compiled everywhere.

- The heap is one reservation of a gibibyte made at the first call, holding the shadow and then the region as a single mapping so that the bias between them is fixed by construction rather than by whichever pair of addresses the kernel happened to return. It is a fixed size because growing the region would move the bias every capability was built on. A list of regions instead is milestone S2, and so is the reporter: a refused free stops the program today rather than saying what happened, and everything the C library allocates through a name other than those four is still S3, so a program that frees one of those results gets a refusal it did not earn.

- A request for nothing gets an instance of one granule. A payload of no bytes rounds to no granules, and no granules is not a size class: it has no version of its own, so it would share the next instance's, and no word to hold a free list link. C also lets `malloc(0)` hand back an address, and an address a program may pass to `free` has to be an instance like any other.

### Changed

- The pass manager verifies the function a pass changed rather than the module it was in, which is section 41.4 of `spec/optimizer/41-correctness.md`. A pass here is a function pass, so the only thing it can have broken is the function it was given, and walking the other ones again after every one of them is the quadratic walk `rucc_ir::verify_func` exists to avoid. It is also what lets the complaint name the function, which the module walk could not, and it puts the failure next to the pass that caused it rather than at the end of the module.

- The other three things section 41.4 asks for are already true here and are now written down where somebody would go looking for them. GCC verifies what the IR currently is by consulting `curr_properties`, because its IR passes through GENERIC, GIMPLE with and without a CFG, GIMPLE in SSA and RTL. rucc has one IR, it is in SSA from the moment the lowering walk builds it, and it always has a CFG, so the applicable set never varies and a bitmask saying so would have nothing to say. GCC guards the verifiers with `!seen_error()`, because after a user error the IR is legitimately malformed and an internal error raised over it hides the real diagnostic. Here the optimizer is not reached at all after a parse, check or lowering error, which is the same guard one level up where it cannot be forgotten. And GCC asserts that a verifier did not change the dominator state, where a verifier here takes the module by shared reference, so that is a type error rather than an assertion.

## 0.5.0

### Added

- The dominance frontier and the control dependence relation, `rucc_opt::Frontiers` and `rucc_opt::ControlDependence`, which are section 6.3 of `spec/optimizer/06-cfg-dominance.md`. The frontier of a block is where its influence ends: the blocks it does not dominate but reaches by one edge out of something it does. The same definition on the reversed graph is the relation a pass means when it asks whether a block runs because of a branch, so both are one algorithm walked over two trees. Each is an analysis the manager holds, the frontier resting on the dominator tree and the relation on the post-dominator tree, and each falls when the tree under it does whatever the pass claimed.

- Cytron's iterated frontier is deliberately not here. It exists to place phi nodes, rucc builds SSA during lowering, and code with no caller is code nothing keeps honest. What the relation is for is aggressive dead code elimination, which needs it to know that a branch is live because something it controls is live, and if-conversion, which needs it to know which blocks a branch is the only reason for.

- The invented exit edges the post-dominator tree adds for a loop with no exit stay in the relation rather than being filtered back out, so a block inside an infinite loop comes out control dependent on the test that keeps it there. That is the safe direction. A pass that keeps something it did not have to is slow, and a pass that deletes an infinite loop is wrong.

- Both relations are checked against a direct reading of the definition rather than against themselves: on ten named shapes covering branches, joins, loops, switches, an irreducible graph, a back edge to the entry, an unreachable block and one and two infinite loops, and on a thousand random graphs each.

- Milestone S0 of `spec/safe-memory/16-milestones.md` is complete, which is issue #426. The IR extension the memory safety monitor is built on now exists in full, and it still checks nothing, which was the plan: an IR mistake found four milestones later costs a rewrite of every pass above it.

- `runtime/rucc-safe-rt` is the second package, beside `rucc-builtins`, outside the layer stack, `#![no_std]` and compiled for the target rather than the host. What is in it is the one part of the runtime the backend has to agree with. A failed check branches to a call of `__rucc_safety_fail` with a single number, and that number indexes a descriptor in a `.rucc_safety_desc` section holding the judgement, the class, the size of the access and the address of the failing branch. Everything else the report needs is looked up rather than passed, which is what keeps the per-check code to a compare and a branch and lets the cold path be arbitrarily detailed.

- The signature does not promise never to return, even though the only posture implemented today always aborts. `-fsafety-on-error=continue` has to come back, since that is what a corpus run needs so one bug does not hide a hundred, and the signature is ABI, so committing to the narrower form now would mean breaking it later. The source location is not in the descriptor either: it comes from the DWARF already emitted, because a compiler that ships line tables twice is a compiler whose two copies eventually disagree.

- The forms the IR is not allowed to express are written down in `spec/safe-memory/06-instrumentation.md` section 6.6 rather than living only in the verifier's source. Thirty of them in five tables, twenty three rejected by the verifier and seven by the parser, each row saying what the form is, why it is not allowed, and which test pins it. Writing the list found two rows with nothing checking them, which is roughly the argument for writing it.

- `cargo xtask malformed` keeps the list true. It reads the section, pulls out every name that looks like a test, and fails on one that is not in `verify.rs` or `parse.rs`, and the per-commit job runs it beside the layer rule and the prose check. The failure it exists to stop is a test getting renamed while the row keeps the old name, which leaves a document describing a compiler we used to have, and that is worse than no document because a reader would believe it.

### Changed

- The pass manager verifies the function a pass changed rather than the module it was in, which is section 41.4 of `spec/optimizer/41-correctness.md`. A pass here is a function pass, so the only thing it can have broken is the function it was given, and walking the other ones again after every one of them is the quadratic walk `rucc_ir::verify_func` exists to avoid. It is also what lets the complaint name the function, which the module walk could not, and it puts the failure next to the pass that caused it rather than at the end of the module.

- The other three things section 41.4 asks for are already true here and are now written down where somebody would go looking for them. GCC verifies what the IR currently is by consulting `curr_properties`, because its IR passes through GENERIC, GIMPLE with and without a CFG, GIMPLE in SSA and RTL. rucc has one IR, it is in SSA from the moment the lowering walk builds it, and it always has a CFG, so the applicable set never varies and a bitmask saying so would have nothing to say. GCC guards the verifiers with `!seen_error()`, because after a user error the IR is legitimately malformed and an internal error raised over it hides the real diagnostic. Here the optimizer is not reached at all after a parse, check or lowering error, which is the same guard one level up where it cannot be forgotten. And GCC asserts that a verifier did not change the dominator state, where a verifier here takes the module by shared reference, so that is a type error rather than an assertion.

## 0.4.3

### Fixed

- A call in a branch a constant condition never takes no longer reaches the linker, at every optimization level including `-O0`. This is issue #359, which is `execute/medce-1.c` out of the gcc torture suite: a program that guards a call to a function it does not link with `if (0)` failed to link. The new `simplify-cfg` pass turns a branch whose condition is known into a jump and removes the blocks that leaves stranded, and it is in the `-O0` pipeline because a link failure is not a missed optimization. The condition it reads is either a constant or a comparison of two constants, and it reads the comparison rather than folding it, because a one-bit integer standing on its own has no lowering and that is issue #352. The comparison is left for `dce`, which is where it goes anyway once nothing reads it.

- Arithmetic on a bit-field wider than an `int` happens at the field's own width, so `x.b << 32` on a `unsigned long long b : 40` is zero rather than a value with a bit above the fortieth. This is issue #360 and issue #328, and it takes `bitfld-3.c`, `pr32244-1.c`, `pr34971.c` and `991118-1.c` off the exclusion list in `tamnd/rucc-compat`. C17 says such a field promotes to `unsigned int`, which drops eight of the bits, and C23 says it keeps its declared type, which adds twenty four. Neither is what gcc 16.2.0 or clang does: both give the field a type whose precision is the width. That type is `_BitInt(40)` here, which is what makes the usual arithmetic conversions rank a 33-bit field below a 41-bit one and wrap their product at forty one bits without anything downstream knowing where either operand came from.

- The back end puts an integer of a width the machine has no register for into the narrowest one it has, in a new pass that runs before every other back end pass. The spare bits hold nothing, so the instructions that read them get their operands shaped first and the ones whose low bits are the whole answer are left alone. A width that crosses the function's own boundary is still refused by name, because the psABI extends such an argument according to a signedness the IR does not carry, and that is issue #425.

- A static bit-field initializer whose first byte is zero is written rather than dropped, so `struct s { unsigned f : 20; } x = { 0x12300 };` is `0x12300` and not zero. The bytes a run of bit-fields lands in are put together first and taken back out as the run they make, and the run was only recorded from the first byte that had a bit set in it, so the run started in the wrong place and no entry claimed it. This was there for every width and is not new with the widths above.

### Added

- The analysis manager, `rucc_opt::Analyses`, which is section 4.3 of `spec/optimizer/04-pass-manager.md` and the half of the pass manager that was missing. It computes an analysis the first time a pass asks for one, caches it per function across the passes of a pipeline, and throws out what a pass broke. The four it holds are the CFG, the dominator tree, the post-dominator tree and the loop forest, which are the ones that own their data and are worth keeping. Alias, memory SSA, scalar evolution and the ranges borrow the function and are built where they are used, so caching them would be a lifetime fight with nothing on the other side of it.

- What gets thrown out comes from the pass rather than from a table kept next to it. Every pass answers `preserves`, there is no default on purpose, and the safe answer costs a recomputation rather than a wrong answer. Invalidation is transitive, so a pass that broke the CFG loses the dominator tree and the loop forest with it whatever it said about those two, and it is worked out in one walk over the four because each analysis only rests on ones that come before it. Nothing is thrown out after a pass that reported it changed nothing, which is what keeps the declaration a property of the pass and not of the run.

- A pass that says it preserved an analysis it broke is caught under `--verify`, by recomputing the analysis and comparing it against the cached one. The pipeline reports it the same way it reports a verifier failure, naming the pass, the analysis and the function. The check is off by default and costs nothing when it is off, because the recomputation is the expensive half of the analysis and it does not happen.

- `simplify-cfg`, the fifth pass. A branch whose condition is known one way is rewritten to a jump, and the blocks nothing reaches after that are removed. Reachability is the verifier's own rule, including the edge from a block holding a `block_addr` to the block it names, so the pass and the verifier cannot disagree in either direction. Fuel is charged per branch, not per block removed: a cut between folding the branch and removing what it stranded would leave a block nothing reaches, and the verifier refuses that, so one unit of fuel buys one whole change.

- `abs`, `labs` and `llabs` are the magnitude of an integer rather than a call, whoever declared the name. This is the first of the family `spec/13-gnu-compat.md` section 13.5 calls the library calls with known semantics, and it is issue #360. The names are reserved to the implementation by C23 7.1.3, so a program that writes one means the function the C library promises and a compiler that knows what that one does may write the arithmetic instead of the call. `gcc.c-torture/execute/20021127-1.c` is the program that insists on it: it defines `llabs` to abort and expects never to reach the definition.

- What is written is the sign of the value spread over every bit, an exclusive or with that, and a subtraction of it, which is four instructions with no branch and no condition code. The most negative value comes back as itself, because that is what the arithmetic gives and its magnitude is not representable, which is where C says the result is undefined and where gcc's own `neg` and `cmovns` land as well. Nothing waits for a constant argument, because the point is not that `llabs(-1)` is one, it is that the call does not happen.

- The plain name is the library's only where nothing else has taken it, so the declaration is looked at as well as the name. It has to be a function rather than a pointer some object holds, it has to have external linkage, and its type has to be the one the library gives that name, spelled with a prototype. A `static long long llabs(long long)` is a program meaning its own function, and gcc 16.2.0 calls it, measured rather than assumed.

- `-fno-builtin` and `-fno-builtin-<name>` are read, which is the same question asked from the command line, and `-ffreestanding` answers it too, because a freestanding program has no C library for the name to be the name of. The `__builtin_` spellings go on meaning the library's function through all of it, which is what the prefix is for and what lets a freestanding build reach one deliberately.

- The fourth analysis is scalar evolution, `rucc_opt::Scev`, which is the second half of document 07 and the rest of the four questions in section 7.6. It says how a value changes across the iterations of a loop and how many iterations there are. A value comes back invariant, or as the affine chain of recurrences `{base, +, step}` with both parts invariant in the loop, or unknown, and the operations that stay inside that are addition, subtraction, multiplication by something invariant, shifting by a constant, and extension. What it leaves out of GCC's four thousand lines is the polynomial and mutually recursive part, which serves Fortran and the polyhedral framework rather than any induction variable a C programmer writes. It is demand driven and memoized, because the cost is a function of how many values get asked about rather than of the size of the function.

- A `ptr_add` is treated as an addition with the byte offset as its step, which is the one deliberate extension past affine and is what makes `for (p = a; p != end; p++)` as analysable as `for (i = 0; i < n; i++)`. The base and the step are kept as `scale * value + offset` rather than as a value the function computes, which is what lets `j = 2 * i + 3` come out as `{3, +, 2}`: neither part of that chrec is anything the IR names, and a representation that could only point at existing values would have to answer unknown. Extension is allowed only where the increment carried the matching `nsw` or `nuw`, because `{0, +, 1}` in `unsigned char` is not the sequence zero, one, two, it is that sequence modulo two hundred and fifty six, and widening one that wraps produces numbers the loop never held.

- A trip count comes back as a `Bound`, and there is no way to read the number out of one without seeing what it rests on. `parts` hands back both and `proven` hands back the count only when there is nothing left to prove, so a pass that meant to emit a runtime check and forgot cannot get at the count instead. The assumptions are that the counter starts on the near side of its limit, that it does not wrap before it arrives, and that signed overflow is undefined, which is the one `-fwrapv` withdraws. `Bound` and `Estimate` are different structs even though both wrap an integer: a bound is for correctness and an estimate is for deciding whether a transformation pays for itself, and section 7.5 of the design calls conflating them a category error that costs correctness. GCC keeps them apart by naming them `max_loop_iterations` and `estimate_numbers_of_iterations`, and the names do not stop anyone.

- Section 7.7 lists four ways of getting this wrong and each one has a test. A step of zero is reported as invariant rather than as a chrec, so nothing downstream divides the distance by it. An exit tested with `!=` is solved separately from one tested with an ordering, because an ordering ends the loop the moment the counter is past the limit and `!=` only ends it on the iteration where the counter is the limit, so a counter that steps over its limit or starts on the far side of it runs forever rather than zero times. Constants are held as their signed reading, so an unsigned test reads them back at its own width before working anything out, which is the difference between `for (unsigned char i = 0; i < 200; i++)` running two hundred times and appearing to run none. `crates/rucc-opt/tests/scev.rs` is where those are checked, on two thousand loops built from random widths, limits, steps and predicates, by running each one and holding the analysis to what the run did. Both of the last two were found by that test rather than by reading the code.

- The fifth analysis is alias analysis, `rucc_opt::Alias`, which is document 08 of `spec/optimizer` and the rest of issue #398. It answers the one question every memory optimization is gated on, which is whether two references can touch the same byte. The answer is not a boolean. It is `May` or a `No` carrying the layer that concluded it, which section 8.5 of the design asks for and which pays for itself three ways: a miscompilation from an alias bug is localised to one layer rather than bisected across the whole analysis, the per layer statistics come for free, and a user who asks why something was not optimized gets a sentence rather than a shrug. It costs one byte in a return value that was going in a register anyway.

- Five of the six layers section 8.2 lists are here, and the order they run in is load bearing. Two volatile accesses are treated as conflicting before anything else is asked, because that is what stops either being moved across the other. Distinct storage and provenance are one walk, since the IR names the object a pointer came from: chase back through `ptr_add` and `bitcast` to the `alloca` or the `global_addr` it started at, and two different objects never alias. Offsets run next and only for two references to one object. Escape says a local whose address never left the function is not what a pointer this function cannot follow is pointing at. `restrict` is one comparison of two small numbers. Type based aliasing runs last.

- Running offsets before the type based layer rather than after is the whole of what keeps union type punning working. Writing through one member of a union and reading another is two accesses to one object at overlapping offsets whose types are unrelated. It is undefined in ISO C, it is defined by GCC, a great deal of real C rests on it, and a layer that asked about the types first would answer no and miscompile all of it. GCC's comment at `gcc/tree-ssa-alias.cc:2461` says exactly this, and rucc reproduces the ordering on purpose rather than by accident, with a test named after the case so that a later reordering fails rather than ships.

- `-fno-strict-aliasing` is one condition in one place. Section 41.9 asks that the flag disable the type based component and nothing else, exactly as `gcc/alias.cc:420` and `:556` do, and the way to make that true rather than hoped for is to have a single `Options::strict_aliasing` read at a single site. The test for it turns the flag off on a pair that both the type layer and the `restrict` layer would disambiguate, and requires the `restrict` answer to survive.

- A memory operation now carries a `restrict` clique and a base, which is `rucc_ir::Restrict`. Two small numbers, the same pair GCC calls `MR_DEPENDENCE_CLIQUE` and `MR_DEPENDENCE_BASE` at `gcc/tree-ssa-alias.cc:2503`, and one rule: same clique and different base means the two accesses cannot touch the same byte. A clique is one scope, numbered as lowering enters it, a base is one `restrict` pointer declared inside it, and clique zero means nothing is known. They fit in padding `MemInfo` already had. The trap is written down beside them, which is PR71062: `restrict` constrains what is read and written through a pointer and says nothing about the pointer's value, so two pointers that may not be used to reach the same object can still compare equal, and folding `p == q` to false on the strength of a no from this analysis is wrong.

- The escape analysis is a whitelist and the reason is in the comment above it. An opcode it does not name lets the address out, which means an opcode added to the IR after it was written escapes by default. Written the other way round, the next person to add an opcode introduces a miscompilation without touching the file. Comparing two addresses is on the list, because a comparison neither reads an address nor keeps one, and turning an address into an integer is not, because the integer can be turned back into a pointer anywhere including in another translation unit.

- What a call touches is answered from attributes and from escape only. A local whose address never left the function cannot be reached by any call at all. `__attribute__((const))` reads and writes nothing, `pure` writes nothing, and the attribute that says a callee touches no memory except through the pointers it was passed is used by asking about each of those pointers in turn, which is sound on the non transitive reading rucc gives it. The full mod and ref summary is `ipa-modref`, it is five and a half thousand lines, and it is document 34's rather than this one's.

- Correctness is checked by generating programs whose memory the test knows and holding the analysis to them. Four hundred functions of random accesses through `restrict` parameters and locals, where the generator keeps the promise `restrict` makes rather than merely writing the metadata down and tags every access with the type of the object it really touches, so the analysis is being told the truth in the way real C would tell it. Every pair is asked about, and a no that disagrees with what the generator knows fails the run. Symmetry, repeatability and the attribution of every no to exactly one layer are checked alongside it, and the run refuses to pass if too few pairs were disambiguated, because a suite where the analysis answered `May` to everything would satisfy every other assertion in it. Three deliberate mutations were tried against it before it was called done, one per layer, and all three failed the suite.

- Two things document 08 asks for are deliberately not here yet and neither is silent. Layer 6 is the points to solver, which is a module wide fixed point rather than a fact the IR already carries, and it lands separately with the constraint generator split from the solver the way GCC 16 split its own. `Origin` in this analysis is provenance and there is no points to type for it to be confused with, which section 8.6 lists as one of the ways this goes wrong. The other is that lowering does not yet attach type nodes or `restrict` cliques to anything, so layers 3 and 5 are correct on the accesses that carry them and idle on the accesses the front end produces today. Giving them something to read is the next piece of work, and the thing it has to be careful about is that an alias set has to come from a canonical encoding of the type and never from allocation order, or document 35's link time optimization quietly gains disambiguations that were never true.

- The IR can now carry memory as a value, which is the first half of document 09 of `spec/optimizer` and the rest of issue #398. There is a `mem` type, a `mem_entry` instruction that produces the version of memory a function was called with, and a rule that the version an instruction reads is its last operand and the version it produces is its last result. That is the entire representation. A memory phi is an ordinary parameter of a block, so the CFG updates that keep memory SSA in step with the blocks are the ones every other value already needed, and there is no side table to get out of date.

- Putting memory last among the operands and last among the results is what made this a small change rather than a wide one. Every other operand keeps the position it had, so a pass that reads the address of a load as its first operand goes on working whether or not memory has been threaded through it, and the verifier's per opcode shape check takes the memory operand and the memory result off before it runs, so not one of its arms had to change. The two accessors that know about the extra operand are `Func::mem_in` and `Func::mem_out`, and the rest of the compiler reads those or reads nothing.

- Memory is all or nothing within a function, and the verifier is what says so. Either every instruction that touches memory is on the chain or none of them is, because a walk that reaches an instruction with no memory operand cannot tell whether that instruction was left off the chain by a pass or does not belong on it. Section 9.6 gives this as the main argument for the explicit operand over a side table: a side table has no place to write down that it is incomplete, and this does. The other rules are the ones that make the chain mean something, which is that an instruction that does not touch memory is not on it, that a write is on it at both ends or at neither, that a version of memory reaches a memory operand or a block argument and nowhere else, that a function has one `mem_entry` and it is in the entry block, and that no function takes or returns memory, since memory stops at the call and what a call does to it is what the callee's attributes say.

- A function at `-O0` and `-O1` has no `mem` in it at all, which is not a degraded form. It is a function whose memory operations are ordered by asking the alias analysis directly, and `None` from `Func::mem_in` means exactly that. The back end never sees a `mem` either, because memory SSA is built at `-O2`, read by the passes that need it, and taken back off before instruction selection.

- Threading memory through an instruction makes a new instruction rather than editing the old one, which is `Func::with_mem`. The results of an instruction are values next to each other and there is no room after them, so a result cannot be added to one that already exists. Construction therefore rebuilds each memory instruction in one pass over the function, forwards the old results to the new ones, which are at the same positions, and deletes the old. This is the honest shape of the operation and it is written down rather than discovered by whoever writes the construction pass.

- `Type` now packs five kinds rather than four, so the kind takes three bits and the lane count takes thirteen. The widest vector the IR could describe before is still describable, nothing in the workspace hardcoded the old lane limit, and the on disk format version does not move because no format holds a `mem`.

- What is not here is the analysis. The clobber walk, its budget, the `translate` callback that sees through a `memcpy`, and the construction pass that places the memory block parameters are the second half of document 09 and land next. This is the representation and the rules that keep it honest, and it is separate because a verifier rule that arrives with the pass it constrains is a rule nobody has tested against a pass that breaks it.
- Memory SSA is now built and read, which is the second half of document 09 and the rest of issue #398. Construction is the iterated dominance frontier over one variable: the blocks that write memory are the definitions, the joins their versions reach get a memory parameter, and a walk of the dominator tree threads the operand through every instruction that touches memory. It declines rather than half building on a function that has no memory in it, one that is already on the chain, and one with a block nothing reaches, because half a function on the chain is worse than none of it.

- The walk back from a load to the store it sees is GCC's `walk_non_aliased_vuses`, and it comes with GCC's budget under GCC's name. `sccvn-max-alias-queries-per-access` defaults to a thousand queries per walk in both compilers, so a person who has learned to raise one does not have to learn a second name for the other. Running out gives an answer that says nothing rather than an answer that says no, and the count of walks that ran out is kept, because section 9.3 makes that number the one that decides whether the caching walker ever gets built. There is no cache today and that is the decision, not an omission.

- The answer has five cases and three of them are a clobber. A store that wrote exactly the bytes a load reads is exact and its value can be taken. One that wrote some of them is partial, and getting the value out of it is a shift and a truncate that redundant load elimination decides on rather than this. One that may have written them is neither. Section 9.5 says a walk that collapses these into a yes and a no is a class of miscompilation, so there is no `Option` in the return and no default arm to fall into.

- `translate` is the reason the walk is a way to rewrite the question rather than only a stopping condition. When it reaches a definition it cannot see past, the caller may adjust the reference and carry on, which is what lets value numbering follow a load through the `memcpy` a struct assignment lowers to. Every retry spends budget and clears what the walk had already visited, so the budget bounds the whole thing while the visited set still cuts the cycles between retries.

- Volatile is checked before the alias analysis is asked anything, and every atomic and every fence is a full definition and a full use of memory. Both are conservative on purpose. Alias analysis says nothing about how many times an access happens and volatile constrains that too, and a relaxed atomic load orders nothing and is still a load, so hoisting one out of a loop changes an observable. Doing better than this means modelling the memory model rather than the memory, which document 09 puts after 1.0.

- The walk is checked against the truth rather than against itself. Two thousand acyclic functions of random accesses to random locals are generated, every path from the entry to every load is enumerated, and the store the load sees on each path is worked out by looking at the bytes. A clobber the walk names has to be the one every path agrees on and a no has to mean no path wrote anything, while an answer that claims nothing is allowed anywhere, which is the asymmetry the whole design rests on. The same functions are all built and run through the verifier, so the ten rules the chain has are what the construction is held to on a few thousand random control flow graphs rather than on the examples somebody thought of.

- Integer value ranges, document 10 and the rest of issue #398. A range is a few disjoint intervals and the bits that are known, on one object, because section 10.2 says the two refine each other and keeping them apart is what a from-scratch implementation gets wrong: the low three bits being zero says the value is a multiple of eight, which pulls an interval in, and an interval of `[0, 15]` says the top bits are clear. There are three intervals before it collapses to the hull, which covers `x != 0`, `x != 0 && x != 1` and the exclusions a switch produces, and an unbounded count is how a range implementation becomes a memory problem.

- The intervals are over bit patterns rather than over signed numbers, which is a departure from the document and the one place rucc's IR made a different answer better. GCC's `irange` holds bounds in the domain of its tree type, which carries a signedness. An `i32` here does not, which is why there is an `icmp slt` and an `icmp ult`, so a range in the signed domain would have to guess which reading a value is going to get. In this domain the fact section 10.2 calls the most useful one in a C compiler, that a value is not zero, costs one interval rather than two. What it costs back is that a small signed range around zero is two, and both fit.

- Pointers are not in it, for the reason section 10.2 gives for GCC splitting `prange` out of `irange` in GCC 14: a pointer range is about null and about provenance, and forcing it through integer interval arithmetic produces a pointer in `[0x1000, 0x2000]` that no target promised. Floats are not in it either, which is what section 10.2 asks for in M4.

- The arithmetic is the table section 10.4 describes, at the subset it names: addition, subtraction, multiplication, the bitwise operations, the shifts, the comparisons, truncation, both extensions and negation. Not division, not remainder, not the overflow builtins, which are cheap to add later against the same tests and expensive to get subtly wrong now.

- Every operation that can overflow takes the instruction's flags, so there is no way to call one and forget. Section 10.7 names this as the way a range implementation gets a program wrong. Without the flags the answer is the wrapping one and `[100, 200] + [100, 200]` in eight bits is not `[200, 400]`. With `nsw` the sums that do not fit cannot have happened, so they are cut, and if none of them fit the range comes back empty, which is the analysis proving that code unreachable.

- The overflow promise is checked against each pairing of intervals rather than against the two hulls, and that is worth a paragraph because it is most of the precision. One and minus four added to three and minus two in three signed bits has exactly one sum that fits, and the answer is that one value. A version working on the hulls would see `[-4, 1]` and `[-2, 3]`, find a window that fits, and learn nothing.

- The comparison inverse is the entry that earns the module. On the true edge of `if (x < 10)` the useful fact is not about the comparison, it is about `x`, and turning one into the other is what makes a branch teach the analysis anything. All ten predicates have it, signed and unsigned, and `x != y` narrows only when `y` is one value, since otherwise whichever value `x` is, the other `y` is still there.

- It is checked exhaustively rather than on examples. At three bits there are a hundred and twenty seven ranges that describe a set of values exactly, and every operation is run on every pair of them against every pair of values in them. Losing a value is a wrong program and is checked always. Holding an extra one is a slow program, is allowed, and is checked to not happen where the operands were one value each, because an implementation that answered everything from every entry would pass a soundness check on its own and be worth nothing. The representation gets the same treatment at four bits, where there are ten thousand.

- What is not here is the query. The on-demand engine that walks back through branch conditions to answer what a value is at a point, the GORI-style inversion, the relational oracle keyed by block, and the bounded cache with its counters are sections 10.3 and 10.6 and are the entry below. This is the thing they are made of, and it is separate because a table verified against every pair of values is a different kind of argument from an engine verified against a program.

- The query over those ranges is `rucc_opt::Ranges`, which is sections 10.1, 10.3 and 10.6 of the same document. It answers what a value holds at a block rather than what it holds where it was defined, and the difference between those two questions is the whole reason the analysis is worth having: on the true edge of `if (x < 10)` the value of `x` is not the value of `x` in the block above, and a pass that asks the wrong one of those either misses the optimization or performs it in the wrong place. Nothing is computed until something asks, and what is computed is one value at one place rather than a table over the function, which is what section 10.1 means by on demand and what makes the cost a function of how many questions a pass asks.

- A query walks up the dominator tree from the block it was asked about towards the block the value was defined in, and takes the fact off every edge it crosses where the block it came from has one predecessor. The single predecessor rule is what makes the fact sound: an edge that is the only way in carries its condition to everything below it, and an edge that is one of several does not, whatever the branch above it said. GCC goes further and reasons about dominating edges whose siblings are unreachable, which buys real cases and needs the unreachability to be established first, so it is not here.

- The condition is carried back to the value it was computed from, which is GORI in GCC and is where most of the precision comes from. `if (x + 1 < 10)` says something about `x` and not about the sum, so the range worked out for the sum is pushed back through the addition, the subtraction, the exclusive or and the two extensions, one operand at a time, and the other operand is looked up so the inversion has something to subtract. The walk is bounded by `ranger-logical-depth`, six as it is in GCC, because the chain being inverted can be as long as the function and each step is a range operation.

- A switch edge is a fact on every one of its arms and not just on the cases. Taking the arm for case seven pins the value to seven, taking an arm two cases share pins it to either of them, and taking the default excludes them all, up to the number of intervals a range can hold before it collapses to the hull. An arm a case and the default both lead to says nothing, because the edge does not record which of the two ways control came, and answering as though it were the default there would be the kind of wrong answer that only shows up in generated code.

- Some facts are not intervals and the relational oracle is where they live. `if (a < b)` with nothing known about either one narrows neither, and the fact that `a` is below `b` is still worth keeping, because the comparison a later pass makes between them is answerable from it and from nothing else. The relations are kept per block, inherited down the dominator tree, and composed one step: two relations that share a value give a third when their directions agree, and the result is strict when either of the two is. They are not closed transitively, which section 10.3 asks for and which is what keeps a block with a hundred comparisons in it from costing ten thousand.

- The cache is bounded on purpose and gives up rather than growing. A value keeps its range at the definition and up to eight refinements at blocks that narrowed it, and past that a query walks and does not record, which turns an unbounded map into a slower answer instead of into memory nobody budgeted for. A value being computed that is asked for again is a cycle, which in SSA is a loop-carried value, and the answer there is the range of the type. That answer is sound because every operation over ranges is monotone, so one step from everything is above the fixed point, and it is deliberately not cached, because a cached answer that depended on the guard would depend on the order the queries arrived in and the same compilation would stop giving the same output.

- Six counters say what the engine did, and the one that matters is the loss count, which records the opcode every time a query comes back as the whole of a type. Precision that leaks away is invisible without it: a range analysis that has quietly stopped answering looks exactly like a range analysis whose input was genuinely unknown, and the difference is a list of opcodes with numbers beside them. There is also a count of the ranges that are only true because signed overflow is undefined, which is section 10.7's question about what is being assumed, asked of every operation that carries a flag.

- Correctness is checked by running the programs. `crates/rucc-opt/tests/ranges.rs` generates four hundred random acyclic functions of one byte wide parameter, runs each of them on all two hundred and fifty six inputs, and requires that every value the run produced is inside the range the query gives for that value at that block, that every comparison the query called settled came out that way on every input, and that every relation the oracle claimed held. The check is one directional, since a range wider than the truth is imprecision and a range narrower than it is a miscompilation, and what keeps it from passing on an analysis that gives up everywhere is a count of how often the walk through the branches narrowed something, which has to happen on a hundredth of the queries or the test fails.

- What is not here is widening. A value carried round a loop comes back as one step of its recurrence applied to the whole of its type, which is sound and is looser than the interval a fixed point would find, and there is no widening operator to close the gap. Section 10.7 says the honest fix is scalar evolution, which already knows the shape of a loop-carried value and does not have to guess at one, and that is the direction this goes rather than the direction GCC took.
- What is not here is the query. The on-demand engine that walks back through branch conditions to answer what a value is at a point, the GORI-style inversion, the relational oracle keyed by block, and the bounded cache with its counters are sections 10.3 and 10.6 and land next. This is the thing they are made of, and it is separate because a table verified against every pair of values is a different kind of argument from an engine verified against a program.

- The IR grew a value type and nineteen instructions for the memory safety monitor, which is milestone S0 of `spec/safe-memory/16-milestones.md` and issue #426. None of it checks anything yet, and that is the point: an IR mistake found four milestones later costs a rewrite of every pass above it, so the extension goes in first and on its own.

- `cap` is the type of a capability, the four words that say what a pointer is allowed to reach. It cannot appear in a function's parameters or results, because a capability does not cross a call: the other side recovers one with `cap_recover`, and a type that could be passed would make that a choice somebody could forget to take. The verifier reports either spelling.

- The instructions are six that make and move capabilities, six that ask a question about one, five that write the metadata planes, and two that mark a region the monitor is told not to judge. They are spelled with underscores rather than dots because a dot after an opcode is the type suffix in the textual IR, so `cap.of` would read back as an opcode `cap` with a suffix `of`, which is not a type.

- Four facts about a value came with them: `!bounds`, `!live`, `!init` and `!aligned`. A check is code and costs something, a fact is a thing the optimizer may assume and costs nothing, and keeping them apart is what makes check elimination a dataflow problem rather than a pass with its own opinions. They live in a side table on the function rather than in the value, so that a function compiled without `-fsafety` prints byte for byte what it printed before any of this existed, and they print in a `facts:` block at the end of the body so that a block parameter and an instruction result are spelled the same way.

- `!bounds` names two values and not two numbers, since the range of a heap allocation is not known until it is made, and the verifier requires both of them to reach the value the fact is about. A range named by something the program computes after the pointer is a range that half the pointer's uses could not name.

- Metadata gained a second node kind, the plane entry, in the same table and the same numbering as the aliasing nodes. `rucc-opt` is rank 9 and cannot see `rucc-types`, so a type plane fact has to travel as an opaque interned id in module metadata, which is exactly what an aliasing node already is, and a second table would have meant two of everything for no gain. The cost is that a reference no longer says which kind it is, so the verifier pins it: `check_type` and `meta_type` name a plane entry, everything else names an aliasing node.

- Every new instruction has a verifier rule, and the forms those rules reject are written down in `spec/safe-memory/06-instrumentation.md` section 6.6 rather than living only in the verifier's source. Two of them are worth the section on their own. A check handed the same value as both its capability and its pointer would pass, always, because a pointer is inside its own bounds however wrong those bounds are, and it would look exactly like a check that worked.
## 0.4.2

- `crates/rucc-safety` exists, at rank 9 beside `rucc-lower` and `rucc-opt` and therefore able to depend on neither. That is the constraint rather than an inconvenience: the pass reads IR and writes IR, it never sees the AST, and the driver at rank 12 is what sequences it between the two. What it does so far is one walk that puts a `cap_of` and a `check_bounds` in front of every `load` and `store`, discharging nothing, because discharging is the optimizer's job and this pass staying dumb is what makes the split reviewable. It hands back how many accesses it checked, which is the denominator of every measurement the specification asks for later and is not recoverable once the optimizer has removed the ones it could prove.

### Added

- There is a cost model, in a new crate `rucc-cost`, which is document 40 of `spec/optimizer/` made real. It answers what an operation costs on a target, and it is one place rather than thirty because a compiler where each pass has its own idea of what a multiply costs is a compiler where two passes undo each other and neither of them is wrong. Nothing consults it yet. It is landing before the passes that need it because the alternative order is landing the passes first and then finding all the numbers, and the numbers are exactly what is hard to find.

- Time and space are separate types that do not convert. `Cycles` is fixed point with two decimal places, so a table can say that an operation costs 1.33 of another without anybody rounding it first, and `Bytes` is an exact count from the encoder. A cost compared against a threshold in the other unit is the failure section 40.1 opens with, and it is a hard one to find because both are small integers and the result is a heuristic that is quietly wrong rather than a compiler that stops.

- A cost is a time and a complexity compared lexicographically, from GCC's `comp_cost`, with an explicit infinity that means the thing is not possible rather than that it is expensive. Arithmetic saturates at infinity instead of wrapping, and a cost that saturated remembers that it did, because section 40.13 asks for the saturating case to be visible and a number that silently became `i64::MIN` decides something.

- A target's table is filled in completely or it does not exist. There is no `Default`, no way to construct one through a struct literal, and one way in: a builder that refuses to produce a table while any of its twenty six fields is unset, and names the ones that are missing when it refuses. Section 40.13 asks that the completeness check "must check every field, not merely that the struct was constructed", and putting it in the constructor rather than in a test means a target added next year cannot skip it. A field left unset would read as zero, a zero cost makes an operation free, and every heuristic that consulted it would go wrong in the same direction.

- Optimizing for size is a second table rather than a weighting applied to the first, which is what `ix86_cur_cost()` does. `-Os` selects the other table and every pass goes on asking the same questions in the same way, so no pass has to remember to check which goal it is working towards, which is the kind of thing a pass forgets in exactly one of its five decisions. The clearest case of why one table cannot do the job is `lea` against `add` on x86-64: they tie on time and the `lea` loses on size, and no single weighting produces a tie in one direction and a loss in the other.

- The two tables are checked against each other. A capability is spelled as an infinite cost, so "the speed table and the size table agree about what the machine can do" is a comparison of which entries are infinite in each, and that is a test rather than a convention. If they ever disagreed, selection and costing would disagree about what is legal and it would show up as unexplained code at `-Os` only.

- Half of a cost model is booleans rather than numbers, so there is a `TuneFlag` for each of the six decisions that are yes or no: whether the target prefers an `lea` to an `add`, whether scheduling helps it, whether a predictable branch is worth if-converting, whether unaligned access is cheap, whether the multiplier is fast, and whether partial register writes stall. Each defaults to the answer that is correct and slow, and each says at its declaration what that default costs, so a target is right before it is tuned.

- Every threshold any pass consults now lives in one file with the document that justifies it, the GCC parameter it corresponds to where there is one, and whether it was chosen or measured. That last field is the point. Section 40.13 puts it plainly, a constant marked chosen is a constant nobody should defend, and the two constants the documents said would have to be measured and have not been are marked as placeholders rather than dressed up as numbers that were earned.

- `cargo xtask thresholds` is the coding standard check section 40.12 asks for: a pass may not compare against a number it made up. It reads every pass source file and reports a bare integer literal on either side of a comparison, so `if size > 40` fails and `if size > limit` does not. Counts of zero, one and two are allowed because they are structure rather than tuning, a line that mentions a width is allowed because eight bits is a fact about the machine, and a line carrying `// not a threshold:` and a reason is allowed because somebody had to write the reason down. It runs in `cargo xtask ci` and the current tree passes it.

- A local aggregate of sixteen bytes or more is given an alignment of sixteen whatever its members ask for, which is what gcc does and what issue #347 asked for. Neither answer was wrong before: any address that satisfies the type's own alignment conforms, and a program that measures one is measuring a choice rather than a rule. The choice is made this way for two reasons. Sixteen bytes is a vector register, so the copy that moves such an object, the initialization that fills one and any vectorization of a loop over one all want an aligned address, and the frame is the one place where the alignment is free because the objects are being laid out anyway. And real code assumes it, since a `char buf[16]` on the stack handed to something that wants an aligned pointer is relying on what gcc does rather than on what the standard says. It only ever raises, so a declaration that asked for more with `alignas` keeps what it asked for, and a scalar is left alone however wide it is, which is gcc's rule and not an accident.

- The optimizer has analyses now, and the first two are the control flow graph and dominance. This is part of issue #397 and it is what documents 07 through 11 of `spec/optimizer` are all built on. `rucc_opt::Cfg` is the shape of a function with the instructions taken out: the predecessors of every block, which `Func` does not store because an argument travels on the edge that carries it rather than in a list kept beside one, a postorder, and which blocks control reaches at all. It reads one instruction per block rather than all of them, which is the invariant the verifier proves being spent somewhere.

- `block_addr` is not an edge here and the verifier's treatment of it does not carry over. The verifier counts a block whose address was taken as a successor of the block that took it, which can only add predecessors and so only take dominators away, which makes its check stricter and never looser. An analysis that copied that would report a live block as having a predecessor that never branches anywhere. Control arrives at such a block from an `indirect_br`, that instruction is a terminator, and it lists every block the address can hold, so the edge is already there from the place control really leaves.

- `rucc_opt::Dominators` answers which block every path from the entry has to go through, by the iterative algorithm of Cooper, Harvey and Kennedy. GCC uses Lengauer and Tarjan, which is asymptotically better and, on graphs the size of a function, slower. A query is two integer comparisons rather than a walk up the tree, from a depth first numbering of the tree that GCC also has and calls `compute_dom_fast_query`, because GVN and code motion ask per pair inside a loop rather than once per definition. There is no ET-forest and no incremental update: a change to the shape of a function throws the tree away.

- An unreachable block dominates nothing and is dominated by nothing, which is the one place this differs from the verifier's copy of the same algorithm. The verifier calls such a block vacuously dominated so that it is reported once, for being unreachable, rather than again for every value it uses. An optimizer must not, because "defined in a block that dominates this one" and "defined in a block control reaches" are different questions, and a pass that answers the first when it meant the second will move a use of a value into a place the value does not exist.

- `rucc_opt::PostDominators` answers the same question backwards, over the reversed graph, for the passes that want it: aggressive dead code elimination, if conversion, and the control dependence relation. `while (1) { }` has no path out of the function, so the reversed graph is disconnected and post-dominance is undefined for everything in the loop. The answer is GCC's, which is to invent an edge to the exit from the far end of each such region, with two differences: the invented edges are recorded and readable through `fake_exits`, so a pass that reasons about paths can tell that one of them is not a path the program can take, and every reachable block is asserted to have a path out before any answer is given, which turns a wrong answer into a crash.

- The six graphs section 6.6 of the design asks for are in `crates/rucc-opt/tests/dominators.rs` with the dominator tree of each written out by hand, including the irreducible two-entry loop, where the answer is that neither candidate header dominates the other and no special case in the algorithm produces it. With them is the test that matters most, which checks the analysis against the definition rather than against a second implementation of itself: for a thousand random graphs, `dominates(a, b)` has to agree with whether `b` is still reachable once `a` is deleted, which is the same question because taking the cycles out of a walk leaves a path over a subset of its blocks. That is the test that would catch a wrong meet, and a wrong meet is a silent wrong answer in every pass downstream.

- The third analysis is the loop forest, `rucc_opt::Loops`, which is the first half of document 07 of `spec/optimizer` and the start of issue #398. It answers the first two of the four questions section 7.6 says loop analysis exists to answer: what loops there are and nested how, and whether a value is the same on every iteration. For each loop it keeps the header, every block in it, the latches, the edges out of it, its parent and children, and its depth, and for each block it keeps the innermost loop holding it. The exits are kept rather than recomputed because GCC's `get_loop_exit_edges` is a walk of the loop body and every loop pass wants one, which is why GCC caches them too and calls it `LOOPS_HAVE_RECORDED_EXITS`.

- It finds them from strongly connected components rather than from back edges, which is the same set of loops by a shorter route. Take a component with a cycle in it and ask whether the nearest block dominating all of it is one of its own blocks. If it is, that block is the header, it is the only way in, and the component is exactly the natural loop of the back edges arriving at it. If it is not, the component has two ways in and is irreducible. Taking the header out and looking again finds what is nested inside, so the forest, the nesting and the irreducible set all come out of one walk and cannot disagree with each other. `crates/rucc-opt/tests/loops.rs` is where that claim is checked, by writing the textbook definition out a second time and holding the analysis against it on a thousand random graphs.

- Two back edges to one header are two latches of one loop, and the analysis does not try to work out whether one of them belongs to an inner loop. GCC does, from the profile if it has one and from induction variables if it does not, and section 7.1 of the design says rucc should not: a single latch is a canonical form, and the canonicalizer creates one by editing the graph, which is always right where a guess is sometimes wrong. `preheader` reports the same way, naming the one block outside the loop that every path in comes through when there is one and saying nothing when there is not, rather than inventing an answer a pass would then hoist into.

- Irreducible regions are reported and not repaired, which document 06 already committed to. `goto` into the middle of a loop makes one, node splitting is the repair and can multiply code size, and the payoff is loop optimization of code that is rare and usually cold. The one place this is more conservative than the textbook is a back edge buried inside an irreducible region, whose head does not become a loop header, and the effect is that the blocks are reported as irreducible instead. A loop pass skips them either way.

- Memory SSA is now built and read, which is the second half of document 09 and the rest of issue #398. Construction is the iterated dominance frontier over one variable: the blocks that write memory are the definitions, the joins their versions reach get a memory parameter, and a walk of the dominator tree threads the operand through every instruction that touches memory.
