It is one `lea` off the instruction pointer with the name on it, and it is built by the lowering rather than matched by a rule, for the same shape of reason a call and a local are. What a rule replaces a term with is instructions over values, and the operand of this one is a symbol, which the rule language has no way to bind and the solver has no way to say anything about: there is nothing in `lea sym(%rip)` a proof over bitvectors could discharge, because what makes it the right answer is the relocation and what the linker does with it. Both halves of that were already written, since a call to a name the file does not define needed the printer to write `sym(%rip)` and the encoder to emit the relocation first. There is deliberately no name for it in the term language, which is what stops the address being folded into the instruction that reads it; folding it is the right thing to do and turns a load of a global from two instructions into one, and it is a separate question about addressing modes with an issue of its own.

- A cast between a pointer and an integer as wide as one, which is no instruction at all and is therefore something no rule could have named. An address on this machine is an integer as wide as the machine addresses, so the cast changes what the type system calls the value and changes nothing about the value, and the register holding it afterward is the register that held it before. The front end never writes one at any other width, because it widens or narrows around the cast rather than through it, so `(int)(long)p` is a truncate that already had a rule and `(void *)i` is a sign extension that already had one. A cast written at a width the machine does not address at is refused by name rather than compiled to a move that quietly keeps the high half, since IR shaped that way came from somewhere other than this compiler's front end.

- Reading the operand of one of those casts is what materializes a null pointer. A constant is written into a register where it is wanted rather than where the IR defined it, and a null pointer is an `inttoptr` of zero, so without the read there would be no instruction and nothing to return.

- Four C programs that could not be compiled before were compiled on this machine, linked on a linux x86-64 host against a second translation unit built by gcc 16.2.0, and run: an `extern int` read by name, an `extern int[4]` indexed at both ends, a `char *` cast to a `long` and back and passed across a call, and a null pointer passed to a function that compares it. Each exits with exactly what a gcc build of the whole program exits with. The second translation unit is built by the reference on purpose, because the definition of a global is the half of this that is not written yet: a module carries its globals and nothing walks them, so a file that defines the variable it reads compiles to a reference the linker cannot resolve. That is #293 and it is what stands between here and `rucc hello.c -o hello && ./hello`.

## 0.3.7

### Added

- A `switch`, which is the last of the three programs that refused in the end to end sweep at 0.3.6. It becomes a compare and a branch for each case, in the order the cases were written, which is the version every compiler starts with and is correct for any number of them. It is also slow for a large one, and that is the right first answer: a slow `switch` is a working `switch` and no `switch` is not. A jump table for a dense one and a binary search on the value for a sparse one are what comes later, and both want a read only section to put a table in and a relocation to reach it, neither of which exists yet.

- The rewrite is the first thing in the back end that turns a block into blocks rather than an instruction into instructions, and it is not a rule and cannot be. A rule replaces a term with a term, and a term is instructions, so a rule has nowhere to put a block. What that means is that the IR reaching instruction selection is no longer quite the IR the middle end produced, which is written down in the pipeline and is the only place it is true. `--emit=ir` prints before any of it runs, so what a reader sees is still what the program said. Where a `switch` becomes what is a target decision rather than a language one, which is why it survives the front end intact rather than being expanded where it was parsed.

### Fixed

- A pointer parameter, which the ABI lowering used to turn away as a width no register holds. An address has no width in the IR, deliberately, because how wide one is belongs to the target and not to the program, so asking a pointer type how many bits it is gets nothing back and two separate places took that to mean the type was one they had no register and no rule for. `int g(char *s)` was the smallest program that refused. Both places now ask one question in one place, which is the same list of widths the rule set names terms by, so an argument the rules could lower can no longer be a function the ABI declines, and a width the ABI accepts can no longer be a register nothing downstream reads. The old comment warning that whoever widens one of the two should widen both is gone along with the second list.

- Addresses are named in the rule set at the address width of the machine, and adding to a pointer is named the add it already is, so every rule ever written about an add now reaches address arithmetic, including the ones that fold it into an addressing mode. Nothing was added to the rule set for this and nothing needed to be; what was missing was a name.

- A local whose address is taken, which is the other half of what stopped eight real programs at five. A fixed-size `alloca` is built from the frame rather than matched by a rule, for the same shape of reason a call is built from the calling convention: what a rule may replace a term with is instructions, and what a local wants first is bytes, which the rule language cannot ask for. Selection records what each one asked for and writes the one instruction that computes its address, and the distance into the frame is filled in after the frame is laid out, which is the first moment it is known. A variable length one is refused by name instead, because growing the stack where the declaration stands means moving the stack pointer mid-function and reaching everything else through a frame pointer afterward, and the frame lays out neither yet.

- A constant wanted in two blocks was written into a register in one of them and read in both. That is only right when the block it was written in dominates the other, and the second arm of a branch never does, so the other block was reading a register nothing had written. The IR guarantees a definition dominates its uses and this is the one place that moves a definition, since a constant is written where it is wanted rather than where the IR put it, and the guarantee does not survive the move. Each block gets its own copy of the number now, which is also the cheaper answer: one instruction that reads nothing beats holding a register live across a branch, and it is what a rematerializing allocator would do with the value anyway. The bug predates the `switch` work and needed a constant used in two arms and not foldable into an immediate in either, which is rare enough that nothing had hit it; expanding a `switch` produces exactly that shape and hit it immediately.

- An instruction written for a value an edge carries was landing after the branch at the end of the block, which is after the point the branch has already jumped away from. Arguments on the edges out of a block are read once every instruction in it is written, because a constant among them is materialized where it is first wanted and the end of the block is where an edge wants it, and the end of a block that leaves two ways is one instruction too far. The branch goes back on the end when that happened. Before it was noticed this crashed the block layout rather than miscompiling, which is the failure worth having.

- A vector was being named after the width of its lane, which no rule could have lowered correctly, since a rule for one integer would have been handed an instruction meaning several. It is refused now. Nothing produced one yet, so this is a hole closed rather than a bug observed.

- Ten C programs that could not be compiled before were compiled on this machine, assembled and linked on two linux x86-64 hosts, and run. Four are about addresses: a local whose address is taken and written through a pointer, a four-element array summed in a loop, a `swap` through two pointer parameters across a call, and `strlen` written out by hand. Six are switches: the three way one from the issue, one that falls through from one case into the next, one with no default at all, one over negative values, one over values too large for a byte, and one inside a loop. Each exits with exactly what a build of the same C exits with, under the system gcc on one host and under gcc 16.2.0 on the other. The array indexing folds into a single `leaq (%rcx,%rax,4), %rax`, and a leaf function whose local fits keeps its red zone and emits no prologue at all.

## 0.3.6

### Added

- What every x86-64 instruction is in bytes, which is the other half of the description `-S` already prints from and which `spec/11-asm-objects-debug.md` section 11.1 asks to be one description rather than two. The text an assembler reads and the bytes a processor reads are two spellings of one instruction, so both paths walk the same list of written instructions and only the last step differs, which means an instruction cannot be in the listing and missing from the object file, or be written with one operand and encoded with another. The table is keyed the way an assembler keys one, by the mnemonic, by what kind of thing each argument is and by the range the immediate falls in, because a mnemonic is not an instruction: `movl` is four different instructions depending on whether it is given a number, a register, a load or a store. It is not keyed by machine opcode, which would be a third description of the same instruction and a third place for the three to disagree. What the encoder does with a row is the mechanical part, which is the prefix that says a register the machine gained later is meant, the byte that names the registers, the second byte an address needs when it counts from the stack pointer, and however many bytes of immediate the row said.

- Choosing between two encodings of one instruction is the table's job and not the encoder's. Where the machine has a shorter form for a smaller number, and it does for every arithmetic instruction and for the 64-bit move, the short form is a row in front of the general one and the lookup takes the first that holds the number, so the encoder stays a function of the row it was handed rather than a pile of special cases. A number no form of an instruction can hold is refused by name rather than quietly cut down to fit, which is the bug this found in its own first draft: a 64-bit add of a number too large for four bytes was accepting the number and encoding a different one. Two short forms are deliberately not taken and say so, the accumulator-only encodings and the shift that carries no count and means one, because each saves a byte in a case narrow enough that a reader stops checking the row. The short form of a jump is not a row at all, because how far a jump goes is not known until every block has a place, which makes it a pass over the whole function rather than a decision an encoder makes.

- A machine function as the bytes of a text section, which is the other end of the assembly printer and deliberately the same walk over the same description: an opcode is the list of instructions the target says it is, each instruction's arguments come from the operands the target says they come from, and the only difference is that one path writes a name and the other hands it to the encoder. What comes back with the bytes is what a linker has to be told, which is where each function starts and how long it is, and every place in the bytes that names something the file does not contain. A jump inside a function is not among them: by the end of a function every block has a place, so those are filled in here and nothing downstream hears about them. Choosing which relocation goes with which addressing mode is this layer's job rather than the object writer's, per `spec/11-asm-objects-debug.md` section 11.3, because it is a fact about the instruction and not about the file format, so a call asks the linker for a distance it may answer with a stub and a global asks for one it may not. An addend carries the difference between where a relocation counts from, which is its own first byte, and where an instruction counts from, which is its last.

- A relocatable ELF object, which is the first thing this compiler produces that a linker will take. What the layer above the `object` crate holds is the part `object` cannot decide: which relocation an instruction wants, what a symbol's binding is, and the sections a linker expects to find whether or not anything was put in them. `.note.GNU-stack` is written explicitly and a test says so, because a linker that does not find it in every one of its inputs marks the stack executable, and leaving it out is the kind of mistake that produces a working program with a weakness in it. A name the file does not define is left undefined and untyped, because a linker resolves an undefined symbol by its name and the type of one defined nowhere in this file is nothing this file can honestly say. A name wanted twice is one symbol, since two symbols with one name is not a file a linker accepts. The vocabulary the assembler hands over, which is the bytes, where each function sits in them and every place that names something absent, moved down to `rucc-object` so that the crate which writes the file owns the description of what it writes. Mach-O and COFF are not here: the formats disagree about more than their headers, and each is written when the target that needs it is.

- `-c`, which is C in and an object file out, and which is the first thing this compiler produces that a linker will take. It is the same compilation as `-S` with a different last step, which is the point of section 11.1: the two read the same machine functions and differ only in whether an instruction is written as a name or handed to the encoder, so a listing cannot disagree with the object beside it. What a compilation gives back is now either text or bytes rather than a string, because a `Vec<u8>` holding UTF-8 for six kinds and a file format for the seventh would leave every reader guessing which it had. A target with a back end but no object writer says so rather than writing the wrong file.

- The whole path was run end to end on a real machine rather than only tested. Eight C programs were compiled with `-c` on this machine, copied to a linux x86-64 host, linked with the system `cc` and run, and each of the five that compiled exits with exactly what a gcc build of the same C exits with. They cover arithmetic, a recursive call, six arguments, a loop with a `break` and a `continue` in it, and unsigned shifts. The three that do not compile are refused by name rather than miscompiled, and each now has an issue: a local whose address is taken (#273), a pointer parameter (#274) and a `switch` (#275). The linked programs' `GNU_STACK` is readable and writable and not executable, which is the marker doing the job it is there for.

- Finding a linker and running it, so that `rucc a.c -o prog` produces a program rather than an object nobody linked. The linker is invoked directly rather than through the system `cc`, which is what makes the line ours to get right, and it is looked for in the order `spec/04-driver-and-cli.md` section 4.9 gives: an explicit `-fuse-ld=`, then `mold`, then `lld`, then the platform `ld`, and the missing one is reported before anything is compiled rather than after. The startup files and the library directories are looked for on the machine rather than configured, because a compiler that has to be told where `crt1.o` lives on each installation is a compiler nobody can install. A `-l` is an input and not a setting, since a library written between two files resolves for the file before it and not for the file after, so objects and libraries stay in one list in the order they were typed. The objects of a link go in a directory that is removed afterwards, because the plan says an object feeding the linker is temporary and does not say where, and where only has an answer once something is running. `-static`, `-shared`, `-pie`, `-no-pie`, `-nostdlib`, `-nostartfiles`, `-nodefaultlibs`, `-rdynamic`, `-s`, `-Wl,`, `-Xlinker`, `-fuse-ld=`, `-L` and `-B` are read, and `-###` prints the line it would run. `crtbegin.o`, `crtend.o` and `-lgcc` are left out until there is a `rucc-builtins` to put in them, which a Linux program that needs no helper routine and no static constructor links and runs without. `-static` is where their absence shows first and it is issue #277. The whole path was run on a linux x86-64 host: five C programs each compile, link and exit with what a gcc build of the same C exits with, two files link into one program, a call into libc through `-l` resolves, and a `-no-pie` link produces the other kind of executable.

- `cargo xtask disasm`, the differential disassembly check section 11.1 asks for, and a CI job that runs it. It takes every instruction the target writes, crossed with a register the machine had from the start and one it gained later, an address of every shape, and an immediate of every width, which comes to 804 instructions, and it asks a decoder that has never seen our table what our bytes say and what an assembler makes of the text we printed for the same instruction. The two accounts have to name the same instruction with the same operands. Bytes are not compared directly and should not be: we choose a longer encoding on purpose in the places listed above, and a check that fails on a difference we intend is a check nobody runs. An encoding bug is silent, survives every test that does not run the program, and costs a day to find from the far end, so it is worth a job of its own.

## 0.3.5

### Added

- `-S`, which is C in and assembly text out, and which is the first thing this compiler produces that another program will take. The listing is read out of the instruction description in `rucc-target` rather than written a second time, so there is almost nothing about x86-64 in the printer: what an opcode is called, how many instructions it really is, which operand each of those is given and how wide each is written are all the target's answers. What the printer knows is the syntax, which is that a register carries a percent sign, an immediate carries a dollar, an address is a displacement in front of a parenthesised base and index, and the source is written before the destination, which is the reverse of the order the operands are held in. Intel syntax is a second walk over the same description rather than a second description and is not written yet. The output of the six function program this was checked against assembles with GNU as, links with gcc and runs, returning what a gcc build of the same C returns.

- What an assembler is told about a function, which is the object format's answer and not the machine's, and the three formats disagree in ways that are not cosmetic. A Mach-O symbol carries an underscore in front of the C name and an ELF one does not, so a listing that got it wrong would fail to link against every library on the machine. A label local to a function is spelled `.L` on ELF and COFF and `L` on Mach-O, and one not spelled the local way ends up in the symbol table, where a debugger and a backtrace show it as if it were a function. ELF says how long a function is, in a form the assembler works out, and Mach-O has no way to. And ELF wants the marker saying the stack is not executable, whose absence makes it executable, which `spec/11-asm-objects-debug.md` section 11.3 calls out as a real and recurring security bug. Every function is written global, because a machine function does not carry the linkage the C had and nothing below the driver could ask, which is wrong for a `static` function and is why `-S` output is a thing to read rather than a thing to link until the object writer gives the machine IR somewhere to keep it.

- What every x86-64 instruction is in assembly, which is the description `-S` and the encoder both read and which `spec/11-asm-objects-debug.md` section 11.1 asks to be one description rather than two, on the grounds that an assembly listing which disagrees with the object file next to it is worse than no listing at all. An opcode in the machine IR is one instruction to the register allocator and is not always one instruction to the machine, so what is held for each of them is a list: a comparison is a compare and a set, an unsigned division is the clearing of the high half and then the division, an eight bit division is three instructions because its dividend and its two answers are all in one register, and the three opcodes that exist to say where a value is are no instructions at all. An argument names the operand it comes from by index rather than being the operand, because the assembler writes the source before the destination and the operand vector holds them the other way round, because a two-address instruction names one register once and the allocator has already made the other one equal to it, and because an instruction in the middle of an opcode may name no operand at all. The width is on the argument for the same reason: a comparison of two 64-bit registers sets a byte, a shift of one reads its count from a byte, and an eight bit multiply is done 32 bits at a time because the machine has nothing narrower than 16 and the low eight bits of a product depend on nothing above them. Every spelling in it was assembled by GNU as and read back with a disassembler, including the one place a register is named rather than allocated, which is the high half of an eight bit remainder.

- Every instruction a frame is made of is described alongside the ones a rule selects, rather than being named in one table and described in none. A prologue pushes, a spill stores, an epilogue returns and a copy moves, and not one of those is anything a lowering rule could match, so until now nothing could say what a push does with its operand. They are in the same table as the rest, six of the twelve names are shared with the rules since a prologue taking its frame is the same subtraction a program could have written, and a test pins each of them against the name the target really asks for. Everything downstream reads a function's instructions the same way whatever put them there, and a second table for the ones no rule reaches would only be a second place for an opcode to go missing from.

- The block layout, which is the last pass in the backend and the one that turns a graph into something a machine could run. Nothing before it had said which block comes first in memory, and a machine has no such thing as an arm of a branch: it runs the instruction after the one it just ran. So this picks an order, and then writes the jumps that the edges the order did not put next to each other still need. At `-O0` the order is reverse postorder with each block's successors walked in reverse, which is enough to get the fall-through right on an `if` and on a loop without knowing anything about either, and which leaves a loop as a header, a body and a back edge that is the only jump in it. The chain construction weighted by block frequency that `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.6 describes is what replaces it above `-O0` and is not written yet. Blocks nothing reaches are laid out at the end rather than deleted, because deleting one is a decision about what the program does.

- Where a jump goes, which stays on the block rather than moving into the instruction, since an instruction is twenty four bytes and a block reference does not fit in one. What changes is that the arms now mean something they did not mean before: a block with no arms returns, a block with one falls into it when it is next and jumps to it when it is not, and a block with two ends in a conditional jump to the first and falls into the second, which is always the block laid out next. That last guarantee is what stops a block ever ending in two jumps. When neither arm can be next, because both are blocks the walk has already been to, the layout makes an empty block for the second edge to jump from, which is the critical edge splitting done for a different reason and at the cost the second jump would have been. Which arm is which is therefore no longer which way the condition went, so a target names both conditional jumps and the layout picks the one that matches the order it chose.

- `--emit=mir-final`, which is the whole compiler in one command for the first time: C in, machine instructions out, every register a real one and every offset into the frame a number. Every pass in the backend was written and tested on its own and each was reachable only from its own tests, because the order they run in lived in those tests rather than anywhere a caller could find it. `rucc_codegen::pipeline` is that order written down once. A caller says which machine it is compiling for and hands over a function, and what comes back is the same function after selection, after splitting the critical edges, after allocation, after the frame is worked out and after the prologue is written. A function with something in it no rule reaches is reported by name along with what stopped it, and every function in the file is attempted rather than stopping at the first, so a program that is ahead of the backend in three places says so three times rather than one recompilation at a time. A target with no backend behind it says that instead of quietly generating instructions for a different machine.

- `-fno-omit-frame-pointer` and `-mno-red-zone`, along with the spellings that turn each of them back off. Both knobs were already in the frame layout and neither had any way to reach it from a command line, so the frame this compiler built was the same frame whatever was asked for. A profiler that walks the stack by following saved frame pointers needs the first one, and every kernel in the wild passes the second, because the promise that nothing touches the 128 bytes below the stack pointer is false as soon as an interrupt can be taken on that stack. The last spelling on the command line wins, which is what gcc does with a pair of them.

- Calls are lowered, which is the last piece of the System V convention and the last thing standing between the backend and a program that does something. A call is not a rule and could not be one: a rule matches one term and rewrites it, and what a call's operands are is whatever its signature made them, so `rucc_codegen::abi` builds one out of the same description of the convention the arguments already came from. The values it passes are reads constrained to the registers the convention places them in, what comes back is a definition constrained to the register it comes back in, and every register the callee is free to destroy is a definition of that register and nothing else. Those last ones are the clobbers and they needed nothing new from the register allocator, because a definition of a physical register already blocks it for the length of the instruction, which is exactly what a clobber is. A register an argument or the result already names is not repeated, since naming it once already blocks it. `int f(int a) { return g(a) + a; }` now goes from C through to a machine function that keeps `a` in a register the callee has to put back, and the prologue and the epilogue that put it back are the ones that were already written.

- What a call costs the frame, which nothing after instruction selection could work out. A function is lowered to its machine instructions and to one more fact: how many bytes the widest call in it needs for the arguments it passes below the stack pointer, or nothing at all for a function that calls nothing. Those are the two fields the frame layout asks for and had no source for, and a function that calls nothing is a leaf, which is the function that may use the red zone and the one whose stack pointer nobody else depends on. A call that passes everything in registers still costs thirty two bytes under the Windows convention, because a Windows caller leaves the callee room to spill its register arguments into whether the callee uses it or not, and that falls out of asking the convention rather than being written down twice.

- A variadic call says how many vector registers it passed arguments in, which System V requires and which is easy to leave out because leaving it out works until it does not. A variadic callee reads that count and skips saving its vector registers when it is zero, so leaving whatever happened to be in the register there makes the callee save a register file it was never given. The count is zero today, since a call that passes a float is refused rather than compiled, and it stops being a constant on the day one is not.

- The branches are lowered, and they turned out to need the least of the rule language of anything so far. Where a block goes is on the block in machine IR rather than on its terminator, which means a pattern never has a block in it and a replacement never has a block in it, and once that is true an unconditional jump has nothing left to be: the edge is the whole of it, so it lowers to no instruction at all, the same way a return of nothing does. A conditional branch keeps one thing, which is the condition, and that is what its single rule is about. The instruction it becomes encodes to nothing on its own and exists to hold the condition in a register until something reads it. What turns that into a `test` and a `jcc`, and which way round, is the block layout, because which of the two arms falls through is the layout's answer and not the selector's, and `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.9 already put combining a compare and a branch in the peephole pass that runs after it. That is also why nothing in the rule language mentions flags. A function with an `if` in it now goes from C through to a machine function with every register physical.

- Splitting critical edges, in `rucc_codegen::split`, which is what has to run between lowering and allocation now that a function can have more than one way through it. An edge carries values when the block it goes to takes parameters, and giving a parameter its value is a move that has to happen on the edge itself: putting it at the end of the block the edge leaves runs it on the way to the other arm as well, and putting it at the start of the block the edge arrives at runs it for the other predecessor as well. An edge out of a block with one way out has the first place and an edge into a block with one way in has the second, and an edge that is neither has no place at all, which is the assertion the register allocator has always had and which nothing could reach until there were branches. Such an edge is now turned into two, with an empty block between them, and both halves have somewhere. An edge carrying nothing is left alone whether it is critical or not, since there are no moves to find a place for and an empty block is a jump nobody needed.

- Arguments are lowered, which is the other side of the calling convention and the one part of the backend that is not a lowering rule. A rule matches one term and rewrites it, and which register the third argument arrives in is a fact about neither: it depends on the argument's position and on the classification of every argument before it, so the arguments are built by hand out of what the target says a convention does, the same way a prologue is. The classification itself was already there and already runs earlier, in `rucc-lower`, which is why what reaches the backend is plain scalars with the aggregates already split and a return through memory already an ordinary pointer parameter in front of the rest. What was missing was the step after it, from how a value travels to which register it is actually in, and that is now `rucc_target::Places`: ask it about each value in the order the signature holds them and it answers with a register or a place in the argument area. It knows the one thing about the two x86-64 conventions that was written down as a gap rather than as a field, which is that SysV counts integer and vector positions separately and Windows counts one position for both, so a `double` in the third position on Windows is in `xmm2` and `r8` is spent on nothing.

- A function that takes arguments is a function the backend can compile at all, which it was not before. The register allocator refuses an entry block with parameters on it, because a block parameter gets its value from a move on the edge into the block and there is no edge into an entry block, so a function of even one argument could not be run through it. Each parameter is now bound to the register it arrived in by an instruction that encodes to nothing and exists for its operand, which is the same arrangement the return already used and is decided by the same allocator. `int f(int a, int b) { return a + b; }` goes from C through to a machine function. An argument that arrives on the stack is reported rather than compiled, because where the stack put it is a distance into a frame and no frame is worked out until after allocation, and so is an argument in a vector register, because every rule in the set is about an integer and nothing downstream could use one.

- What a rule with an effect claims, which is the question `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.2 asked to have settled before any rule that has one was written, and which was blocking every remaining piece of the backend. Nothing in the rule set could read or write memory, so nothing in a real program could be lowered: a load, a store, a call and a branch are what a program is made of and each of them does something rather than merely computing something. The answer is that a term may compute a memory rather than a value, and that the two halves of a rule have to agree about which of the two they computed as well as about what it is. Memory is one map from an address to a byte, which is an array in the solver, and there is nothing wider than a byte built in: a load of four bytes is four reads put together and a store of four bytes is four writes nested, both written out in the model file, so the byte order is a thing a reviewer checks against a manual rather than a thing the verifier decides on their behalf. That is the one fact about a memory access that testing on one machine will never catch, and the test that proves it is caught takes a correct model, turns one load's byte order around, and requires the solver to refute it. A rule that touches memory is asked in the theory that has arrays and gets a constant for the memory it starts from, and a rule that does not is asked exactly the question it was asked before, so the hundred and sixty seven rules that were already proved are unaffected. A rule that replaces something computing a value with something computing a memory is refused with that as the reason, rather than reported as a mismatch of widths that no width would fix.

- The loads and the stores themselves, at every width the machine has a `mov` for, through a register and through a register with a constant added, which is every access to a field of a structure and every local reached through a frame pointer. Sixteen rules, all discharged. A store is written with the value first and the address second, because that is the order the IR holds them in and a pattern is matched against an operand list by position, and a test says so: a rule that named the address first would store to the value, and no proof would catch that, since a proof is about the rule file agreeing with itself and both halves would be wrong the same way. The instruction description gained the two forms they need, and a store is the first instruction in it that writes no register at all, which is what having an effect means and is all the allocator needs to know about one.

- Loads and stores are now lowered, which is the selector half of the same piece. A load through a pointer already in a register is one instruction, and so is a read of a field of a structure or of a local through a frame pointer, because the addition of the offset folds into the addressing mode the machine does for free. A displacement is signed and 32 bits, so the rule that folds one has a guard saying the constant fits, and a constant too wide turns the rule down and leaves the addition where it was, which nobody had to write down: it is the next way the selector offers the operand. A store is the first instruction the selector emits that writes no register at all, and the only thing that made it different was that there is no result to put anywhere. What remains before a program can be compiled through is the terminators and the calls, which need no more of the rule language than is now there.

- Returns are lowered, which is the first thing in the backend that is about the calling convention rather than about arithmetic. What a rule for a return can do is put the value where the caller will look for it, and what it cannot do is leave, because the epilogue has to give the frame back first and the epilogue is appended after register allocation rather than chosen by a rule. So the instruction a return lowers to encodes to nothing and exists for its operand: a read constrained to the return register is how the allocator is told where the value has to end up, and it is what keeps the value alive that far. `int main(void) { return 0; }` now goes from C through to a machine function that puts zero in a register, moves it into `rax` and returns. The claim a return rule makes is that the value comes through unchanged, which is everything about a return that arithmetic can say, and which register it comes through is a target fact checked against both conventions by a test rather than by the solver, since a solver has no notion of a register. A return of nothing lowers to nothing at all, because the epilogue is the whole of it. A return of more than one value has no rule, since where each of them goes is the convention's answer rather than a term's.

- The prologue, the epilogue, and the moves the allocator asked for, which together are what turns an allocated function into one an encoder could read. The frame was worked out in the previous release and nothing wrote it down, so a function still had spills the allocator had recorded as edits rather than as instructions and a stack pointer that never moved. `rucc_codegen::finish` writes all three. The moves go in first and the prologue afterwards, because every offset into a frame is measured from the stack pointer the body runs with, so putting the instruction that takes the frame in front of the reloads once they are placed is the arrangement where nothing has to be adjusted twice. An epilogue goes at the end of every block the function returns from, which is every block with no edge out of it, since a function with two ways out has two places that have to give the frame back. Several edits at one place keep the order the allocator gave them, which matters where one of them reloads a value another one is about to store. A frame that had to force its alignment finds its saved registers again through the frame pointer, because forcing the alignment throws away how far the stack pointer had moved and nothing else can say where they went. The instructions are named rather than built, so the file has no x86-64 in it: a target says which opcode moves a register, which one pushes, which one reaches a frame slot, and the same emitter serves any machine that has those. The vector registers a Windows call preserves are stored into the frame rather than pushed, because no machine here pushes one, and the aligned store is correct rather than lucky since a frame with a vector save in it is aligned to sixteen by construction.

- Where a saved vector register goes, which `rucc_codegen::frame` reported as a list of registers with no offsets attached, so nothing could have written them even in principle. Each one is now a register and the place in the frame it lives, and the offsets are shifted along with the spill slots and the locals when the frame decides where its areas start.

- Moving a spilled value into a spilled parameter, which an edge can ask for when a value that had to go to the stack is handed to a block parameter that also had to. No machine has that instruction. The expansion into a load and a store through a register belongs to the allocator rather than to the target, because which register is free to hold it on the way is something only the allocator knows. It uses the second scratch register of the class rather than the first, since the first is the one the move ordering may be holding a value in for the length of a cycle.

### Fixed

- A loop that carried two values round lost one of them, which is a compiled `gcd` returning the wrong answer. The moves an edge out of a block turns into went in front of the last instruction in that block, on the reasoning that the last instruction of a block with an edge is the branch. It is not one here: an unconditional jump is the edge itself and does not become an instruction until the block layout runs, which is after this, so the moves went in front of an ordinary instruction that was still owed moves of its own. In `gcd` that instruction is the one the second argument arrives on, and the edge's move wrote over the register it arrived in before the move that took it out of there had run. They now go behind everything in the block, which is what the end of a block means when the block does not end in anything.

- A division lost its remainder when nothing read its quotient. An instruction that writes a register before it has read its operands says so, which is what stops a divisor being put in the register the answer is about to land in, and it was recorded as being live at that one point and no longer. A value nothing reads is live only where it was written, so a quotient nobody wanted was live at a point in front of everything else the same instruction wrote, and the register it went to looked free to them. The remainder was then given the same register, and the copy taking the unwanted quotient out of the register the machine insists on landed on top of it. A register written early is now live to the end of the instruction that wrote it as well as from before its operands are read, which is what early has always meant and what the interval was only half saying.

- A machine IR block with nothing in it printed as though it went nowhere. Where a block goes is on the block, and the printer put the arms after the last instruction, so a block that had no last instruction lost them. Nothing made one of those until splitting a critical edge did, and a block that is an edge and no instructions is exactly what splitting one leaves behind. The arms now go on a line of their own when there is no instruction to hang them off.

- An `__int128` argument that could not have both the general purpose registers it needs took one of them anyway and reported that it had travelled in registers. On SysV a value that cannot have all the registers it needs takes none of them and goes to memory, and the ones it did not take are still there for the arguments after it, which is what makes the eighth argument of a call depend on the first seven. Aggregates were already right about this and scalars were not, so a call whose sixth register was spent by half of an `__int128` put the argument after it in memory with a register still free, which is a mismatch between a caller and a callee compiled from different declarations rather than an error either of them would notice.

## 0.3.4

### Added

- The lowering rules compile into a matcher the compiler links against. `x86-64.rules` has been read and proved by `rucc-verify` since it was written, and nothing in the compiler could match anything with it, which was the half of `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.2 that was missing: the rules are data, the matcher is a generated automaton rather than a chain of conditionals, and no lowering is a hand written `match` arm. `rucc-rules` emits the trie over the patterns as Rust, the build of `rucc-codegen` runs it over every rule file that crate owns, and `rucc_codegen::select` is the walk over what comes out. For x86-64 that is 167 rules over 602 trie nodes. A pattern is matched against a `Subject`, which is the three questions the automaton asks of a term, so the selector will answer them out of the IR rather than building terms to throw away, and a match gives back the rule that fired and what its pattern bound in the order it binds them. A guard becomes a function of the constants the pattern matched, and a rule whose guard is false is a rule that did not match rather than a match that failed, so the walk carries on looking: an immediate too wide for `add` finds no rule at all, which is the right answer, because putting the constant in a register first is a decision for the selector and not for the table. A binding that is not a constant makes a guard false, since a rule about a number has nothing to say about a register. The guard language ends in the emitter, so a guard written in something it cannot compile is refused with the line it is on rather than emitted and found later as an error in generated code nobody wrote.

- The x86-64 register file, and what each of the two calling conventions over it does with each register. `rucc-target` could describe a register file and no target had one, which every piece of the backend that is about to be written needs: the machine IR prints registers by name, the allocator hands out the registers a convention says it may, an argument has to arrive somewhere, and a prologue has to know which registers it is obliged to put back. Registers are numbered the way the instruction encoding numbers them, so `rax` is zero and `rsp` is four, and the names are the sixty four bit ones because `al` and `ax` and `eax` are three ways of writing part of `rax` rather than three registers. The three classes are `gpr`, `xmm` and the x87 stack, which is described because a `long double` comes back in `st0` and nothing allocates from it. SysV and Windows are both written down and they disagree about nearly everything: `rdi` is where the first argument arrives on one and a register the callee has to preserve on the other, the upper ten vector registers are preserved on Windows and none of them are on SysV, and a SysV leaf function has a hundred and twenty eight byte red zone while a Windows caller reserves thirty two bytes below the call instead. The allocation order is part of the convention rather than of the file, because which registers to hand out first follows from which ones a call destroys. `--print-config` reports the file a target has, or `none` for one nobody has written down, which is AArch64 and RISC-V until their backends arrive.

- What each x86-64 instruction does with its operands, which is the other half of a lowering rule and the half the rule file does not hold. A rule says which machine instruction computes a term and `rucc-verify` proves that it does, and neither says where the operands are allowed to live, so the allocator has nothing to read. That is a fact about the instruction rather than about the rewrite, and the same instruction is reached by many rules, so it is written once per opcode instead of once per rule. Every one of the 156 opcodes the x86-64 rule set can produce now has a form, and the eleven forms between them are the whole of what the machine insists on: an ordinary arithmetic instruction is two address, so its destination is its first source and the allocator is what has to make that true, a shift by a variable count reads the count from `cl` and from nowhere else, a division puts its dividend and its quotient in `rax` and destroys `rdx` on the way, and a compare and set writes a byte that has nothing to do with either operand. The registers a division does not want its answer in are written as an early definition, which is what says the divisor may not be sitting there. Nothing in any form mentions flags, because a comparison and the set that reads it are one opcode, and that is a constraint on the rule set rather than a simplification of the machine. Two tests hold the description and the rule file against each other, in both directions, so an opcode a rule selects and nobody described is a failing build rather than a selector that panics on one program in a thousand. An operand's role and its constraint moved down from `rucc-mir` to `rucc-target` to make room for this, since a target describes its instructions before there is any machine IR to describe them in, and `rucc-mir` re-exports both so nothing that reads them changed.

- The selector, which is the pass that turns an IR function into machine IR. The rules were data and the matcher could answer one question about one term, and nothing walked a function and asked it, so a compiler that had a proved lowering table still had no way to reach it. `rucc_codegen::lower` is that walk. Nothing in it decides what an IR term lowers to, which is the whole point of the arrangement: the decisions are in `rules/x86-64.rules`, they are proved before they are used, and this file is short. The one question it does answer is which of the several ways an instruction can be shown to the matcher to offer first, because the automaton gives one answer per node and does not backtrack across the alternatives, while one value is legitimately three different terms: the register it is in, the number it is if it is a constant, and the instruction that computed it. So the choice is made before the walk rather than during it, and the ways are tried most offered first, which is the maximal munch `spec/10-backend.md` asks for. `x + y * 4` on three IR instructions comes out as one `lea`, because the rule that matched reached down and took the multiply, and the multiply is not emitted since nothing else reads it. An operand read by two instructions is folded into neither, since computing it twice is not a saving. Two things fall out of the arrangement that nobody had to write down. One is the immediate fallback: a rule that wants an immediate has a guard that turns a number too wide for the machine down, so on `x + 2147483648` it does not fire, and the next way of showing the operand puts the constant in a register, which is a `mov` and an `add` and is the right answer. The other is that a constant every use folded into an immediate leaves nothing behind, because a constant is not lowered where it is written but materialized where a register for it is first wanted, which also gives it the shortest live range it could have. What the machine insists on comes from the instruction description rather than from the rules, so a shift by a variable count asks for the count in `cl` and a division writes its quotient in `rax` and destroys `rdx`, and the allocator is what will act on all of it. The two address constructors a rule can write are described there as well and for the same reason, since an address is a thing the machine has rather than a thing a rewrite invents. Anything with an effect is still turned down, loads and stores and calls and every terminator, because `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.2 wants the language for an effect settled before the rules that have one are written, and a function containing any of them is reported as one this cannot lower rather than lowered wrongly.

- Where every value in a machine function is live, which is the question both allocators ask first and which `rucc-regalloc` could not answer because it was an empty crate holding a layer rank. Asking it of machine IR that is still in SSA form is what makes it cheap: a value is written once, so its live range is one interval from where it is written to the last place it is read, and no use has to be traced back to which of several definitions it reads from. The function is laid out in the order it was written in, which is the order the encoder will emit the blocks in, because an allocator working over some other order would be allocating for a program nobody emits. An instruction has two points in that line rather than one, an operand being read at the first and written at the second, and that is the whole of what makes a two address instruction possible: the register a value was read from is free by the time the result is written. An operand written early is written at the first point instead, where it collides with everything read there, which is what a division does to the register it destroys and the reason the points come in pairs. What arrives live in each block and what leaves live is a fixpoint over the blocks rather than one pass, because a loop carries a value from the end of a block round to a block laid out in front of it, and a block a value is live through contributes the whole of that block so that the interval covers the loop instead of stopping at the last instruction that says the value's name.

- The order the moves an edge turns into are made in. A block's parameters arrive from every predecessor, and after allocation each parameter is a place and each argument is a place, so an edge is a set of moves that all happen at once, and no machine has that instruction. Most of the time any order will do. The case where none will is two values that swap, where each move destroys what the other has still to read, and the way out is a third place to hold one of them, which costs one extra move for a cycle of any length however long it is. `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.4 says this is a small algorithm that is wrong in a startling number of compilers, so it is written on its own and tested on its own rather than being a loop inside the allocator, and it knows nothing about what a place is, since a place is a register for a value that has one and a stack slot for a value that was spilled and the algorithm is the same either way. The tests check what the places hold afterwards rather than what order came out, because that is the property that matters and an order can get it right in more than one way.

- The single pass allocator's decision: where every value of a function goes, which is what `-O0` asks for and what `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.4 describes as linear scan with spill on demand, no splitting and no coalescing. The values are taken in the order they are written, each is given a register that nothing else live at the same time is in, and when there is no such register the value in flight whose range ends last goes to the stack, since a value wanted for a long time is the cheapest to spill per instruction it frees a register over. A register an instruction insists on is left alone rather than handed to the value that wants it, so a division's dividend is not given `rax` for the whole of its life but `rax` is kept free over that one instruction and a move puts the value there on the way in. That costs a move the backtracking allocator will not need and it buys one rule that holds everywhere, which is that a fixed register is a fact about the instruction rather than about the value in it. An operand that can only be read from memory is the other way round and forces the value it names onto the stack whatever else is true of it. A two address instruction is the one place this looks past the interval it is placing: the value being written is treated as live from where the instruction reads, so that copying the source into the destination is always safe, and when the value being reused is read there for the last time the destination simply takes its register and no copy is written at all, which is worth the dozen lines because otherwise every piece of arithmetic in the output would carry a move in front of it. Nothing here touches the function. What comes out is a table saying where each value went and what each slot of the frame is for, and the rewrite that makes it true reads that, which is what lets the decision be checked by looking at it.

- The pass that makes an assignment true in the function it was worked out for, which is the rest of the `-O0` allocator. Every operand is rewritten to the place its value was given, every block stops asking for anything and every edge stops carrying anything, which is the point at which machine IR stops being in SSA form and starts being something an encoder could read. The moves are handed back rather than written, because a move is an instruction and an instruction has an opcode and an opcode belongs to a target, and `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.8 says this crate holds none of that. What comes back is a move between two places, the class it is in, and where in the function it goes, and `rucc-codegen` turns each one into whatever its target moves a register with, which for a value on the stack is a load or a store rather than a move at all. The edits at any one place are in the order they have to be made in, which matters twice: a spilled operand is read into a scratch register in front of the instruction that wants it, and a two address instruction's copy has to come after that read because what it copies may be the thing that was just read in. A fixed register turns into a move each way, since the assignment deliberately gave the value some other register, so a division gets a move into `rax` in front of it and a move out of `rax` behind it. An edge turns into the moves that write the block's parameters, in an order they can be made in one at a time, and where those moves go depends on the shape of the edge: a block with one way out puts them at its own end in front of the branch it finishes with, and a block with a choice puts them at the start of the block the edge arrives in, which is safe exactly because nothing else arrives there. An edge that is critical has neither place to put them and has to have been split before allocation ran, which is checked rather than assumed. `rucc_regalloc::run` is the two halves together and is the shape section 10.4 gives an allocator: a function and the registers it may use in, an assignment and the moves that make it true out, which is the same question the backtracking allocator will answer the same way.

- The allocation checker, which reads an assignment back and says whether it is one the machine can actually run. `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.4 asks for it in debug and CI builds and the reason is the shape of the bug it exists to find: an allocator does not change what a program means, so a wrong allocation compiles and links and runs, and then produces the wrong number in one function of one program under one register pressure, with the stack trace pointing at arithmetic that is correct and reading a value something unrelated overwrote four instructions earlier. The checker turns all of that into an assertion at the point the mistake was made, naming the two values and the register they were both put in. It asks four things, which are the whole of what an assignment has to get right: every value has somewhere to live, two values wanted at the same time are not in the same register or the same slot, nothing is sitting in a register an instruction insists on over the instruction that insists on it, and a value an instruction can only read from memory is in memory. It does not ask whether the allocation is any good, since a function with every value on the stack is slow and correct and this is the thing that says which of the two a problem is. It works out the two address instructions again for itself rather than borrowing that from the allocator, because a checker that shares its reasoning with the thing it checks agrees with it about the mistakes too, and the one bug it could never find is the one in the code they have in common. It is allowed to be slow for the same reason. `Assignment` grew a way to build one by hand, which is what lets the checker's own tests hand it a deliberately wrong answer and what the backtracking allocator in M4 will say its answer with, since what an assignment is has to be separable from how any one allocator arrives at one.

- What a function's stack looks like while it runs, which `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.7 calls the frame and which is worked out after register allocation rather than before, because the largest area in most frames is the spill slots and nothing knows how many of those there are until the allocator has finished running out of registers. The areas are the ones section 10.7 lists and they are in that order from the stack pointer upward: the outgoing argument area at the bottom, because a call reads its stack arguments from the stack pointer upward, then the spill slots, then the locals, then the saved vector registers, then the saved general purpose registers the prologue pushed, then the saved frame pointer and the return address and the arguments the caller passed on the stack. Every offset is from the stack pointer in the body of the function, which is the one base register that is always there. The size the prologue subtracts is not the size of the areas, because a call has to leave the stack pointer on a multiple of sixteen and the call that reached this function pushed a return address and left it eight bytes off, so the frame is whatever brings it back given however many pushes came in front of it, which is why one push can make a frame smaller. A leaf function small enough and quiet enough gets the red zone and moves the stack pointer not at all, which is what the hundred and twenty eight bytes SysV promises are for and what makes such a function's prologue and epilogue empty. Locals are placed widest alignment first so that nothing is padded to reach the next one, and a local wanting more alignment than a call leaves the stack pointer with is reported as a realignment the prologue has to force rather than being placed by arithmetic that cannot know what the caller's stack pointer was a multiple of. A realigned frame needs a frame pointer and no longer knows how far away the caller's stack is, and it says so rather than answering with a number that would be wrong. Which registers the prologue has to put back is read off the rewritten function rather than off the assignment, because a spilled value is reloaded into a scratch register that no assignment mentions and a scratch register the convention preserves is one that has to be saved. They come back in the convention's order rather than the order the allocator reached for them, so that two functions saving the same registers get the same prologue. A convention now also says what the stack pointer has to be a multiple of at a call, how many bytes the call instruction itself pushes, how wide a saved register is, and which class its integer and vector registers are in, which are the four facts frame arithmetic needs and the only ones that were missing.

### Changed

- The rule files moved from `rules/` at the root of the repository to `crates/rucc-codegen/rules/`, and the gate is now `cargo run -p rucc-verify -- crates/rucc-codegen/rules`. A published crate has to build from its own source archive and a build script cannot read a file outside the package it belongs to, so the rules live in the crate whose build compiles them. Nothing else moved and there is still one copy of every rule and one gate over it. `cargo xtask layers` learned the difference this needs: a build tool may be a build dependency of a crate in the stack, because what runs during a build is not what ships in the binary, and it is still refused as an ordinary or a development dependency.

## 0.3.3

### Added

- `__builtin_fabs` and `__builtin_copysign`, in each of the three floating point widths. These are functions of `math.h` and a program can call them, which is what makes them different from the classification family, and what makes them different from the library builtins is where the function lives. Everything the library family calls is in the C library, which is on the link line of every program, so a call to `abort` that nothing folds still links. `copysign` is in the math library, which is not, and a program that only ever wrote `__builtin_copysign` had no reason to ask for `-lm`, so leaving the call behind turns a program gcc links into one that does not. There is nothing to call anyway: `fabs(x)` is `x` with its sign bit clear and `copysign(x, y)` is `x` with `y`'s sign bit, and neither rounds, raises anything or has a case it cannot answer. Both are described over the bits rather than written with comparisons and negation, because that is what they are. A negative zero has a sign bit to clear while comparing equal to a positive zero, and a nan compares equal to nothing at all and keeps its payload through both operations, so `__builtin_fabs(-__builtin_nan("0x1"))` is that same nan with the sign gone. In the IR each is a bitcast, a mask and a bitcast back, and for the x87 format the mask is as wide as the value and not as wide as the object it sits in, so the six bytes of padding are left alone. Each folds in the front end as well, which is what a static initializer written with one needs. Five files come off the gcc.c-torture exclusion list and the suite goes to 1655 of 1772.

- The builtins whose answer is a constant, `__builtin_inf` and `__builtin_huge_val` and `__builtin_nan` and `__builtin_nans` in each of the three floating point widths, and `__builtin_strlen` and `__builtin_strcmp` of a string literal. These were declared already and reached the IR as a call to the library function of the same name, which is right wherever a call may be made, and a program writes them in two places where none may. One is a static initializer: `double x = __builtin_inf();` at file scope initializes an object with static storage duration, so there is no point in the program at which the call could happen, and it was refused with "initializer element is not constant". The other is a file that has taken the plain name for something of its own, which is the reason the prefixed spelling exists at all, and `execute/921007-1.c` in the torture suite defines `static int strcmp(){return-1;}` and then calls `__builtin_strcmp`, where the call this compiler emitted collided with that definition and came out as an internal error. Both are answered in the front end now and neither leaves a call behind. The nan payload is read the way `strtoull` reads a number, base sixteen or eight or ten by what it starts with, and it goes into the low bits of the significand, with the quiet bit set for `__builtin_nan` and clear for `__builtin_nans`, except that a signalling nan with nothing in it would be an infinity so it takes the next bit down instead, which is what gcc does with `__builtin_nans("")`. A payload that is not a number, and one that is not a literal at all, are both left as the call to the library that gcc also emits for them. The encodings were measured against gcc 16 rather than derived, in all three formats, the x87 one on a machine that has it. The gcc.c-torture execution suite goes to 1650 of 1772.

- The floating point classification builtins, `__builtin_isnan`, `__builtin_isinf`, `__builtin_isfinite`, `__builtin_signbit`, `__builtin_isunordered`, `__builtin_islessgreater`, the four relational ones, and the spellings of each that name a width, nineteen names in all. Every macro `math.h` gives these names to expands to the builtin of the same name, so a compiler that does not answer them cannot include `math.h` and mean it, and eighteen files in the gcc.c-torture execution suite were on the exclusion list for it. None of them is a call to anything: there is no function of any of these names under the macro on any platform, and what each one is instead is a comparison. Four of them are operators C already has, `isgreater` is `>` and `isless` is `<` and so on, and become exactly those, because the difference the standard draws between the macro and the operator is that the macro does not raise the invalid operation exception on a quiet NaN and this compiler does not model floating point exceptions. The other six are a node of their own, since `isunordered` and `islessgreater` cannot be written with the operators without naming an operand twice, and naming it twice would evaluate it twice, which is wrong for `isnan(f())`. In the IR each becomes what it means and nothing more: `isnan(x)` is `fcmp uno %0, %0`, the value that is unordered with itself, `isinf` and `isfinite` are written against the infinities, which are the one constant that is exact in every format, and `signbit` is a `bitcast` and a comparison against zero, because a negative zero compares equal to a positive one and the question is about the bits. The family is type generic the way the standard's macros are, so a `float` against a `long double` is compared as a `long double`, while the spellings ending in a width convert first and the difference shows: `__builtin_isinff(1e300)` is one and `__builtin_isinf(1e300)` is zero. Each is folded in the front end as well, so `int flag = __builtin_isinf(1.0 / 0.0);` at file scope is an initializer with a value rather than a program that is refused. The gcc.c-torture execution suite goes from 1630 of 1772 to 1648.

## 0.3.2

### Added

- The library builtins, the family where GCC's answer is the library function of the same name with the `__builtin_` prefix taken off. `__builtin_abort` was an undeclared identifier, and a call to a `__builtin_` this compiler had never heard of was stopping 510 of the 1773 files in the gcc.c-torture execution suite, 349 of them on `__builtin_abort` alone, because announcing a wrong answer is what a test in that suite does. Forty one of them are declared now, out of the same `features.toml` the rest of the matrix comes from: the memory and string family, the allocation family, `abs` and its widths, the `printf` family whose type can be written without naming `FILE`, and `abort` and `exit`. A row carries the name of the function it is, and `rucc_sema::library_name` is what turns the name in the program into the name in the object file, so `__builtin_strlen(s)` reaches the IR as `call @strlen(%0)` while the declaration keeps the spelling the program wrote, which is the one a diagnostic about the call has to say. A program writes the prefixed name to reach the function the C library promises where a macro or a definition of its own has taken the plain one, and that is the meaning of the call whether or not anything folds it: gcc folds several of these when the arguments allow it, and folding is an optimization on top of a call that was already right. The gcc.c-torture execution suite goes from 1203 of 1773 to 1625, which is 422 files off the exclusion list at once, and what the remaining 88 of that block are waiting on is now sorted into what each one actually needs: #223, #224, #225, #226, #227, #228, #229 and #230.

### Fixed

- A parameter a definition left unnamed, `int f(int a, int) { return a; }`, which was refused with "a function definition without a prototype is not supported yet". C23 6.7.7.4p1 lets the name be left out and gcc has taken one for far longer than that, and the object is passed either way. The front end dropped it for having no name, which left the list of what a definition takes one shorter than the list of types the function has, and that is what an old style definition looks like from the walk to the IR, so the walk read it as one and turned it down. An unnamed parameter is a declaration in the list now with nothing to say it by, which is what it is: an object the call passes that the body has no way of reading.

- `d = e = a[0] = c` where each of the four is a structure, which was refused with "this as an object to read or write is not supported yet". What an assignment is worth is the value it stored, and the value of an object is the object, so the middle one of a chain reads from the place the inner one wrote rather than from anything new. That makes the chain a sequence of copies out of the one source and needs no temporary anywhere in it, which is what it now lowers to.

- A string literal that fills the array it initializes exactly, which was refused with "an initializer that writes over an earlier one is not supported yet". C 6.7.10p14 puts the terminating null in only if there is room for it, so `"xyz"` in an array of three is the three characters, and the excess of a literal longer still is discarded, which is what gcc does after warning about it. The excess was being laid into the object anyway, so `const char a[2][3] = { "1234", "xyz" }` wrote five bytes into a row of three and then found the next row written over. The value of a literal filling an array is now the type of that array, which is where an entry that is a block copy says how many bytes it copies, so the cut is made once and where the rest of the initializer can see it.

- Five files come off the gcc.c-torture exclusion list between the three, and the suite goes to 1630 of 1772.

## 0.3.1

### Fixed

- `va_arg` of a structure or a union, which was refused with "va_arg of a structure or a union is not supported yet". Reading an aggregate off a variable argument list is what every function that takes one of those variadically does, `single-exec/00204.c` in c-testsuite is nine lines of it, and fifteen files across the corpus set were on the exclusion list for it. The intrinsic produced one value and an aggregate is not one, so the walk had the list and the type in hand and nowhere to put the answer. There is a second instruction for it now, `va_object`, which answers where the object is rather than what it is: `%1 = va_object %0, size 16, align 8`. An address is a place already, so nothing is copied out of it in the walk, and `struct s v = va_arg(ap, struct s);` is the one copy the initializer asks for and not two. The size and the alignment travel on the instruction because they are what the algorithm steps the list on by and what a target that has to put arriving registers somewhere needs to know, which is the copy each psABI's own description of `va_arg` makes. It is a second opcode rather than a wider reading of the first because the two cannot be told apart otherwise: an object the ABI puts in memory answers a `ptr` and so does `va_arg(ap, void *)`, and a rule that guessed between them from the size would be wrong on a packed structure the size of a pointer. With this, c-testsuite is at 220 of 220 with no exclusions at all, which is the first corpus here to get there. The gcc.c-torture execution suite goes from 1189 of 1773 to 1203 and the tcc suite from 83 of 87 to 84.

- A `goto` in a function that has a variable length array in it, which was refused outright with "a goto in a function with a variable length array is not supported yet". The stack an array like that sits on moves, so leaving the scope it was made in has to put the stack back, and the walk knew how to do that for every ordinary way out of a scope and not for a jump, because what the stack should be on arrival is decided by where the label is and a forward `goto` names a label the walk has not reached. Every scope has a name of its own now and the walk remembers, for each label and for each jump, which of them were open there, so the decision is made at the end of the function when both ends are known. A jump that leaves a scope that grew the stack gets a `stackrestore` in front of the branch already emitted, a jump to a label the array is still alive at is left alone, and a jump back to a label in front of the array gives the stack back every time round the loop, which is `again: { int a[n]; if (use(a)) goto again; }` and is the shape that leaks without it. Two scopes count as the same only when they are the same scope and grew the stack at the same point, which is what tells those last two apart. A jump into the scope of a variably modified declaration is a constraint violation, C11 6.8.6.1p1, and it is the front end that says so now, in gcc's words and at gcc's spans with a note on the label and a note on the declaration, which is what makes the lowering safe rather than lucky: a pointer to a variable length array is variably modified even though the pointer itself has the size every pointer has, so `int (*p)[n]` is in the rule as much as `int a[n]` is. A computed `goto` and an `asm goto` in such a function are still turned down, since neither can be given one answer that is right on every edge it has. `single-exec/00207.c` in c-testsuite, `78_vla_label.c` in the tcc suite and three files in the gcc.c-torture execution suite come off the exclusion list, and c-testsuite is down to a single exclusion.

- A structure passed to a variadic function past the end of its prototype, where the ABI puts the bytes of the object in the argument area, which was refused outright. `f1(f, &f, 2, 1, f, &f)` against an `int f1(struct foo, struct foo *, int, ...)` is the shape of it, and `single-exec/00140.c` in c-testsuite is fourteen lines of exactly that. Nothing about the classification was missing: it knew the bytes travel rather than an address and it knew their size and their alignment, and what it had nowhere to write down was that they do. The IR says bytes travel with a `byval`, a `byval` sits on a parameter of the signature, and an argument past the end of a parameter list has no parameter to sit on, so the walk gave up at the point where it already had the answer in hand. A call carries what the ABI asks of the arguments its signature does not name now, one entry for each of them, which is empty for every call whose extra arguments travel as the values in hand and is where the `byval` goes for the one that does not. The text writes it on the argument, `call @p(%0, %1, %2 byval(24, align 8)) : (ptr, ...) -> i32`, since the signature comes after the arguments and cannot be the place, and writing one on an argument the signature does name is turned down rather than letting one of the two answers quietly win. The verifier holds the list to one entry for each unnamed argument of a call that is variadic, each of them describing something an argument can do, which leaves `sret` out, because the space a return value goes to arrives before anything the function was called with and is a parameter. This was the only exclusion in the whole corpus set that named a platform: a structure that size travels as the address of a copy the caller makes under the Mac's ABI and as its own bytes in the argument area under Linux's, and only the second needs somewhere to say so, so the two now measure the same.

## 0.3.0

### Added

- Old style function definitions, `int add(a, b) int a; int b; { return a + b; }`, which the parser has always read and the checker refused outright. That is the only kind of definition C had before C89 added prototypes and it is what every file older than that is written in, so refusing it puts the compiler out of reach of a large part of the code it is meant to compile. The identifier list says the names and their order and nothing about types, 6.7.6.3p14, and the declarations written under it give the types in whatever order suits, 6.9.1p6, so a parameter is declared in two halves and the two can disagree in every way two lists can.
