Each of those is a sentence gcc has and each is now the sentence this gives, in its words: a name in the list twice, a declaration for a name the list does not have, a name declared twice, a parameter with an initializer, and a storage class that is not `register`, which is the one a parameter may carry. A name the declarations never mention is an `int`, which C89 said and which every dialect after it made a diagnostic, so it is silent under `-std=c89` and an error from `-std=c99` on. What the function takes is the declared types with the default argument promotions over them, which is what a caller of an unprototyped function hands over, while the body still sees each parameter as the type it was declared with. A prototype already in scope overrules the promoted types, because `int narrow(char);` over a definition of `narrow` written this way is the pairing all such code relies on and taking 6.7.6.3p15 literally would refuse it, and a parameter that matches neither what the prototype says nor what the promotions would make of it is still the mistake it looks like. C23 removed the form from the language and gcc kept accepting it with a warning that is on by default in that dialect alone, which is what this does, because the code written this way is not going to be rewritten. A call is laid out against the parameters where the argument facing one is compatible with it, which is the usual case and is what lets the call go to the name, and against what was actually written where it is not, since 6.5.2.2p6 checks a call against a prototype and against nothing at all otherwise and nothing converted the argument. `20001101.c` in the gcc.c-torture execution suite is the file that passes a zero where the definition wrote a pointer, which gcc marks as needing untyped assembly and which used to build IR the verifier would not take. One divergence is deliberate: a tag declared under the identifier list is not in scope in the body, which is what clang does and gcc does not, and nothing outside the definition can name such a tag anyway. The gcc.c-torture execution suite goes from 1153 of 1773 to 1186, and thirty three exclusions come off the list.

- The GNU line marker, `# 42 "file.c" 1 2`, which is the form `-E` writes and which was an error to read. A preprocessed file handed back to the compiler is full of them, so a compiler that cannot read its own output is not much of a compiler, and this one could not. It is `#line` with three differences. Nothing on it is macro expanded, because the tokens came from a preprocessor rather than from a person. Zero is a line number, because a generator counting from zero is allowed to say so where a person writing `#line 0` is not. And the flags may follow the name, of which `3` and `4` say nothing this phase acts on while `1` and `2` are a nesting: a `1` records the file entered, a `2` returns to one, and a `2` that names a file in neither the markers nor the real include stack is ignored with the warning gcc gives it, since that is a marker set describing a nesting nothing was ever in. Where gcc asks whether the file named is the one directly outside, this asks whether it is anywhere outside, because a `-E` that writes markers where its tokens are rather than where its files change leaves return markers out, this compiler's own included, and warning on every file would make the warning noise. Found by the chibicc test suite, which is the first thing to ask this compiler to read a line marker.

### Fixed

- A `constexpr` object, which was accepted and then not usable anywhere a constant is required. `constexpr int side = 4; int square[side * side];` was a variably modified type at file scope rather than an array of sixteen, which leaves the keyword promising that the initializer folds and nothing else. Being one of the things an integer constant expression may be built out of is the whole reason C23 added it, 6.6p8, and it is the difference between it and `const`: `const int n = 1; int a[n];` is a variable length array and has to stay one, so the folder cannot decide from the type and has to know whether the keyword was written. The declaration records that now, and folding a read of a named constant gives its initializer, which covers the two forms 6.6p8 lists, the constant itself and a member of one of a structure or union type. A subscript of one is not on that list and gcc 16 calls it variably modified as well, so it stays refused in the same words. The keyword implies `const`, 6.7.2p6, which is what makes `constexpr int c = 3; int *p = &c;` the discarded qualifier warning gcc gives it rather than silence, and an array gets the qualifier on its element because that is where 6.7.3p10 puts it. An enumerator is the one place a named constant is read with no conversion written around it, `enum e { four = side };`, and it had to ask for the read explicitly, since nothing before this folded an lvalue at all and so nothing ever had to tell an object apart from a read of one.

- A type refused as too large well before the largest object it could have been. `struct huge_struct { short buf[(1L << 62) - 256]; int a, b, c, d; };` was an error and gcc takes it, which `991014-1.c` in the gcc.c-torture execution suite is the file that asks about, since a type nothing is ever an object of is a type `sizeof` still has to answer about. A record was laid out by counting its bits in a `u64`, so the largest one it could reach was an eighth of the largest object C allows, and what produced the message was the multiply by eight overflowing rather than any rule saying so. A member now holds a byte offset and the bit within that byte, which is what every other compiler holds, and the bit position a record is being built at is counted in a `u128`, where eight times the largest object fits with room to spare. The limit is `PTRDIFF_MAX`, which is what C 6.5.6 needs it to be for a subtraction of two pointers into one object to have an answer, and it is stated once, on the target description, where the array bound and the record size both read it. It used to be written twice, once as a constant hard coding a 64-bit target and once as wherever the arithmetic happened to overflow. A record of exactly that many bytes is laid out and one byte more is refused, which is the line gcc 16 draws too, and a bit-field at the end of one has a bit offset no `u64` could have named.

- `__builtin_constant_p`, which was recognised and then treated as an ordinary function. In a block it lowered to `call @__builtin_constant_p`, which is a call to a symbol nothing defines and which no link would ever resolve, and in a constant expression it was refused as a non constant element, so `int a = __builtin_constant_p(1);` was an error where gcc gives it a one. It is answered in the front end now and never reaches the IR, with a one where the argument folds there and a zero otherwise. gcc folds it after optimization, so its answer for an argument that is not written as a constant can differ between `-O0` and `-O2`, and this gives the same answer at every level, which is the answer every small compiler gives and the one glibc's headers are written against. A string literal is a constant and the address of an object is not, which is measured on gcc 16 rather than reasoned about. The argument is checked and then dropped, so `__builtin_constant_p(i++)` leaves `i` alone, which is also what gcc does with it. `__builtin_choose_expr` is what this unblocks: its first argument has to be a constant expression, the idiom in real headers is the two of them together, and `pr19449.c` in the gcc.c-torture execution suite is the file that asks for exactly that.

- A byte in a string literal that is not part of a character, which was replaced rather than kept. A source file is UTF-8 and an identifier in it is text, but the body of a literal is bytes and does not have to be text at all, so `char s[] = "\xff";` written with the byte itself is a one byte array and gcc has always taken it. The lexer interned every spelling as a `String`, which is UTF-8 and has nowhere to put such a byte, so the byte became the replacement character and the array became three bytes long. That is the wrong object rather than a warning about a stray byte, and it stood even where the diagnostic was ignored. The interner takes a spelling that is not text now, keeping the bytes beside the lossy reading so that everything printing a name still gets a `&str` and the one caller that wants an object gets the bytes. A byte that is not part of a character anywhere but a literal is still an error, an identifier included, which is where gcc draws the same line. Found by `20000227-1.c` in the gcc.c-torture execution suite, which is three lines of exactly this.

- `packed`, `aligned` and `#pragma pack`, none of which reached the layout engine. The engine has taken all three since it was written and nothing ever handed them to it, so `struct s { char c; int i; } __attribute__((packed));` was eight bytes rather than five and a header that packs a wire format laid its fields at the wrong offsets, which is a miscompile rather than a missed optimization. The attributes are read off the record and off each member now, in both the `__attribute__` and the `[[gnu::]]` spellings, together with an `_Alignas` on a member, which C23 6.7.5 allows and which means what `aligned` means there. `#pragma pack` is read at the closing brace of a record body, which is the point gcc lays a record out and which is why a line written in the middle of a body settles the whole record rather than the members after it. The three do not compose the way the names suggest: `packed` drops an alignment to one, an explicit `aligned` raises whatever is left, so `packed, aligned(4)` gives four, and `#pragma pack` then caps the result, so a member written `aligned(8)` under `pack(2)` sits on a two byte boundary while the same number on the record itself is untouched because it is not a member's. Every one of those is a number read off gcc 16 on x86-64 rather than reasoned about, over thirty seven layouts covering bit-fields, unions, nested records, `push` and `pop` and the cases where an attribute asks for less than the record already had. A `pack` line that is not one is a warning in the words gcc uses for it and the line is dropped, which covers a missing parenthesis, a `pop` with no `push`, an alignment that is not a small power of two, an unknown action and junk at the end.

- A GNU attribute written among the declaration specifiers, which was read and then thrown away. `struct s { int x : 12; __attribute__((packed)) char y : 6; };` is where a member's `packed` is usually written, and the specifier list handed the attributes it had collected to the list it was building by assigning over it rather than joining onto it, so whatever the `__attribute__` keyword itself had put there was lost and only the `[[...]]` spelling and whatever the caller passed in survived. The two lists are joined now. Nothing before this could have noticed, since the only attributes that changed anything were the ones on a record's closing brace, and this landed alongside the work that made a member's matter.

- Where a bit-field goes when the record is packed, which is not what the shape of the old test said. A bit-field goes at the next free bit unless that would make it span more storage than its own type occupies, in which case it moves to the next boundary of its alignment, and packing takes that rule out. What the layout asked was whether an alignment had been lowered, which is a different question and gives the same answer only most of the time: `#pragma pack(4)` around `struct { char c; int b : 30; }` lowers nothing, because four is what an `int` wanted anyway, and gcc still leaves `b` at bit eight rather than moving it to bit thirty two. What gcc looks at is whether a maximum field alignment was set at all, so any of `packed` on the record, `packed` on the member and a `#pragma pack` of any number now counts. Measured, since the opposite reading is at least as plausible from the documents, and the same measurement is what settled it.

- A bit-field lying in more than eight bytes, which was refused outright. Packing is what puts one there: a field of sixty three bits pushed off a byte boundary covers nine, and tcc's two bit-field tests both do it, so the refusal turned up the moment `#pragma pack(push, 1)` started applying. The run is assembled sixteen bytes wide now, which is the widest an `__int128` bit-field can reach, and the pieces it is read and written in are unchanged: a run of nine bytes is still loaded as an eight and a one, so nothing asks a machine for a width it does not have.

- The head of a `for` loop, which opened a scope and never closed it. The marks a scope saves and restores are a stack, so a scope opened and not closed is not one mark left behind: it is every close after it taking the wrong mark off, which put the restore that belonged at the end of the loop body somewhere after the loop, restoring a stack pointer saved in a block that does not reach there. The verifier is what said so, and the case it said it about is a variable length array declared in a loop with a `continue` in it, which is tcc's `79_vla_continue.c`. A head is a scope in C 6.8.5p5 whether or not it declares anything, and it is now closed where the loop is left, which is the one place that can close it given that a `break` leaves the loop from the middle of the body.

- `static constexpr int x = 1;`, which was refused as multiple storage classes. C23 6.7.1 counts `constexpr` among the storage class specifiers and then says in the same clause that it may be written beside `auto`, `register` or `static`, so it is the one of them that is not exclusive with the rest, and holding it in the same field as the other five made every legal pairing an error. It is a flag of its own now, beside `_Thread_local`, and the four pairings that are wrong are refused one at a time in the words gcc 16 uses for each rather than by counting keywords: `constexpr` with `typedef`, `constexpr` with `extern`, `_Thread_local` with `constexpr`, and `auto` with `constexpr`. Which pairings are allowed is decided once the whole specifier list has been read, since `auto` beside a type is a storage class and `auto` alone is a deduced type and there is no telling which one is written until the list ends. Writing the keyword twice is a duplicate rather than a pairing and says so.

- A compound literal used as a value in a static initializer, which was called a non constant element and then, once it was not, had nowhere to go. C 6.7.11p4 gives a compound literal at file scope static storage duration, which is what makes `struct t a = { (struct s){ 2 }, 3 };` a constant initializer, and tcc's struct initializer test and c-testsuite's copy of it are both written that way. Two things were wrong. Reading an object is a node of its own, so the literal arrived as a read of a literal and the check for constancy stopped at the read, which is why `&(struct s){ 1 }` was fine and the same literal without the ampersand was not. And an image is bytes, so a literal that is a whole object had no datum to become, where now its own initializer is built at the offset the entry is at and the literal's bytes are laid into the image rather than a second object being named. A cast of a record to its own type belongs to the same case, since tcc writes one in the middle of that initializer, and the walk to the IR looks through it now the way it looks through a read.

- The object a compound literal at file scope is, which nothing emitted. `struct s *q = &(struct s){ 9 };` built an image naming `.Lanon.0` in a module that defined no such symbol, so the link is what would have found out. Nothing declares a compound literal, which is why the address of one is also the only thing that can ask for it to be emitted, and it asks now.

- An initializer for a flexible array member, which built an image larger than the object it was the image of and stopped the verifier. A structure whose last member is an array with no length is one size as a type and another as an object: `sizeof` answers without the array, and a definition that gives the array something to hold has to be large enough to hold it. C 6.7.2.1p18 leaves that size to the implementation, gcc grows the object, and this now grows it to the size of its image, which gives `struct { int i; int j[]; } x = { 1, { 2, 0, 2, 3 } };` the twenty bytes gcc 16 gives it rather than four bytes with twenty bytes written into them. A string literal filling one is no longer cut to the room that was left either, since there is no room left by that measure and the object is what grows. An array with a length of its own still cuts the literal down to it, which is the one case in C where a string initializer drops its terminator.

- A name that is not ASCII, which the IR printer wrote and the IR reader would not read back. C23 says an identifier may be written in any script and gcc has taken them for far longer, so `int été = 1;` is a program, and the reader stopped at the first byte above ASCII and said a name was expected. Nothing is decoded, because a name is a run of bytes either way and the file it came from was checked for being UTF-8 when it was read.

- An object of no size at all, which the IR printer wrote in a form the reader would not take. `unsigned char foo[1][0];` is an object with an image and the image has nothing in it, which is not the same as an object with no image, and the two have to read differently for the reader to tell a definition from a declaration. The empty one is now written `{}` and the reader looks for the brace before it asks for a piece, where it used to ask for a piece first and call the closing brace a type that does not exist.

- An `extern` declaration in a block that has a local of the same name in a block outside it, which was refused as a contradiction. C 6.2.2p4 hands `extern` the linkage of a visible prior declaration only where that declaration has a linkage of its own, and a local has none, so `int v = 4; { extern int v; }` leaves the inner declaration naming the object at file scope rather than making a second declaration of the local. The same pair written in one block is the contradiction, and that is still an error in the words gcc uses for it. The lookup now walks outwards past a declaration with no linkage instead of stopping at the first one it sees.

- A function declared `static` and then defined with nothing in front of it, which was called a contradiction and is how a great deal of real code is written. C 6.2.2p5 gives a function declared with no storage class the linkage `extern` would have given it, and `extern` takes whatever the declaration before it had, so the definition is a second declaration of the same static function rather than a disagreement about linkage. An object is where the two differ: a file scope `int j;` has external linkage of its own, so it does contradict a `static int j;` above it, and that case is still an error in the same words gcc uses. What the checker held was whether the keyword `extern` was written, which is the answer to the wrong question, and it now holds whether the declaration says nothing about linkage and so takes what is there.

- A crash on GNU's nested functions, which are the settled exception in `spec/13-gnu-compat.md` section 13.3 and which nothing in the compiler had ever turned down. A definition inside a block parses like any other definition, semantic analysis took it, and the walk to the IR had no name to give it other than the one it was written with, so a file with two functions each holding a nested `g` built a module with two symbols called `g` and the assertion that a module has one symbol per name fired. It is now an error where it is written, with a note saying why it is not coming: a call to a nested function goes through a trampoline written on the stack, and no target that enforces an unexecutable stack allows one. The name is declared without the body, so the calls under it resolve and one definition is one error rather than one for itself and one for every call. `-fnested-functions` gets the same sentence in place of the unknown option error, since a build reaching for the flag is asking for the feature and deserves to be told, and `-fno-nested-functions` is what this compiler does anyway and is accepted. Found by the gcc.c-torture execution suite, whose `20061220-1.c` is the file with the two of them.

- `#pragma once` in the file named on the command line, which was warned about and then thrown away. The warning is fair, since the line usually means a header that ended up being compiled on its own, but the case where it does work in a main file is the file that includes itself, and dropping the line there turned that file into a redefinition of everything in it. gcc warns and applies it, and so does this now.

- What the multiple-include optimization calls a file, which was the name the include used. One header is reached under several names on any project with more than one include directory, so `./tree/x.h` and `/abs/tree/x.h` were two files, each read in full, each with its guard recorded separately. The file system is now what says which file a name is, which leaves a preprocessor over a map from name to bytes with a text answer and gives the one over a real file system a canonical path, so a relative name, a `..` and a symlink all land on the same entry. Two hard links to one file are still two entries where gcc, which compares device and inode, sees one. The spec has claimed the identity rule since it was written and the implementation had not caught up.

- A branch into the middle of a loop or an `if` that nothing else reaches, which the walk to the IR turned down rather than lowered. Both shapes are legal C: a `goto` into a loop body, and a `case` on a statement inside a loop that sits in a `switch`, which is the family Duff's device belongs to without being the one member of it that already worked. What the walk does now is the same thing it does for a block it cannot fall into, which is to start a block nothing branches to and carry on building from there, so the loop is built with its own header and its own test and the label inside it is an ordinary edge into the body. What that builds ahead of the label is reached by nothing and goes out with the rest of the unreachable blocks at the end, which is machinery that was already there. This was the last thing in the way of c-testsuite 00213 and tcc's `87_dead_code.c`, and both are off the exclusion list.

- An attribute written just inside a grouping parenthesis, as in `int (ATTR *)(void)`, which was parsed as a type name for a function returning a function and refused three times over. An attribute is the one token that begins both of the things a parenthesis after a declarator can hold, since a parameter's declaration may start with one and so may the declarator inside a grouping parenthesis, and the parser decided on the first token and got the wrong one. What decides is the `*` or the name behind the attribute, and an attribute is more tokens than the four the parser is allowed to peek past, so the attributes are now stepped over and the cursor put back before the answer is given. Nothing is read during that step in the sense the cursor means it, since a speculative parse that reported a diagnostic or added a node would be describing a reading of the source that was abandoned. The attributes are then read properly and put on the pointer written after them, which is what they appertain to, and dropped when no pointer follows because a declarator has nowhere to carry one. This is the misparse c-testsuite 00210 exists to catch, and that case is off the exclusion list.

- A conditional with one void arm, which C says is not a conditional at all and which gcc accepts with a warning nobody sees outside `-Wpedantic`. The rule the standard writes is that both arms are void or neither is, and the rule real code is written against is gcc's: the whole expression is void the moment either arm is. That difference is not academic, because a statement expression ending in a `goto` has type void, and an arm of that shape against an `int` on the other side is how the dead code tests in tcc's suite write a branch that must not be generated, so the strict reading turned a file that compiles everywhere into an error about incompatible operand types. Using the value is still an error, since the result is void whichever arm produced it.

## 0.2.21

### Added

- The x86-64 lowering rules, one hundred and sixty seven of them, and the model that says what every term in them means. This is the first thing in M3 that is target work rather than infrastructure, and all of it is proved: one hundred and sixty six discharged at their own widths and one at bounded widths, in about eleven seconds. What they cover is the integer core. Constants, the six arithmetic and bitwise operations with a register and again with an immediate, the address forms `lea` computes without going to memory, negation and complement, signed and unsigned division and remainder, shifts by a constant and by a register, forty comparisons, and the conversions between widths. Several of them are rules that could not have been written a week ago, because a rule relating two widths is what per term widths were added for, and `movsx` is now a claim the solver checks rather than a comment. The one rule with a `(bounded "...")` clause is the sixty four bit multiply by an immediate, which z3 does not settle in ten seconds because it bit blasts two sixty four bit multiplies rather than using the guard, and the reason written on it is that what the rule turns on is the immediate rather than the multiply. That guard is written `(= k (sign_extend 32 64 (extract 31 0 k)))` rather than as a pair of range comparisons, because the range form scales into a guard that is true of everything when the rule is asked again at sixteen bits, and a vacuous guard is a bounded proof of nothing.

- Nothing has an effect yet. Loads, stores, branches and calls are rules whose claim includes what they do to memory or to control, and the rule language has to be able to say that before those rules can be written, so they follow with the selector rather than being written here in a form the verifier would have to take on trust.

- The model is one entry per instruction per operand size, so `addl` and `addq` are two entries, because a model whose entries do not stand one to one with instructions is a model nobody can check against a manual. Two hundred and forty six of them, half the IR side and half the machine side, and the IR half will move out of this file when a second target arrives. The parts of the machine that are easy to get wrong are the parts written out: an immediate on a sixty four bit operation is sign extended from thirty two, a shift takes its count modulo thirty two below sixty four bits and modulo sixty four at it, which is why the eight and sixteen bit shifts by a register mask the count first and are not the same rule as the wider ones, and a compare and the byte a condition sets are one term because the flags between the two instructions are not a value.

- The gate now also compiles every rule file into the matcher it will be matched with, since a rule that can never fire is a mistake whatever a solver says about it, and the one place the whole file is read at once is the place to find that out. `rucc-verify` reads the eight comparison operators under the solver's own names as well as under the symbols, so a rule that means the signed comparison can say so rather than depend on which way a table happens to read.

- Widths a rule can convert between, which is what the verifier held one of per rule until now and what every `sext`, `zext` and `trunc` in a lowering needs more than one of. A head that ends in `.iN` is that many bits wide, anything else is as wide as the term it sits inside, and a name is as wide as the place in the pattern that bound it, so `(add.i32 (value.i64 x) (value.i64 y))` is a thirty two bit add of two sixty four bit registers and both numbers are in the question that gets asked. The conversions themselves are written the way `spec/10-backend.md` writes them, `(sign_extend 32 64 x)` and `(extract 31 0 x)`, with the widths spelled out rather than inferred from context, and the RISC-V rule that document writes out is now a rule the crate discharges rather than an example it cannot state. When the machine term is wider than the IR term it replaces, which is what lowering a value into a wider register looks like, what is asked is that the two agree on the bits the IR term has, and what the rest of the register holds is left to the rule's own `spec` clause, because that clause is the only place a target's extension rule is written down and so the only place it can be checked. A replacement narrower than what it replaces loses bits and is refused rather than asked about. The widths are checked here rather than left to the solver, so two bitvectors of different sorts are a sentence naming both of them rather than a complaint from z3 about a line in generated text, and an opcode that names a width and means something of another width is a mistake in the model that is caught in the model. A bounded proof scales every width in a rule by the one ratio instead of flattening them onto a single number, so a rule asked again at eight bits is still the rule somebody wrote.

- The gate the milestone asks for: a rule the solver cannot discharge does not enter the rule set, and the count of bounded proofs is reported. `admit` is the front door, the `rucc-verify` program is what CI runs it from, and a file with anything in it that is not a proof is refused whole rather than having the failing rules quietly dropped, because a compiler built from the rules that happened to pass is a compiler nobody described. The other half is what to do when the solver shrugs. Some claims no bitvector solver settles in the time anybody will wait, and a division checked against a multiplication is the usual one: the rule the tests use is answered in hundredths of a second at eight bits and not in five seconds at sixteen. Such a rule may carry a `(bounded "...")` clause saying why a proof at narrower widths is enough, and then the shrug is answered by asking the same question again at four and eight bits, where every one of them has to come back `unsat`. The clause excuses nothing on its own: the rule is still asked at its own width first, a bounded proof is a verdict of its own rather than a discharge, and a tool that fell back to narrow widths without a written reason would turn every rule the solver is slow on into a rule nobody checked, which is the failure the whole crate exists to prevent. How many rules needed one is printed on every run, since that number going up is the signal worth watching. A counterexample found at a narrow width is reported as one, named with the width it was found at, because the two things it can be are a rule that is wrong and a rule whose constants do not fit in four bits and both are for a person to look at. An empty `rules/` is something the gate says out loud rather than passing quietly, which is what let it stand before there was a rule set and let the x86-64 rules land into it rather than after it.

- MIR, the machine level representation, with the printer and the parser that are its text form. This is the fourth piece of M3 and the one the rest of the milestone is written in terms of: the lowering rules produce it, the allocator rewrites it, and the encoder reads it. Nothing in the crate knows what any instruction means. An opcode is a name and an operand is a register with a class, a role and a constraint, which is everything a pass needs in order to move an instruction, split a live range or insert a spill, and it is what lets one crate hold the representation while each target holds its own opcodes, as `spec/10-backend.md` section 10.8 requires. An instruction is twenty four bytes, which is what section 10.1 asks for, and getting there without hiding anything in bit fields meant putting the successors on the block rather than on the terminator, which is the arrangement the allocator interface in section 10.4 wanted anyway, and leaving the span in a parallel array the way `rucc-ir` does. Before and after allocation are the same text form, so `--emit=mir` and `--emit=mir-final` are one printer and one reader, and both round trip byte for byte, which is what lets a test of the allocator state its input by writing one down. A virtual register says its class where it is written and nowhere else, because the class is a fact about the register rather than about each reading of it, and a text that can say it twice is a text that can say it two ways. The description of a target's registers lives in `rucc-target`, since the allocator and the ABI read the same description the printer and the parser do.

- `rucc-verify`, which hands the same rule text to a bitvector solver, and the machine model it needs to do it. This is the third piece of M3 and the one the milestone exists to test: if rule based lowering with SMT verification does not work in practice, this is where that is found out. A rule is turned into the negation of two claims at once. What its pattern means and what its replacement means have to agree, both read out of the model rather than out of anybody's description of them, and the rule's own `spec` clause has to hold as well. The second is not redundant, because the clause is written by hand and a rule whose stated claim is not what its pattern means would otherwise be checked against its own mistake, and there is a test that is exactly that rule. A guard is an assumption rather than part of the claim, which is what makes a rule that only holds for some constants provable at all. Nothing but `unsat` is a pass: a solver that gives up is reported as having given up rather than folded into either answer. The solver is a program found on PATH rather than a crate, because a bitvector solver in the dependency tree would be the largest thing in it and would have to hold the same 1.85 floor the workspace holds. Every head a rule uses needs an entry in the model, and one that has none is an error rather than an unchecked assumption, which is Crocus's stated tax being paid from the first rule.

- The matcher a rule set compiles into, which is the second piece of M3. A pattern is a tree and so is the term it is matched against, and the obvious way to do that is a chain of conditionals per rule, which is what `spec/10-backend.md` says not to build: with several hundred rules per target it tests the same opcode hundreds of times and it puts the order the rules are tried in beyond anybody's control. What is built instead is a trie over the flattened patterns, so the test that a term is an `add.i64` happens once however many rules begin with one, and specificity is the shape rather than a sort, because the concrete tests at a node are tried before the wildcard. That is the maximal munch the document asks for and the first match wins rule that `-O0` is defined to use, and both are properties of the structure rather than of a comparison function somebody has to keep right. A rule whose pattern an earlier rule already covers can never fire and is refused rather than dropped, since it is always either a rule that was meant to say something else or one that should not be there.

- The language the lowering rules are written in, and the reader that turns a file of them into something a compiler of rules can work on. This is the first piece of M3, and it comes first because everything else in that milestone is written in it: the roughly one hundred and fifty rules that get x86-64 off the ground, the automaton `rucc-rules` compiles them into, and the specifications `rucc-verify` discharges are all the same text. The two rules `spec/10-backend.md` writes out are the two the tests read, so the language the document describes and the language the crate accepts are checked against each other rather than merely intended to agree. Three things the examples in that document leave open are settled here: a bare name is a variable and a parenthesised one is an application, which is why a constructor taking nothing is still written `(result)`; a name may be bound only once in a pattern, since two occurrences would ask the matcher for an equality test it does not have; and the `spec` clause is part of the grammar, so a rule without one is a syntax error rather than an unverified rule. A malformed rule costs one message rather than turning the rest of the file into noise, because the reader skips to the next `(rule` and carries on.

## 0.2.20

### Added

- Twenty three programs that must not compile, which is the half of the accept suite that was missing and the last thing standing between M2 and its exit criterion. Each of them was run through gcc 16 in every dialect it is rejected in, and the sentence in its `message` directive is the one gcc prints rather than the one rucc happened to print, so the four places the two disagreed came out as fixes below rather than as expectations written around a divergence. What they cover is the ordinary mistakes: a duplicate member, an array of functions, a negative bound, an object of an incomplete type, two storage classes, a redefinition, a value returned from a `void` function and a missing one from the other kind, a void value read, a non constant static initializer, an undefined label, an assignment to a constant, `break` and `continue` and `case` with nothing to belong to, a call of something that is not a function, a member of an `int`, a subscript of one, and both argument count mismatches. The suite already ran every case under all ten dialects, so these are two hundred and thirty runs.

- `__builtin_ceil`, `__builtin_floor` and the `f` and `l` widths of both, which no header declares and which SQLite calls directly under `__GNUC__`. That is the path every build of it that is not MSVC takes, so the two names were the last thing standing between the compiler and SQLite's shell, which now gets through the front end whole. They are declared and not yet folded, the same as the rest of the table, so `__has_builtin` still answers no for them and a header that asks takes its fallback path.

- `__func__`, and gcc's `__FUNCTION__` and `__PRETTY_FUNCTION__` alongside it. C99 declares the first as if a function body began with `static const char __func__[] = "who";`, and none of the three was implemented at all, so every one of them was an undeclared name. That is more than a missing convenience: glibc's `assert.h` writes `__assert_fail(..., __func__)`, so a program that includes it and calls `assert` does not compile, and it is thirty nine of the errors in SQLite's `shell.c` on its own. Each is answered with a string literal whose element type is `const char`, which is what makes `__func__[0] = 'x'` the read-only error gcc gives and leaves the rest to the rules a string already has: an lvalue of array type that decays, `sizeof __func__` as the length of the name plus one, and an address that is an object's. Two mentions of one spelling in one function are one object and the three spellings are three, which is what gcc emits and what a program comparing two of them can see. Outside a function gcc warns and hands back the empty string rather than refusing the file, and so does this. Checked against gcc 16 on the value, the size, the `_Generic` that picks `const char (*)[n]`, the assignment, all three spellings and the use at file scope.

- The three C11 encoding prefixes are available in the GNU dialects from `gnu99` on, which is where gcc offers them, and were available nowhere before C11. A `u8` string is now an array of `char` before C23 and an array of `unsigned char` from C23, which is the type `char8_t` names and is what a `_Generic` against gcc 16 answers in both directions. The one prefix gcc does not offer early is `u8` on a character constant: that is C23 in every dialect, `gnu23` included, and it stays that way here.

- `#line` is applied rather than only recorded. `__LINE__` and `__FILE__` answer with the presented position, a diagnostic prints it, and `-E` writes the marker the directive turns into. This is what a generated parser needs: bison writes `#line` back to the grammar it was given, and an error in the generated C that names the generated file is an error nobody can act on. What does not move is where the bytes are, so the text a caret is drawn under still comes out of the real file at the real offset. The marker goes where the directive was written and not where its effect first shows, because those are two different places: a header included from below a `#line` is added to the source map after the file that includes it, so ordering markers by position would print the rename after the header instead of before it. Checked against gcc 16 on six shapes, including the number arriving from a macro, a directive with nothing after it, and two in a row.

- Seventeen more of the macros gcc predefines, all of them ones a header reads and all of them true here. `__MMX_WITH_SSE__` is the one that was doing damage: gcc's own `xmmintrin.h` reads it to decide how to write `_mm_maskmove_si64`, so a compiler that leaves it undefined is handed a different function body than gcc is, which is what five of the header sweep's failures were. The rest are `__FXSR__` and `__code_model_small__` and the two `__ATOMIC_HLE_*` bits on x86, the four `__GCC_HAVE_SYNC_COMPARE_AND_SWAP_*` widths that are true now that the `__sync_*` builtins exist, `__FINITE_MATH_ONLY__` as zero, `__GCC_IEC_559` as two, the two hardware interference sizes, the two execution charset names, `__REGISTER_PREFIX__` and `__GXX_ABI_VERSION`. `__GCC_IEC_559_COMPLEX` is deliberately not among them, because it is the same claim about complex arithmetic and complex multiplication and division are not lowered.

- `#pragma push_macro("NAME")` and `#pragma pop_macro("NAME")`, which were neither acted on nor consumed. A push saves what the name means and a pop puts it back, and the pair is how a header defines a name for its own use without taking the caller's away: clang's `__clang_cuda_complex_builtins.h` opens with a push of `__DEVICE__` and closes with the pop. Both spellings work, since a `#pragma` cannot be written in a macro body and `_Pragma("push_macro(\"X\")")` is the only form a macro has. A name with no definition pushes the absence, because the pragma is about restoring the state and not being defined is a state. A pop with nothing pushed is silent, since the two are written in pairs across headers that do not know about each other. `#pragma GCC push_macro("X")` is deliberately still passed through and still does nothing, which is what gcc does with it.

### Fixed

- A call with the wrong number of arguments says how many it wanted. gcc writes "too few arguments to function 'g'; expected 2, have 1" and rucc stopped after the name, which leaves the reader counting a parameter list that is usually in another file. A variadic function says "expected at least 2", since the number that can be checked is the number of named parameters. The generic builtins keep the short form, because that is the form gcc uses for them.

- Two declarations of one name in a block are a redefinition when both of them give a value and a redeclaration with no linkage otherwise. rucc said the second of those for every pairing, so `int x = 1; int x = 2;` inside a function was told about linkage, which is not what is wrong with it. gcc draws the line at whether both wrote an initializer, and the seven pairings it can be written in were measured before the check was moved. Conflicting types still win over both, which is also gcc's order.

- A declaration that names two storage classes says "multiple storage classes in declaration specifiers", which is gcc's sentence and names neither of them, instead of naming both in a sentence nothing else prints. Two of the same keyword is a duplicate rather than a conflict, which is the other half of what gcc says. The span stays on the second keyword, which is the one to delete.

- A function type and an array type are spelled hard against their suffix. gcc writes `char(int)` and `int[4]`, because there is nothing between the type and the declarator to separate, and writes `int (*)[4]` with the space because there is. rucc put a space in all three, so a message quoting a type did not match the one gcc quotes for the same program. All eight shapes in the differential now match byte for byte, and the typed tree's textual form reads the same printer, so the golden files move with it.

- A `return` in the wrong shape follows the dialect. C89 let a function return without the value it promised and let a `void` function return one anyway, and gcc still takes both at `-std=c89`, silently for the first and with a warning for the second. rucc made both an error in every dialect, so a program that only builds at c89 did not build. From C99 on they are errors, which is unchanged.

- A call to a function that a prototype further down the file contradicts. `int f(); int use(void) { return f(1, 2, 3); } int f(int a, int b) { return a + b; }` was refused with an internal error, and gcc compiles it with nothing said. The call is checked against the unprototyped declaration in scope where it was written, which names no parameters and so runs no arity check, and the prototype arriving below is what the function ends up with, which left a two parameter function and a three argument call to it in the same module. The walk now asks the declaration what the file settled on: where the arguments travel the way the settled prototype wants, the call goes to the name with the settled signature, and where they do not it goes through the function's address, which is the shape a call through a function pointer already needs. The second half of the same gap was `int f(); f(); int f(void) {}`, which is ordinary old code and was refused as well and is now a direct call. A case is undefined behaviour if control ever arrives at it and that is the programmer's to answer for; refusing to translate the file is not.

- A qualifier on a parameter belongs to the object and not to the function type. `void f(const int x) { x = 1; }` compiled with nothing said, where gcc and clang both call it an assignment to a read-only parameter, and the same gap let a `const` parameter be an `asm` output. C drops the qualifiers from a parameter when one declaration of a function is compared with another, which is a rule about the type, and the object inside the body keeps them; the checker was dropping them from both. The two are now built separately, and which of them an array parameter gets is decided by the type rather than by the brackets, because `typedef int matrix[2][3];` puts an array parameter there with no brackets to read. The other half of the same rule was wrong in the other direction: `void f(int a[const 3])` had type `void (int *const)`, so it did not match the `void f(int *)` written next to it, which is c-testsuite 00162 and is what gcc answers a `_Generic` on. A message about one of these now says `parameter` rather than `variable`, which is gcc's wording and sends the reader to the parameter list instead of into the body.

- Designators out of order in a static initializer. `struct S { int a; int b; }; struct S s = { .b = 2, .a = 1 };` at file scope was refused with "an initializer that writes over an earlier one is not supported yet", and nothing was written over: the two designators name different members, and which order they are written in is the programmer's to choose. The image was being built by walking the entries as written and calling any backwards offset an overlap, so it now sorts them by offset first. The sort is stable, which is what settles the case that really is an overlap: naming one place twice is legal, the last of them stands, and among the entries at one offset the written order is kept and all but the last are dropped. A bit-field named twice takes only the bits of the field rather than the two values or-ed together, which it did before. The same initializer inside a function always worked, so this was file scope only. Checked against gcc 16's assembly on eleven shapes, including `[3] = 4, [1] = 2`, a designator resumed from with `[2] = 3, 4`, an excess element after a designator, and bit-fields both out of order and repeated. The cases are c-testsuite 00048, 00049, 00147 and 00148, which come off the exclusion list in the compatibility repository.

- A directory named twice on the include path is searched once. The driver adds the standard directories after the ones the command line asked for, so `-I/usr/include` or a `-isystem` naming a directory that is already there put a second entry on the path, and a second entry is not harmless: `#include_next` means the next directory after the one holding the file, so a header found in the first copy of `/usr/include` resolved its own `#include_next` to the second copy and included itself, and `__has_include_next` answered yes where gcc answered no. clang's `stdint.h` and `endian.h` and its `zos_wrappers` headers are written on that answer, and all twenty of the remaining failures in the glibc header sweep were this. A `-I` naming a system directory is the entry that goes rather than the system one, which is gcc's documented rule and the reason the check runs once over the finished path rather than as each directory is pushed.

- Three features the dialect did not gate: `//` comments, a declaration in a `for` header, and the `'` digit separator. All three were accepted in every dialect, so `-std=c89` took a program gcc refuses, which is the whole point of asking for c89. `//` is C99 and gcc offers it in gnu89, so the one dialect without it is c89, where it is now an error said once a file and the comment is skipped anyway rather than turning into a syntax error on every line. A declaration in a `for` header is C99 and gcc refuses it in gnu89 too, which is one of the places a GNU dialect is not the iso one with more allowed. The digit separator is C23 and is not a GNU extension, so `1'000'000` before C23 is a number, a multi-character character constant and another number, which is how gcc reads it and why it does not parse. The three cases in the accept suite had a gap on eleven dialect pairs between them and now have none, so `KNOWN_GAPS` goes from 27 to 16.

- Two pointers whose pointees are the same integer type with the other sign are a warning and not an error. They point at the same bytes, and the code that writes one is usually reading a byte buffer rather than confusing two types, which is why gcc calls it `-Wpointer-sign` and keeps the rest of the mismatches an error. Rank and not width decides which of the two it is, so `unsigned char *` to `char *` is the warning and `long long *` to `long *` is still the error even though the two are the same width here. The wording is gcc's for all four of assignment, argument, initialization and return. gcc keeps the warning off until `-Wall` or `-pedantic` asks for it and there is no such switch here yet, so it is always on.

## 0.2.19

### Added

- `__CHAR8_TYPE__`, in C23 and in no dialect before it, which is where gcc defines it. It is the type behind `char8_t`, and gcc's own `stdatomic.h` declares `atomic_char8_t` under an `#ifdef` on it, so a compiler that never defines it is short a type in C23 and one that defines it everywhere declares a type gcc does not in C17. This was found by moving the reference compiler in the differential job from the GCC 13 the runner ships to the current GCC 16, which is the release that added it.

### Fixed

- The integer limit macros are spelled in hexadecimal, which is how gcc spells them. `__INT_MAX__` was `2147483647` and is now `0x7fffffff`, and the same for the whole family down to `__SCHAR_MAX__` and out to `__UINT_FAST64_MAX__`. The value and the type were never in question, since `0x7fffffff` and `2147483647` are both `int`, and the spelling is, because these macros reach a program's text: glibc's `limits.h` writes `#define INT_MAX __INT_MAX__`, openssl's `err.h` writes `((unsigned int)INT_MAX + 1)`, and `-E` over that header printed a decimal number where gcc printed a hexadecimal one.

- `__UINT8_C` and `__UINT16_C` no longer append `U`. `uint8_t` and `uint16_t` are narrower than `int`, so an integer promotion turns them into a signed `int` and `UINT8_C(1)` has that type, in gcc and in the standard's own words. The `U` was not a harmless extra: `UINT8_C(1) - 2` came out as four billion odd instead of minus one, and a `_Generic` on it picked the unsigned arm.

- `__DECIMAL_DIG__` expands to the number rather than to `__LDBL_DECIMAL_DIG__`. The value is the same, since `long double` is the widest format here, but the two are not the same thing to read and a program that undefines the other macro took this one with it. gcc writes the number.

- `-E` no longer puts a space between two tokens the person wrote next to each other. The rule that stops output from reading back as something else was being asked about every adjacent pair, and the answer for a number followed by a sign is yes because `1e` and `-5` written together are one token. But `52` and `-` came out of the lexer as two tokens and read back as two, so nothing has to go between them, and the kernel's `sound/asound.h` writes an array bound as `[52-2*sizeof(struct timespec)]`. The question is now asked only where the two tokens came out of different macros, which is the only way one can put together two things that were never together, and is where GCC asks it too. Different means the whole chain rather than the outermost call, because the outermost is the same for every token of a nest and the boundaries inside it are real: lz4 writes `LZ4_HASHLOG` as `(LZ4_MEMORY_USAGE-2)` over a `LZ4_MEMORY_USAGE` of 14, and gcc prints `(14 -2)`. Measured over the 1788 headers of the glibc corpus, the two rules together take the count that matches gcc from 982 to 993.

- A `#pragma` written with a space after the hash prints back without one. glibc indents a directive one space per level of conditional it sits inside, so `regex.h` writes `# pragma GCC diagnostic push`, and gcc prints that back as `#pragma GCC diagnostic push`. The rest of the line keeps whatever spacing it was written with, which is also what gcc does.

- `__STDC_NO_VLA__` is no longer defined, because variable length arrays work. A conditional feature macro is a claim not to have something, and this one was not true. It is not a harmless overstatement of caution either: glibc's `regex.h` writes the bound of `regexec`'s match array as `_REGEX_NELTS (__nmatch)`, which expands to the parameter when the dialect has variable length arrays and to nothing at all when a compiler says it does not, so the claim was quietly changing the declaration of a function in a header rather than turning a feature off. The other three stay, each because it is still true: there is no `stdatomic.h` to include, no `threads.h`, and complex arithmetic is not lowered.

## 0.2.18

### Added

- The library's headers, which the compiler did not look for and could not be told to look for except one `-isystem` at a time. A hosted implementation is two halves, the nine headers the compiler ships and the several hundred the library does, and until now only one half was on the path: `#include <stdio.h>` failed on every machine. gcc settles this when it is built, which it can do because a gcc is built for the machine it will run on, and this compiler is one binary that runs wherever it is copied, so it asks the machine. Per platform that is one list of candidates of which the ones that are there are taken. On Linux, `/usr/local/include`, then the multiarch directory named after the target's own libc, then `/usr/include`, which is gcc's order and every distribution's layout. On an Apple platform the SDK and nothing else, since a Mac has had no `/usr/include` since the command line tools stopped installing one, found from `-isysroot`, then `SDKROOT`, then by asking `xcrun`, then where the command line tools put it. On Windows whatever `INCLUDE` names, because nothing there is in a fixed place and the environment `vcvarsall.bat` sets is what every compiler on that platform reads. Cross compiling to another operating system offers nothing without a sysroot on purpose: this machine's `/usr/include` describes this machine's library, and handing it to a program being built for elsewhere turns an `#include` that could not be resolved into a declaration that is quietly wrong. With these, `rucc --emit=ir sqlite3.c` needs no flags at all where it used to need the SDK spelled out, and 208 of the 220 programs in the c-testsuite type check, lower to IR, pass the verifier and round trip through the IR printer, where the count was 149 and every one of the rest failed on a header nobody could find. On a glibc machine the headers are found and then one typedef stopped them being used: glibc writes `typedef float _Float32;` for a compiler claiming anything older than gcc 7 and `_Float32` is a keyword here, which is why the version claimed by default moved to 7 in the entry below.

- `--sysroot=<dir>` and `-isysroot <dir>`, the two spellings of the same thing, which put the library's directories under `<dir>` instead of under the root.

- `--print-config` now ends with the include search path, one `include:` line per directory in the order they are searched, marking which of them are system directories. That is what document 04 said it printed and what a person diffing a build against gcc's reaches for first.

- The builtins whose type comes from the call rather than from a prototype: the whole `__atomic_*` family, the older `__sync_*` one it replaced, and `__builtin_add_overflow` and its relatives. There is no signature to write down for these, because `__atomic_load_n` on an `int *` returns an `int` and on a `long *` in the same file returns a `long`, and the declaration a call needs is the one its own arguments describe. So each is a row in a small table saying what its parameters mean, and a call builds the function type it wants out of the object its first argument points at and then goes through the same argument checking as any other call. A program that declares one of these names itself still wins, since the rule only applies where the lookup finds nothing, which is how a header that writes its own `__sync_synchronize` keeps working. The arguments that are not the object are checked for what they have to be rather than left alone: the count against the row, a pointer where a pointer is required, an integer where an integer is required, and a pointer to an integer for the place `__builtin_add_overflow` stores its result.

- `__extension__` in front of a declaration. The keyword was already accepted where an expression is expected and rejected everywhere else, which is the wrong half: glibc writes it in front of every declaration in its headers that mentions `long long`, so a parser that stops at it stops at `stdlib.h`. It cannot be the keyword alone that decides which of the two it is, because `__extension__ int x;` is a declaration and `__extension__ (x + 1);` is an expression statement and both are legal in the same place, so the decision looks past a run of them at the token that follows. It still suppresses no warnings, which waits on `-pedantic` being wired through, the same thing the expression form is waiting on.

### Changed

- The GCC version claimed by default is 7.0.0, where it was 4.2.1. glibc gates most of what it hands a caller on `__GNUC_PREREQ`, so the claim decides which half of `sys/cdefs.h` a program gets, and below seven `bits/floatn-common.h` writes `typedef float _Float32;` over a keyword this compiler already has, which stops every header that reaches it. Measured against Ubuntu 24.04's glibc 2.39, 180 of its 214 headers preprocessed and parsed clean at the old claim and 202 do at the new one, the amalgamated SQLite goes from four errors to none, and the c-testsuite goes from 148 of 220 to 207 of 220 on that machine. The macOS SDK is unmoved at 336 of 363, so nothing was traded for it. Thirteen and sixteen were measured alongside seven and came out identical everywhere, so the next move up is cheap; it stays at seven because claiming a version whose promises have not been kept means being handed syntax the compiler cannot parse, and nothing yet needs more. `-fgnuc-version=` still sets it to anything, including back down.

## 0.2.17

### Added

- The headers a compiler has to ship itself, which is what stood between this compiler and any real program. A hosted C implementation is two halves, and only one of them is the library: `<stdarg.h>` is the target's calling convention, `<limits.h>` and `<float.h>` are the target's types, `<stddef.h>` is the ABI, and no library can write any of them. Nine are here, `float.h`, `iso646.h`, `limits.h`, `stdalign.h`, `stdarg.h`, `stdbool.h`, `stddef.h`, `stdint.h` and `stdnoreturn.h`, and they are compiled into the binary rather than installed beside it, so the compiler does not have to find its own installation before it can preprocess a file and a single static binary works wherever it is copied. They appear on the search path under the name `<builtin>`, spelled with the angle brackets because no directory a user can create is spelled that way, which is what makes it impossible for a real path to shadow them or to be shadowed by them. The position is gcc's: after every `-isystem`, so a directory the user named outranks ours, and before the library's, so ours outranks the library's. `-nostdinc` drops the entry along with the standard directories, since the two halves are a pair and half a pair is worse than none. `limits.h` and `stdint.h` chain onward with `#include_next` when the system has its own, because on a hosted implementation the library's is what the rest of the library was written against, and glibc's `limits.h` reads `_GCC_LIMITS_H_` to decide whether the compiler's has already been seen, so ours sets that name before the chain rather than after it, which is what stops the two headers including each other. `stddef.h` implements the `__need_` protocol in full, because glibc does not include it whole: `<stdio.h>` asks for `size_t` and `NULL` alone and would be wrong to receive `offsetof` as well. Every typedef is guarded under all the names the platform libraries use for the same job, glibc's, musl's, Darwin's and the BSD ones, since a second typedef of the same type is legal C11 but an error under `-std=c99 -pedantic-errors` and musl's `<bits/alltypes.h>` will write one otherwise. With these on the path the SQLite amalgamation preprocesses from end to end against the host's own libc headers, three megabytes of output and not one diagnostic, which it could not do at all before. What is not here is `stdatomic.h`, `stdbit.h` and `stdckdint.h`, each of which needs builtins that do not exist yet, and `unreachable()` in `stddef.h` for the same reason.

- `-nostdinc`, which drops the standard include directories and the compiler's own along with them.

- The twenty width macros, `__INT_WIDTH__`, `__LONG_WIDTH__`, `__SIZE_WIDTH__` and the rest of them, which C23's `limits.h` and `stdint.h` are written out of and which nothing here defined. Twenty is gcc's set exactly, checked against it: there is no exact width member, because the width of an `int32_t` is in its name, and there is no unsigned member of any pair, because a signed type and its unsigned counterpart are the same width and a header that wants `UINTMAX_WIDTH` writes `__INTMAX_WIDTH__`. Each of them comes off the target description rather than off a table, so `__LONG_WIDTH__` is thirty two on Windows, `__WINT_WIDTH__` is sixteen there and thirty two elsewhere, and `__INT_FAST16_WIDTH__` is sixty four on x86-64 glibc and thirty two on x86-64 musl, which is the same split the type macros already had and the one a program that switches on the width would otherwise get wrong.

- `__int128_t` and `__uint128_t`, gcc's two other spellings of the 128 bit integers, which are keywords here for the reason `__builtin_va_list` is: the compiler is the only thing that can name the type and no header of ours declares them. gcc predeclares them as typedefs instead, which shows in the one place a program would have to work at to reach, `unsigned __int128_t x;`, where gcc reads the typedef name as the declarator and complains about the `x` while we say there are two data types in the specifiers. Both are errors and neither is a program. What made these worth having is that Apple's `<mach/arm/_structs.h>` declares the NEON register state with `__uint128_t` and there is no way to include anything at all on that platform without going through it.

- The declarations of the builtins, which nothing had and which no header can supply. `__builtin_clzll` is called without being declared and without including anything, because the reserved prefix is what says the name belongs to the implementation, so the implementation has to declare it and until now this one did not. The type comes out of `features.toml`, the same table `__has_builtin` answers from, as a prototype written on the row: one table rather than two, since two lists of the same builtins are two lists that disagree. It is a string rather than a structure because `size_t` is a different type on two targets and that table has no target, and the words it may be written in are a closed set checked by that crate's build script, so a typo fails a build rather than somebody's compile. Forty five builtins have one, which is the bit manipulation family in all three widths, the `mem` and `str` family, the ones that name an address or a size, and the floating point constants. A builtin whose type depends on what it is handed deliberately has none, since `__builtin_constant_p` takes anything and `__builtin_add_overflow` takes three types that have to agree, and a fixed type there would be a worse answer than no answer. The declaration is made the first time the name is looked up and not found rather than before the first line is read, which is where gcc makes all of them, and it goes in the file scope wherever the call was, so it does not go away when the block that called it closes. Nothing folds one yet, which is why every row stays unimplemented in the matrix and `__has_builtin` still answers no: a call reaches the IR as a call to a name no object file defines, and the answer that sends a header down its fallback path is the honest one until it does not. With these the SQLite amalgamation type checks from end to end, no errors and no warnings, which is the first time any real program has.

- A `?:` whose value is a structure or a union, which until now said it was not something that could be read or written. The answer is the address of whichever arm was taken, carried to the join as a block parameter, and not a copy of the arm into a third object. C makes the value an rvalue that cannot be assigned to and both arms are objects that outlive the full expression, so there is nothing a copy could make observable, and `c ? x : y` on a structure of any size lowers to two jumps and no `memcpy` at all. SQLite's parser generator writes one of these, which is what asked for it. The two conditionals now share the shape they always had in common, the condition, one block per arm and a join that whichever arms came back branch to, so an arm that does not return is thrown away rather than joined in both of them.

### Fixed

- A null pointer constant in a static initializer no longer stops the compiler. `void *p = 0;` built an integer immediate of pointer type and panicked on the way out, because an immediate takes its width from its type and a pointer has none of its own: the width of an address belongs to the target and not to `ptr`. An image is bytes and every scalar in one has to say how many of them it takes, so the constant goes in as the integer it is at the width the target's addresses have, which leaves an address the linker fills in as the only pointer an image can hold. The first attempt kept the pointer type and widened only the immediate, which measured the scalar at zero bytes and dropped the value silently, so `(char *) 4096` would have emitted eight zero bytes and been wrong code rather than a crash. The verifier now rejects a scalar whose type has no width, so the invariant is checked rather than remembered.

- `extern const int x;` no longer fails the verifier. The rule was that a global with no image cannot be constant, and a global with no image is a declaration of an object some other module defines: whether writing through a pointer to it is undefined is a fact about the object rather than about which module holds its bytes. glibc declares `sys_errlist` and `sys_nerr` exactly this way and so does every library that exports something it does not want written to.

- A `#pragma` no longer reaches the parser as a stray `#`. The preprocessor leaves the line alone on purpose, since what a pragma means is not phase 4's business, so phase 7 is the first place that can take it out, and until now it did not: the `#` arrived at a parser whose grammar has no place for one, which is a syntax error on any header that packs a structure. The line is now recorded beside the token stream rather than in it, with the index of the token it stood before, because a pragma can appear between any two tokens at all and leaving it in the stream would mean every rule in the parser had to know about a token that is in no production. Nothing acts on one yet. The record is there so that when something does, `#pragma pack` first, the position it has to be applied at has not already been thrown away. `_Pragma("...")` lands in the same place, and what follows one now starts a line even when the source wrote it in the middle of one, since a pragma is a line and the thing after it is not part of it.

- Casting a constant to a pointer no longer warns about the integer being of a different size. The warning is about an address that was truncated on its way into the pointer, and a constant is a number the program chose rather than an address it was given, so nothing was lost. `(void *) 0` is what `NULL` expands to and `(D) -1` is how a library writes a sentinel, which between them are most of the casts a program makes, and SQLite alone took 49 of these. gcc has the same exemption and applies it after folding, so `(void *) (1 + 2)` is exempt and `(void *) (x ? 1 : 2)` is not, and now the same is true here. Casting a pointer to an integer is unchanged, since that one warns about a constant too and gcc warns about it as well.

- A `;` on its own at file scope is no longer an error. The parser has always accepted it, with the pedantic warning gcc gives, and then the checker reported the declaration in front of it as having no type, which is a message about a declaration that was never written. Every project has one of these, usually left behind by a macro that expanded to nothing after a call site wrote a semicolon after it, and Apple's `<sys/socket.h>` has two. The rest of the family came with it, since the checker had one answer for all of them: `static;` and `const;` now say which specifier was the useless one and match gcc word for word, `inline;` and `_Noreturn;` are errors because neither has a meaning away from a function, `auto;` and `register;` are errors at file scope in C17 and `auto;` is one at any scope in C23 where the word means a deduced type instead, and `__auto_type;` is one everywhere.

- A failed `#include` with an empty search path said the name was an absolute path, which sent the reader after a path that was never there. Looking nowhere because there was nowhere to look and looking nowhere because the name was opened directly are two different things and now say so.

## 0.2.16

### Added

- The rest of the variable argument family, `__builtin_va_list`, `__builtin_va_start`, `__builtin_va_end` and `__builtin_va_copy`, which is what stood between the compiler and a variadic function that does anything with its arguments. `__builtin_va_list` is a keyword naming a type the target chooses rather than a name a header declares, and which type it is is the psABI's answer and now `rucc --print=config` says which: an array of one `struct __va_list_tag` on SysV, a structure of five fields on AAPCS, a `char *` on Windows and on Apple's arm64 where the variadic arguments are all on the stack, and a `void *` on RISC-V. It is built once per translation unit and kept, so that two mentions of it in one file are one type and not two structures that happen to match. The names inside it are interned before the first source character is read, which is what makes them the same symbols in every translation unit and keeps the type builder from having to intern a field name in the middle of building a type. What the other three operators are handed is the address of the list rather than the list, which is the one form that works everywhere: on SysV the address is what the array of one decays to and is what a `va_list` parameter already holds, and on the targets where the type is not an array the address is taken here, which is what makes a list passed to a callee the caller's own list rather than a copy of it. That is the same answer gcc reaches by declaring the three as functions taking a `__va_list_tag *` and having its header pass the array, without the special case. What is refused is refused in gcc's own words, measured rather than recalled: a first argument that is not a list, a `va_start` in a function with a fixed argument list, a `va_start` with one argument, and a `va_start` whose second argument is not the last named parameter, which is a warning because a program that names the wrong one reads its arguments from the wrong place and gets no other warning about it. The second argument is checked and then dropped, since the enclosing function's own type says where the named arguments stopped and nothing evaluates it, and a zero there is not a mistake because that is what C23's `va_start(ap, ...)` expands to. In the IR the three become `va_start`, `va_end` and `va_copy` over that address, and a list a function declares for itself gets a stack slot for the same reason an object whose address is taken does. `va_end` is nothing at all on every psABI here and is emitted anyway, since it is what says the list stops being read. What is still turned down is `va_arg` of a structure or a union.

- The IR as an input to the compiler, `rucc --emit=ir a.ir`, which is `.ir` in the table of extensions and `-x ir` for anything spelled another way. It is not a GCC input kind, because GCC has no textual IR, and it is here because the printer and the parser are a pair and a pair is only known to agree if something reads back what was written. `rucc --emit=ir a.c -o a.ir` and then `rucc --emit=ir a.ir -o b.ir` makes the round trip that document 08 asks for into two files a byte comparison has an opinion about, over whatever code is at hand rather than over the modules a test happens to build, which is what the M2 exit criterion needs to be checkable over SQLite rather than believable in principle. A module arriving this way is verified the same way one the walk built is, since a module a person edited has not been through the verifier, and what does not parse is reported with the line it stopped on. An input file whose output would have the name it has itself is now refused rather than read and written over, which is GCC's rule and which `rucc --emit=ir a.ir` would otherwise walk straight into.

- Inline assembly, from the parser's shape through checking and into the IR, which is the last of the constructs M2 named and the one the kernel cannot be built without. An `asm` statement keeps its operands in the order they were written, because the template counts them and renumbering them would make `%1` name something the program did not write, and what checking adds is the type of every operand, the labels resolved to the ones the function declares, and one answer the walk would otherwise have to work out for itself: whether an operand travels as a value or as the address of an object. That answer is recorded rather than recomputed because two passes need it and they have to agree, since the walk builds the address of a memory operand and the scan that runs before it decides which locals get a stack slot, and an operand the walk takes the address of has to be one the scan already knew about. A constraint that allows nothing but memory travels by address, and so does a structure or a union whatever else its constraint allows, since there is no register that holds one. Named operands are resolved to numbers here, so `%[out]` has become `%0` by the time anything downstream reads the template and nothing after this point has to know the names. In the IR the instruction is `inline_asm`, carrying the template, the clobbers and one comma separated constraint list in the numbering the template uses, the outputs first and then the inputs, which is what makes the instruction readable on its own: an output in a register is a result and is an argument as well when it is `+`, an output in memory is an argument that is an address, and an input is an argument that is a value unless its constraint names memory. An `asm` with no outputs is volatile whether or not it was written that way, since nothing reads a result and dropping it would drop the only thing it did. An `asm goto` is a terminator whose first successor is the block control falls through to and whose others are the labels in the order they were written, which is what makes an output of one well defined: the result is only in scope where the instruction dominates, so writing the outputs into their objects in the fall through block leaves the edges to the labels carrying the values those objects had before the assembly ran, which is what `spec/11-asm-objects-debug.md` asks for. Eight ways of writing one that no assembler could be handed are reported here in the words GCC uses for them, among them an output whose constraint lacks `=`, an output that is not an lvalue or is read-only, an input whose constraint claims to write, and a name in the template that no operand has. What is turned down is an `asm goto` in a function that has a variable length array in it, for the reason an ordinary `goto` there is, and an `asm` at file scope, which is a place in the module rather than an instruction in a function and which the IR has no slot for yet.

## 0.2.15

### Added

- GNU's computed goto, `&&label` and `goto *p`, in the walk to the IR and in the IR itself, which is what a threaded interpreter dispatches with and what SQLite's virtual machine wants from a compiler. Two instructions are new. `block_addr block3` is the address of a block, and it is the one instruction that names a block without arriving at it, so what a block whose address is taken owes its parameters to is the branches that go there and never the place the address was taken. `indirect_br %0, block1, block2` is a branch to an address, and it lists every block control can arrive at, which is what makes the edges of a computed goto ordinary edges: the values live across the jump are passed on each of them the way they are passed on a `jump`, the dominance the verifier checks is the dominance of that graph, and nothing downstream has to know that an address decided which target was taken.
